General provisions :-



***Article 12(Definition — General)***



Defines “the State” for Part III: Government and Parliament of India, State Governments and Legislatures, and local or other authorities within India or under the control of the Government of India. Simple: Fundamental Rights commonly protect people against unconstitutional action by government bodies and public authorities.



**Applies to / conditions / relevance :-**

Mainly identifies the bodies against which Part III rights can be enforced. Whether a particular body counts as an “other authority” can be fact-specific. Link to Articles 13, 32 and 226.

Article 13 — Laws inconsistent with or in derogation of Fundamental Rights — General

Accurate explanation: Laws made before the Constitution that conflict with Fundamental Rights are void to the extent of the conflict. The State cannot make laws that take away or reduce Fundamental Rights; such laws are void to that extent. “Law” includes rules, regulations, orders, bye-laws, notifications, customs and usages having force of law.



Simple: The government cannot use an ordinary law, rule, or notification to remove a Fundamental Right.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Relevant when a person challenges a law, rule, official order, policy, or notification that violates a Fundamental Right. Article 13(4) states that this Article does not apply to constitutional amendments made under Article 368. Link to Articles 12, 32, 226 and 368.

Right to Equality

Article 14 — Equality before law — Right to Equality

Accurate explanation: Guarantees equality before the law and equal protection of laws.



Simple: Government authorities must treat similarly placed people fairly and cannot act arbitrarily.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to every person, including non-citizens. It does not prevent every legal classification, but a classification must have a valid constitutional basis. Useful where government action is arbitrary, unequal, discriminatory, or irrational. Link to Articles 15, 16 and 21.

Article 15 — Prohibition of discrimination on grounds of religion, race, caste, sex or place of birth — Right to Equality

Accurate explanation: The State must not discriminate against a citizen only on the grounds of religion, race, caste, sex, place of birth, or any of them. Citizens cannot be denied access to public places such as shops, restaurants, hotels, roads, wells, tanks, bathing ghats, and public resorts only on these grounds.



Simple: You should not be excluded from public places or State benefits merely because of your religion, caste, sex, birthplace, or race.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to citizens. The Constitution permits special provisions for women and children; socially and educationally backward classes, Scheduled Castes, and Scheduled Tribes; and economically weaker sections.



Important exception: Article 15(6) permits special provisions, including reservation up to 10% in addition to existing reservation, for economically weaker sections in educational institutions. Minority educational institutions protected by Article 30(1) are excluded from the Article 15(5) admission-reservation provision.



Related provisions: Articles 14, 16, 17, 29(2) and 30.



Article 16 — Equality of opportunity in matters of public employment — Right to Equality

Accurate explanation: Provides equality of opportunity for citizens in matters of employment or appointment to offices under the State. The State cannot discriminate only on religion, race, caste, sex, descent, place of birth, residence, or any of them.



Simple: Government jobs must be offered fairly. A person cannot be rejected from a public job only because of the listed identity grounds.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to citizens and to employment or appointments under the State. It does not automatically regulate every private-sector job.



Important exceptions: Parliament may prescribe residence requirements for certain public jobs. The Constitution permits reservations for backward classes that are inadequately represented, SC/ST promotion reservation in specified circumstances, carry-forward reserved vacancies, and EWS reservation up to 10% in addition to existing reservation. Religious institutions may require religious office-holders to belong to that religion or denomination.



Related provisions: Articles 14, 15, 16(4), 16(4A), 16(4B), 16(5), 16(6) and 19(1)(g).



Article 17 — Abolition of Untouchability — Right to Equality

Accurate explanation: “Untouchability” is abolished, and its practice in any form is forbidden. Enforcing a disability arising from untouchability is an offence punishable by law.



Simple: No one can lawfully impose caste-based exclusion, humiliation, restrictions, or disabilities rooted in untouchability.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Protects all persons affected by the prohibited practice. It applies to conduct by private persons as well as public bodies because enforcement is supported by criminal law.



Important related laws: Protection of Civil Rights Act, 1955; Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989.



Related provisions: Articles 14, 15, 21 and 23.

Article 18 — Abolition of titles — Right to Equality

Accurate explanation: The State cannot confer titles except military or academic distinctions. Indian citizens cannot accept titles from foreign States. Persons holding offices of profit or trust under the State need Presidential consent before accepting foreign titles, presents, emoluments, or offices in the situations specified by the Article.



Simple: The Constitution does not allow titles that create hereditary or social rank, but academic degrees and military distinctions are allowed.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies differently to the State, citizens, non-citizens holding State offices, and persons holding offices of profit or trust under the State.



Common misunderstanding: An academic degree, job designation, military distinction, or civilian award is not automatically a prohibited constitutional “title.”



Related provisions: Articles 14–17.



Right to Freedom

Article 19 — Protection of certain rights regarding freedom of speech, etc. — Right to Freedom

Accurate explanation: Gives citizens the freedom of speech and expression; peaceful and unarmed assembly; forming associations or unions; moving freely throughout India; residing and settling anywhere in India; and practising any profession or carrying on any occupation, trade, or business.



Simple: Citizens can speak, peacefully protest, form groups, travel, live across India, and pursue lawful work or business.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to citizens only.



Important restrictions: These freedoms are not absolute. The State may impose reasonable restrictions by law on specific constitutional grounds such as sovereignty and integrity of India, security of the State, public order, decency, morality, defamation, contempt of court, and incitement to an offence.



Common misunderstanding: Article 19 does not give a right to violence, hate-based conduct, defamation, contempt of court, unlawful assembly, or unregulated activity that valid law restricts.



Related provisions: Articles 14, 19(2)–19(6), 21, 25 and 32.

Article 20 — Protection in respect of conviction for offences — Right to Freedom

Accurate explanation: Protects against retrospective criminal punishment, double jeopardy, and compelled self-incrimination.



Simple: You cannot be punished under a criminal law made after you acted, punished twice for the same offence, or forced to testify against yourself when accused of an offence.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to every person. The protection against self-incrimination applies to a person accused of an offence.



Important conditions: This does not prevent lawful police investigation or the lawful collection of evidence. Whether two proceedings involve the “same offence” can require legal analysis.



Related provisions: Articles 21 and 22; Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023; Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam, 2023.



Article 21 — Protection of life and personal liberty — Right to Freedom

Accurate explanation: No person may be deprived of life or personal liberty except according to procedure established by law.



Simple: The State cannot take away your liberty or life except through a lawful procedure.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to every person, including non-citizens.



Practical relevance: This Article is central where there are issues involving unlawful detention, custody, bodily integrity, dignity, privacy, fair procedure, and other serious liberty concerns. The exact protection depends on facts and court interpretation.



Important limitation: Article 21 is not a replacement for every personal grievance. Urgent detention, police, medical, or safety matters require prompt legal assistance.



Related provisions: Articles 14, 20, 21A, 22, 32 and 226.

Article 21A — Right to education — Right to Freedom

Accurate explanation: The State must provide free and compulsory education to all children from 6 to 14 years of age, in the manner determined by law.



Simple: Every child aged 6–14 has a constitutional right to free and compulsory elementary education.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to children aged 6 to 14 years.



Important related law: Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act, 2009. Its section 3 provides free and compulsory education in a neighbourhood school until completion of elementary education.



Related provisions: Articles 15(3), 24, 29, 30, 39(e), 39(f) and 45.

Article 22 — Protection against arrest and detention in certain cases — Right to Freedom

Accurate explanation: A person arrested must be informed, as soon as possible, of the grounds for arrest. They cannot be denied the right to consult and be defended by a legal practitioner of their choice. They must be produced before the nearest magistrate within 24 hours of arrest, excluding necessary travel time, and cannot be held beyond that period without magistrate authority.



Simple: If arrested, ask why you are being arrested, ask to speak with a lawyer, and ensure you are produced before a magistrate within 24 hours.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to persons arrested or detained, subject to the exceptions in Article 22(3).



Important exceptions: The ordinary protections in Article 22(1) and 22(2) do not apply to an enemy alien or a person detained under a preventive-detention law. Preventive detention has separate constitutional safeguards, including Advisory Board review, communication of grounds, and an opportunity to make a representation.



Important warning: Arrest, remand, detention, and preventive detention are urgent, fact-specific matters. Contact a lawyer, legal-aid authority, or trusted person immediately.



Related provisions: Articles 20, 21, 32 and 226; Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023.



Right against Exploitation

Article 23 — Prohibition of traffic in human beings and forced labour — Right against Exploitation

Accurate explanation: Prohibits trafficking in human beings, begar, and other similar forms of forced labour. A violation is an offence punishable by law.



Simple: Nobody can lawfully traffic you or force you to work without proper freedom and payment. “Begar” means forced work without payment.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Protects every person. It can apply to private conduct as well as government conduct.



Important exception: The State may impose compulsory service for public purposes, but it cannot discriminate only on religion, race, caste, or class when imposing such service.



Related provisions: Articles 21 and 24; Bonded Labour System (Abolition) Act, 1976.

Article 24 — Prohibition of employment of children in factories, etc. — Right against Exploitation

Accurate explanation: No child below 14 years can be employed in a factory, mine, or other hazardous employment.



Simple: A child under 14 cannot lawfully be made to work in hazardous places such as factories or mines.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to children below 14 years.



Important conditions: Article 24 is the constitutional baseline. Detailed restrictions, exceptions, enforcement measures, and penalties are provided by child-labour legislation.



Related provisions: Articles 21A, 23, 39(e), 39(f) and 45; Child and Adolescent Labour (Prohibition and Regulation) Act, 1986, as amended.

Religious, cultural and educational rights

Article 25 — Freedom of conscience and free profession, practice and propagation of religion — Freedom of Religion

Accurate explanation: All persons are equally entitled to freedom of conscience and the right freely to profess, practise, and propagate religion.



Simple: You may hold religious beliefs and practise or share your religion, subject to constitutional limits.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to all persons.



Important restrictions: Subject to public order, morality, health, and other Fundamental Rights. The State may regulate secular activities connected with religion and make social-welfare or reform laws.



Special provision: Wearing and carrying a kirpan is included in the profession of the Sikh religion. For the Hindu-institution-opening provision, “Hindus” includes Sikh, Jain, and Buddhist persons.



Related provisions: Articles 14, 15, 19, 25–28 and 30.

Article 26 — Freedom to manage religious affairs — Freedom of Religion

Accurate explanation: Every religious denomination or section has the right to establish and maintain institutions for religious and charitable purposes, manage religious affairs, own and acquire property, and administer property according to law.



Simple: A religious denomination may manage its religious affairs and institutions, but the administration of its property can be regulated by law.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to religious denominations or sections of them.



Important restrictions: Subject to public order, morality, and health. Religious matters have constitutional protection, but secular property management may be regulated through valid law.



Related provisions: Articles 25, 27, 28 and 30.

Article 27 — Freedom as to payment of taxes for promotion of any particular religion — Freedom of Religion

Accurate explanation: No person can be compelled to pay taxes if the proceeds are specifically appropriated to promote or maintain a particular religion or religious denomination.



Simple: The State cannot force taxpayers to fund a particular religion as such.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to every person.



Important limitation: This does not automatically prohibit every public expenditure that indirectly benefits a religious institution or community. The purpose and legal structure of the expenditure matter.



Related provisions: Articles 25, 26 and 28.

Article 28 — Freedom as to attendance at religious instruction or religious worship in certain educational institutions — Freedom of Religion

Accurate explanation: Religious instruction cannot be provided in an educational institution wholly maintained by State funds. A State-administered institution established under an endowment or trust requiring religious instruction is excepted. Students in State-recognised or State-aided institutions cannot be required to attend religious instruction or worship without consent.



Simple: In covered schools and colleges, students cannot be forced into religious lessons or worship without valid consent.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to students. If the student is a minor, the guardian must give consent.



Important conditions: The rule differs depending on whether the institution is wholly State-funded, State-administered under a trust/endowment, State-recognised, or State-aided.



Related provisions: Articles 25–27, 29 and 30.

Article 29 — Protection of interests of minorities — Cultural and Educational Rights

Accurate explanation: Any section of citizens with a distinct language, script, or culture has the right to conserve it. No citizen may be denied admission to a State-maintained or State-aided educational institution only on grounds of religion, race, caste, language, or any of them.



Simple: Citizen groups can protect their language, script, and culture. State-funded or State-aided educational institutions cannot reject a citizen only because of the listed identity grounds.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Article 29(1) applies to any section of citizens with a distinct language, script, or culture. Article 29(2) applies to citizens seeking admission.



Common misunderstanding: Article 29(1) is not limited only to communities formally described as “minorities.”



Related provisions: Articles 15, 21A, 28 and 30.

Article 30 — Right of minorities to establish and administer educational institutions — Cultural and Educational Rights

Accurate explanation: Religious and linguistic minorities have the right to establish and administer educational institutions of their choice. The State cannot discriminate when granting aid merely because an institution is managed by a religious or linguistic minority.



Simple: Religious and linguistic minorities can establish and administer educational institutions. They cannot be refused government aid just because they are minority-managed.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies to religious and linguistic minorities.



Important conditions: Article 30(1A) protects minority institutions when property is acquired by the State. This right does not make an institution exempt from every valid educational, safety, employment, or regulatory law.



Related provisions: Articles 15(5), 21A and 29.

Property, remedies and saving provisions

Article 31 — Compulsory acquisition of property — Omitted

Accurate explanation: Article 31 was omitted by the Constitution (Forty-fourth Amendment) Act, 1978.



Simple: The Right to Property is no longer a Fundamental Right.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Property protection now mainly comes from Article 300A, which is outside Part III.



Common misunderstanding: Article 300A is a constitutional legal right, but it is not a Fundamental Right.



Related provisions: Article 300A; Articles 31A, 31B and 31C.

Article 31A — Saving of laws providing for acquisition of estates, etc. — Saving provision

Accurate explanation: Protects specified categories of laws, especially certain land-reform and estate-related laws, from invalidity merely for inconsistency with Articles 14 and 19, subject to constitutional conditions.



Simple: Certain land-reform and similar laws receive special constitutional protection from challenge under Articles 14 and 19.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Not an individual Fundamental Right. It is a constitutional saving provision affecting challenges to particular laws.



Important conditions: State laws relying on Article 31A may require Presidential assent in the circumstances specified by the Article.



Related provisions: Articles 14, 19, 31B, 31C and 300A.



Article 31B — Validation of certain Acts and Regulations — Saving provision

Accurate explanation: Protects Acts and Regulations placed in the Ninth Schedule from invalidity merely for inconsistency with Part III, subject to constitutional limits and legislative repeal or amendment.



Simple: Some laws in the Ninth Schedule have special constitutional protection.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Not an individual right.



Important warning: Do not assume that every Ninth Schedule law is automatically immune from every constitutional challenge. The legal position can depend on the particular law and constitutional judicial review principles.



Related provisions: Articles 13, 14, 19, 31A and 31C.



Article 31C — Saving of laws giving effect to certain Directive Principles — Saving provision

Accurate explanation: Protects laws giving effect to the policy of Article 39(b) and Article 39(c) from invalidity under Articles 14 and 19, to the extent provided by the Article.



Simple: A law genuinely made to distribute important material resources for the common good or prevent harmful concentration of wealth may receive special protection from challenges under Articles 14 and 19.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Not an individual Fundamental Right. It affects constitutional challenges to qualifying laws.



Important limitation: The attempted expansion of Article 31C by the 42nd Amendment to cover all Directive Principles was declared invalid by the Supreme Court in Minerva Mills. Do not state that every law implementing any Directive Principle overrides Articles 14 and 19.



Related provisions: Articles 14, 19, 31A, 39(b) and 39(c).

Article 31D — Saving of laws in respect of anti-national activities — Omitted

Accurate explanation: Article 31D was repealed by the Constitution (Forty-third Amendment) Act, 1977.



Simple: Article 31D is not current law.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Do not use it in LAWOUD as an active constitutional provision.



Related provisions: Articles 19, 21 and 22.

Article 32 — Remedies for enforcement of rights conferred by this Part — Constitutional Remedies

Accurate explanation: Guarantees the right to move the Supreme Court through appropriate proceedings to enforce Fundamental Rights. The Supreme Court may issue directions, orders, or writs, including habeas corpus, mandamus, prohibition, quo warranto, and certiorari.



Simple: If your Fundamental Right is violated, you can approach the Supreme Court for protection.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Relevant to persons alleging a violation of a Part III right. Court procedure, evidence, urgency, and the nature of the violation affect whether the petition is maintainable.



Practical relevance: Article 32 is especially important for serious Fundamental Rights violations, such as unlawful detention or serious unconstitutional State action. The Supreme Court has described it as an important safeguard for enforcing Fundamental Rights.



Related provisions: Articles 13, 21, 22, 32(3), 226 and 359.



Article 32A — Constitutional validity of State laws not to be considered in proceedings under Article 32 — Omitted

Accurate explanation: Article 32A was repealed by the Constitution (Forty-third Amendment) Act, 1977.



Simple: Article 32A is not current law.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Do not use it as an active restriction on Article 32 remedies.



Related provisions: Articles 13, 32 and 226.

Article 33 — Power of Parliament to modify the rights conferred by this Part in their application to Forces, etc. — Restriction provision

Accurate explanation: Parliament may make laws determining how far Fundamental Rights can be restricted or removed for armed forces, forces responsible for public order, intelligence or counter-intelligence organisations, and associated telecommunication personnel, to ensure proper discharge of duties and discipline.



Simple: Parliament can lawfully limit some Fundamental Rights for military, policing, intelligence, and related personnel when needed for discipline and duty.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Applies only where Parliament has made a law under Article 33. It is not a general power for any authority to ignore Fundamental Rights.



Related provisions: Articles 14–32 and 35.



Article 34 — Restriction on rights conferred by this Part while martial law is in force in any area — Restriction provision

Accurate explanation: Parliament may indemnify persons for acts done in connection with maintaining or restoring order in an area where martial law was in force, and may validate certain acts done under martial law.



Simple: During actual martial law in an area, Parliament may provide legal protection for specified actions taken to restore order.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: This is not an ordinary policing provision.



Common misunderstanding: Martial law is not the same as a constitutional emergency under Article 352.



Related provisions: Articles 32, 33, 35 and 352.

Article 35 — Legislation to give effect to the provisions of this Part — Legislative power provision

Accurate explanation: Gives Parliament exclusive power to make laws on matters listed in Article 16(3), Article 32(3), Article 33, and Article 34, and to prescribe punishment for acts declared offences under Part III.



Simple: For certain Fundamental Rights matters, only Parliament—not State Legislatures—can make the required law.



Applies to / conditions / relevance: Explains why some rights are enforced through central legislation.



Practical relevance: Important for understanding the legal basis of laws enforcing Articles 16, 17, 23, 24, 32, 33, and 34.



Related provisions: Articles 16(3), 17, 23, 24, 32(3), 33 and 34.

Part IV — Directive Principles

Part: Part IV — Directive Principles of State Policy

Nature: These are constitutional principles intended to guide the State in making laws, policies, and governance decisions. They are generally not directly enforceable by courts, as Article 37 expressly states. They are not individual Fundamental Rights.



Article 36 — Definition

Provision Type: Directive Principle — interpretation provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles



Accurate Explanation: For Part IV, “the State” has the same meaning as it has in Part III of the Constitution.



Simple Explanation: The government bodies covered by Fundamental Rights rules are generally also the bodies expected to follow the Directive Principles.



Applies To: The Union Government, Parliament, State Governments, State Legislatures, local authorities, and qualifying public authorities, as understood under Article 12.



Conditions: The Article incorporates the Part III definition; it does not create a separate definition.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 36 does not itself create an enforceable individual right.



Practical Relevance: Helps identify which public bodies are constitutionally expected to work toward social, economic, and welfare objectives in Part IV.



Related Articles: Articles 12, 37, 38–51.



Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified by the Constitution.

Article 37 — Application of the principles contained in this Part

Provision Type: Directive Principle — enforceability provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles



Accurate Explanation: The provisions in Part IV are not enforceable by any court. However, the principles are fundamental in the governance of the country, and the State has a duty to apply them when making laws.



Simple Explanation: A person usually cannot demand a Directive Principle directly from a court in the same way as a Fundamental Right. But governments are constitutionally expected to use these principles when creating laws and policies.



Applies To: The State, especially legislatures and governments making law and public policy.



Conditions: Applies to all Directive Principles in Articles 38–51.



Exceptions / Limitations: It does not convert Part IV principles into directly enforceable Fundamental Rights.



Practical Relevance: A citizen may use Directive Principles to understand the constitutional purpose behind welfare laws, labour protections, legal aid, environmental measures, education policies, and local-government reforms.



Related Articles: Articles 36, 38–51; Article 32; Article 226.



Related Acts / Procedures: Depends on the particular Directive Principle and implementing law.

Article 38 — State to secure a social order for the promotion of welfare of the people

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Equality; Social and Economic Policy



Accurate Explanation: The State must strive to promote the welfare of people by securing and protecting, as effectively as possible, a social order in which justice—social, economic, and political—shall inform all institutions of national life. The State must also strive to minimise inequalities in income, status, facilities, and opportunities, not only among individuals but also among groups in different areas or occupations.



Simple Explanation: The government should work toward a fair society where people have better access to justice, opportunities, facilities, and economic security, and where large inequalities are reduced.



Applies To: The State.



Conditions: Article 38(2) directs the State to reduce inequalities between people and groups, including groups living in different places or working in different occupations.



Exceptions / Limitations: This is not directly enforceable in court under Article 37.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to social-welfare policy, poverty reduction, regional development, equal opportunity, public services, and measures designed to reduce major economic or social inequalities.



Related Articles: Preamble; Articles 14, 15, 16, 21, 39, 39A, 41, 46.



Related Acts / Procedures: Depends on the welfare or equality scheme concerned.

Article 39 — Certain principles of policy to be followed by the State

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Equality; Employment; Children; Finance



Accurate Explanation: The State must, in particular, direct its policy toward securing:



Article 39(a): Adequate means of livelihood for citizens, men and women equally.



Article 39(b): Ownership and control of material resources of the community distributed to best serve the common good.



Article 39(c): The economic system should not result in concentration of wealth and means of production that harms the common good.



Article 39(d): Equal pay for equal work for men and women.



Article 39(e): The health and strength of workers, men and women, and the tender age of children must not be abused; citizens must not be forced by economic necessity to enter unsuitable work.



Article 39(f): Children should develop in a healthy manner and in conditions of freedom and dignity; childhood and youth must be protected against exploitation and moral and material abandonment.



Simple Explanation: The government should work toward fair livelihoods, fair pay, protection of workers and children, less harmful concentration of wealth, and fair use of public resources.



Applies To: The State.



Conditions: The Article guides policy. It does not itself prescribe a single scheme, wage amount, reservation rule, or welfare benefit.



Exceptions / Limitations: Not directly enforceable by a court under Article 37. Actual benefits, eligibility, duties, and enforcement come from specific laws and schemes.



Practical Relevance: Important constitutional background for labour policy, equal-pay measures, child protection, welfare programmes, land/resource regulation, and economic justice legislation.



Related Articles: Articles 14, 15, 16, 21, 21A, 23, 24, 31C, 38, 39A, 41–43, 45, 46.



Related Acts / Procedures: Labour laws, child-protection laws, welfare laws, and resource-related laws may be connected depending on the issue.

Article 39A — Equal justice and free legal aid

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active; inserted by the Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Legal Aid; Constitutional Remedies



Accurate Explanation: The State must secure that the operation of the legal system promotes justice on a basis of equal opportunity. It must provide free legal aid, by suitable legislation, schemes, or otherwise, to ensure that opportunities for securing justice are not denied because of economic or other disabilities.



Simple Explanation: The government should help people who cannot afford legal help so that lack of money or disadvantage does not stop them from seeking justice.



Applies To: The State; practically relevant to persons facing economic or other barriers to legal representation.



Conditions: Eligibility for a specific legal-aid service is determined by the applicable legal-services law, regulations, and authority—not by Article 39A alone.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 39A is not itself a direct right to a lawyer in every type of dispute. It is a Directive Principle.



Practical Relevance: Supports access to free legal aid through Legal Services Authorities, including District Legal Services Authorities and State Legal Services Authorities.



Related Articles: Articles 14, 21, 22(1), 32, 37 and 226.



Related Acts / Procedures: Legal Services Authorities Act, 1987; approach the relevant District Legal Services Authority, State Legal Services Authority, or NALSA-linked service for legal-aid information.

Article 40 — Organisation of village panchayats

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Local Government; Panchayats



Accurate Explanation: The State must take steps to organise village panchayats and endow them with powers and authority necessary to enable them to function as units of self-government.



Simple Explanation: The government should create and empower village-level local governments so people can participate in governing local matters.



Applies To: The State, particularly State Governments and State Legislatures.



Conditions: The detailed constitutional framework for Panchayats is now in Part IX, especially Articles 243–243O.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 40 itself does not specify the composition, reservation, duration, election procedure, powers, or finances of a Panchayat.



Practical Relevance: Constitutional foundation for Panchayati Raj and local participation in village development, local infrastructure, welfare delivery, and community decision-making.



Related Articles: Articles 243–243O; Eleventh Schedule.



Related Acts / Procedures: Relevant State Panchayat legislation and local election procedures.

Article 41 — Right to work, to education and to public assistance in certain cases

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Employment; Education; Social Welfare



Accurate Explanation: Within the limits of its economic capacity and development, the State must make effective provision for securing the right to work, to education, and to public assistance in cases of unemployment, old age, sickness, disablement, and other cases of undeserved want.



Simple Explanation: As far as government resources allow, the State should support work opportunities, education, and assistance for people facing unemployment, old age, illness, disability, or serious need.



Applies To: The State and persons covered by welfare measures created under law or government schemes.



Conditions: The express constitutional condition is the State’s economic capacity and level of development.



Exceptions / Limitations: This Article does not automatically create an individual entitlement to a job, pension, benefit, or monetary assistance. Eligibility and benefits depend on applicable laws and schemes.



Practical Relevance: Constitutional basis for employment-support, social-security, disability, old-age, sickness, and welfare measures.



Related Articles: Articles 21A, 38, 39, 41–43, 45, 46.



Related Acts / Procedures: Relevant social-security, disability, education, employment, and welfare legislation or scheme rules.

Article 42 — Provision for just and humane conditions of work and maternity relief

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Employment; Women \& Children



Accurate Explanation: The State must make provision for securing just and humane conditions of work and maternity relief.



Simple Explanation: The government should ensure safe, fair, and humane workplaces and support maternity relief.



Applies To: The State; workers and pregnant women receive actual protections through implementing legislation.



Conditions: Specific maternity benefits, leave periods, workplace standards, and eligibility are created by ordinary legislation and rules.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 42 alone does not specify a maternity-leave duration, payment amount, employer category, or workplace obligation.



Practical Relevance: Constitutional basis for labour protections, workplace safety, maternity benefits, and welfare measures for working women.



Related Articles: Articles 15(3), 21, 39(d), 39(e), 43 and 43A.



Related Acts / Procedures: Maternity Benefit Act, 1961; labour and workplace-safety legislation, where applicable.

Article 43 — Living wage, etc., for workers

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Employment; Rural Development



Accurate Explanation: The State must endeavour, by suitable legislation, economic organisation, or other means, to secure for all workers—agricultural, industrial, or otherwise—a living wage, conditions of work ensuring a decent standard of life and full enjoyment of leisure and social and cultural opportunities. It must also promote cottage industries on an individual or co-operative basis in rural areas.



Simple Explanation: The government should work toward wages that support a decent life, humane work conditions, and rural small industries.



Applies To: The State; relevant to agricultural, industrial, and other workers, as well as rural cottage industries.



Conditions: The Article directs the State to act through legislation, economic organisation, or other means.



Exceptions / Limitations: It does not itself set a wage amount or make every worker automatically entitled to a particular wage. Specific entitlements arise from labour laws, contracts, wage notifications, and other applicable rules.



Practical Relevance: Constitutional basis for minimum-wage policy, worker welfare, decent work conditions, rural employment, and promotion of cottage industries.



Related Articles: Articles 38, 39(a), 39(d), 39(e), 41, 42, 43A and 43B.



Related Acts / Procedures: Labour and wage legislation; relevant government notifications and employment procedures.

Article 43A — Participation of workers in management of industries

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active; inserted by the Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Employment



Accurate Explanation: The State must take steps, by suitable legislation or in any other way, to secure the participation of workers in the management of undertakings, establishments, or other organisations engaged in an industry.



Simple Explanation: The government should encourage workers to have a role in how industrial workplaces are managed.



Applies To: The State and industrial undertakings, establishments, or organisations to the extent provided by law or policy.



Conditions: Implementation depends on suitable legislation, industrial rules, workplace arrangements, or policy.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 43A does not itself create a universal individual right for every worker to sit on management boards or make management decisions.



Practical Relevance: Supports worker participation, labour-management consultation, and industrial democracy measures.



Related Articles: Articles 39, 41, 42 and 43.



Related Acts / Procedures: Depends on current labour and industrial-relations law.

Article 43B — Promotion of co-operative societies

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active; inserted by the Constitution (Ninety-seventh Amendment) Act, 2011



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Co-operative Societies; Local Government



Accurate Explanation: The State must endeavour to promote the voluntary formation, autonomous functioning, democratic control, and professional management of co-operative societies.



Simple Explanation: The government should encourage co-operative societies that are formed voluntarily, work independently, are democratically controlled, and professionally managed.



Applies To: The State and co-operative-society policy.



Conditions: Detailed regulation and registration of co-operative societies come from applicable legislation.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 43B is a Directive Principle, not an individual enforceable right. The Supreme Court held that Part IXB is operative for multi-State co-operative societies, while its application to State co-operative societies was invalidated for want of State ratification. That ruling does not remove Article 43B from the Constitution.



Practical Relevance: Helps explain the constitutional policy supporting democratic, autonomous, and professionally managed co-operatives.



Related Articles: Article 19(1)(c); Articles 243ZH–243ZT; Part IXB.



Related Acts / Procedures: Multi-State Co-operative Societies Act, 2002; applicable State co-operative-society law.

Article 44 — Uniform civil code for the citizens

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Family \& Marriage



Accurate Explanation: The State must endeavour to secure for citizens a uniform civil code throughout the territory of India.



Simple Explanation: The Constitution says the State should work toward common civil-law rules for citizens, especially in areas such as family and personal-law matters.



Applies To: The State; the objective concerns citizens.



Conditions: The Article does not itself enact a uniform civil code or replace any personal law.



Exceptions / Limitations: It is not directly enforceable under Article 37. Whether, when, and how any legislation is enacted depends on Parliament or the relevant legislature, subject to constitutional limits.



Practical Relevance: Relevant when discussing constitutional policy debates concerning marriage, divorce, maintenance, adoption, succession, and personal laws. It does not itself change a person’s current family-law rights or obligations.



Related Articles: Articles 14, 15, 21, 25–28 and 37.



Related Acts / Procedures: Applicable personal laws and family-law statutes remain relevant unless lawfully changed.

Article 45 — Provision for early childhood care and education to children below the age of six years

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active; substituted by the Constitution (Eighty-sixth Amendment) Act, 2002



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Education; Women \& Children



Accurate Explanation: The State must endeavour to provide early childhood care and education for all children until they complete six years of age.



Simple Explanation: The government should work to provide care and early education for children below six years old.



Applies To: The State and children below six years of age.



Conditions: The Article uses “endeavour,” and actual services depend on schemes, laws, budgets, and implementing authorities.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 45 is not directly enforceable. It is distinct from Article 21A, which concerns free and compulsory education for children aged 6–14.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to anganwadi services, early childhood care, pre-school support, nutrition-linked child-development services, and policy for children below six.



Related Articles: Articles 21A, 39(e), 39(f), 41 and 47.



Related Acts / Procedures: Relevant early-childhood and child-development schemes or laws, where applicable.

Article 46 — Promotion of educational and economic interests of Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes and other weaker sections

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Equality; Education; Scheduled Areas; Tribal Areas



Accurate Explanation: The State must promote, with special care, the educational and economic interests of weaker sections of the people, particularly Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes, and protect them from social injustice and all forms of exploitation.



Simple Explanation: The government should give special attention to the education, economic advancement, and protection of SCs, STs, and other weaker sections from exploitation and injustice.



Applies To: The State; particularly relevant to SCs, STs, and other weaker sections.



Conditions: Specific protections, reservations, scholarships, welfare benefits, and procedures arise from applicable constitutional provisions, laws, rules, and schemes.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 46 does not itself define every “weaker section” or automatically grant every benefit. Eligibility depends on the relevant programme or statute.



Practical Relevance: Constitutional basis for protective, educational, welfare, anti-exploitation, and economic-development measures for SCs, STs, and weaker sections.



Related Articles: Articles 15(4), 15(5), 15(6), 16(4), 16(4A), 17, 38, 39, 244, 330, 332, 338, 338A, 342 and 342A.



Related Acts / Procedures: Protection of Civil Rights Act, 1955; Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989; related welfare and educational schemes.

Article 47 — Duty of the State to raise the level of nutrition and the standard of living and to improve public health

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Health; Social Welfare



Accurate Explanation: The State must regard raising the level of nutrition and the standard of living of its people and improving public health as among its primary duties. It must endeavour to bring about prohibition of consumption, except for medicinal purposes, of intoxicating drinks and drugs injurious to health.



Simple Explanation: The government should improve public health, nutrition, and living standards, and should work to reduce harmful alcohol and drug consumption except where medicinal use is involved.



Applies To: The State.



Conditions: The prohibition-related objective is limited by the Article’s exception for medicinal purposes.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 47 does not itself create a directly enforceable individual right to a particular health service, nutrition benefit, or prohibition policy. Detailed regulation comes from legislation and State policy.



Practical Relevance: Constitutional foundation for public-health programmes, nutrition initiatives, food-security measures, and policies regulating harmful intoxicants and drugs.



Related Articles: Articles 21, 39(e), 41, 45 and 48A.



Related Acts / Procedures: Health, food-safety, nutrition, alcohol-control, and narcotic-drug laws may be relevant depending on the issue.

Article 48 — Organisation of agriculture and animal husbandry

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Agriculture; Rural Development



Accurate Explanation: The State must endeavour to organise agriculture and animal husbandry on modern and scientific lines. It must take steps for preserving and improving breeds and prohibiting the slaughter of cows, calves, and other milch and draught cattle.



Simple Explanation: The government should modernise farming and animal husbandry, improve animal breeds, and protect cows, calves, and other milk-producing or working cattle as stated in the Constitution.



Applies To: The State.



Conditions: The Article gives a Directive Principle; detailed rules about agriculture, cattle protection, animal welfare, transport, slaughter, and trade are governed by applicable laws.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 48 does not itself set out a complete criminal offence, penalty, permit system, or uniform rule for all States.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to State policies and legislation on agriculture, animal husbandry, cattle preservation, and slaughter regulation.



Related Articles: Articles 39(b), 43, 48A and 246; Seventh Schedule entries concerning agriculture and animal husbandry.



Related Acts / Procedures: Applicable Central and State agriculture, animal-husbandry, animal-welfare, and slaughter-regulation laws.

Article 48A — Protection and improvement of environment and safeguarding of forests and wildlife

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active; inserted by the Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Environment; Forests; Wildlife



Accurate Explanation: The State must endeavour to protect and improve the environment and safeguard the forests and wildlife of the country.



Simple Explanation: The government should protect the environment, forests, and wildlife.



Applies To: The State.



Conditions: Actual environmental duties, permissions, standards, enforcement mechanisms, and remedies arise from environmental laws and regulations.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 48A is a Directive Principle and is not directly enforceable under Article 37.



Practical Relevance: Important constitutional policy basis for environmental protection, pollution control, forest conservation, wildlife protection, and sustainable-development measures.



Related Articles: Articles 21, 47, 48A, 51A(g), 246 and the Seventh Schedule.



Related Acts / Procedures: Environment (Protection) Act, 1986; Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980; Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972; relevant environmental-clearance and pollution-control processes.

Article 49 — Protection of monuments and places and objects of national importance

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Heritage; Culture



Accurate Explanation: The State must protect every monument, place, or object of artistic or historic interest that Parliament has declared by law to be of national importance, from spoliation, disfigurement, destruction, removal, disposal, or export, as the case may be.



Simple Explanation: The government should protect nationally important historical and artistic places and objects from damage, destruction, illegal removal, sale, or export.



Applies To: The State; relevant to monuments, places, and objects declared by Parliament to be of national importance.



Conditions: The constitutional duty applies where Parliament has declared the monument, place, or object to be of national importance by law.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 49 does not by itself declare every old building or object a protected national monument.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to protection of nationally important heritage sites, monuments, archaeological objects, and historically significant property.



Related Articles: Articles 29, 49, 51A(f), 246 and Seventh Schedule entries.



Related Acts / Procedures: Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958; related heritage-protection law.

Article 50 — Separation of judiciary from executive

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; Judiciary; State Government



Accurate Explanation: The State must take steps to separate the judiciary from the executive in the public services of the State.



Simple Explanation: The government should keep judges and courts independent from executive government control, especially in State public services.



Applies To: The State, especially State-level governance and judicial administration.



Conditions: Implementation is shaped by constitutional provisions, court structures, service rules, and applicable laws.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 50 does not itself establish every court or set out all rules for judicial appointments and control.



Practical Relevance: Supports the principle that judicial decision-making should be independent from executive administration.



Related Articles: Articles 32, 50, 124–147, 214–237 and 233–237.



Related Acts / Procedures: Court-administration and judicial-service rules, where applicable.

Article 51 — Promotion of international peace and security

Provision Type: Directive Principle



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Directive Principles; International Relations



Accurate Explanation: The State must endeavour to:



promote international peace and security;



maintain just and honourable relations between nations;



foster respect for international law and treaty obligations in dealings of organised peoples with one another; and



encourage settlement of international disputes by arbitration.



Simple Explanation: India should work for peace between countries, respect international commitments, and encourage peaceful ways to resolve international disputes.



Applies To: The State, especially the Union in external affairs.



Conditions: Foreign affairs, treaty implementation, and international obligations are governed by the Constitution, parliamentary law, executive action, and applicable international commitments.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 51 is a Directive Principle, not a directly enforceable individual right. It does not automatically make every international treaty enforceable in Indian courts.



Practical Relevance: Provides constitutional guidance for India’s international relations, treaty policy, peaceful dispute resolution, and respect for international law.



Related Articles: Articles 37, 51, 73, 246, 253 and 253-related treaty implementation.



Related Acts / Procedures: Treaty implementation may require parliamentary legislation under Article 253 where needed.

Part IVA — Fundamental Duties

Part: Part IVA — Fundamental Duties

Article: 51A

Official Heading: Fundamental duties

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active. Clauses (a)–(j) were inserted by the Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976. Clause (k) was inserted by the Constitution (Eighty-sixth Amendment) Act, 2002.



Category: Constitution; Fundamental Duties; Citizenship; Environment; Education



Article 51A — Fundamental duties

Accurate Explanation: Article 51A states that it is the duty of every citizen of India to perform the duties listed in clauses (a) to (k).



Simple Explanation: Fundamental Duties describe civic responsibilities expected from Indian citizens—such as respecting the Constitution, protecting the environment, promoting harmony, avoiding violence, and supporting children’s education.

Article 51A — Fundamental duties

Accurate Explanation: Article 51A states that it is the duty of every citizen of India to perform the duties listed in clauses (a) to (k).



Simple Explanation: Fundamental Duties describe civic responsibilities expected from Indian citizens—such as respecting the Constitution, protecting the environment, promoting harmony, avoiding violence, and supporting children’s education.



Applies To: Every citizen of India.



Conditions: These are constitutional duties of citizens. Different duties apply in different real-life situations; for example, clause (k) applies specifically to a parent or guardian of a child or ward aged 6–14 years.



Exceptions / Limitations:



Article 51A does not itself specify a separate penalty, complaint process, or automatic punishment for breach of each duty.



Fundamental Duties are not the same as Fundamental Rights.



A citizen cannot ordinarily file a writ petition solely to force another private citizen to perform a Fundamental Duty.



Parliament and State Legislatures may create valid laws connected with the subject matter of a duty—for example, environmental, public-property, education, or public-order laws—and those laws may contain their own procedures and penalties.



Duties must be understood consistently with Fundamental Rights and other constitutional provisions.



Practical Relevance: Article 51A helps citizens understand constitutional civic responsibility. It is relevant to environmental protection, national symbols, social harmony, public property, non-violence, scientific thinking, education of children, and responsible public conduct.



Related Articles: Preamble; Articles 14, 15, 19, 21, 21A, 25–30, 37, 39(f), 48A, 51, 243G and 253.



Related Acts / Procedures: Depends on the duty involved. Examples may include environmental laws, education laws, laws protecting public property, and laws concerning national symbols. These are ordinary laws separate from Article 51A.

Article 51A(a) — Respect for the Constitution, national institutions, National Flag and National Anthem

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active

Category: Fundamental Duties; Constitution; Citizenship



Accurate Explanation: Every citizen must abide by the Constitution and respect its ideals and institutions, the National Flag, and the National Anthem.



Simple Explanation: Citizens should respect the Constitution, constitutional institutions, the Indian National Flag, and the National Anthem.



Applies To: Every citizen.



Conditions: Respect for constitutional institutions does not remove a citizen’s right to lawful criticism, peaceful protest, or constitutional challenge.



Exceptions / Limitations: The clause does not prohibit lawful expression protected by the Constitution. The exact legal consequences of conduct concerning national symbols depend on relevant ordinary laws and facts.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to civic conduct, public events, government institutions, constitutional literacy, and respectful use of national symbols.



Related Articles: Preamble; Articles 19, 51A and 343.



Related Acts / Procedures: Prevention of Insults to National Honour Act, 1971, where applicable.



Article 51A(b) — Ideals of the freedom struggle

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active

Category: Fundamental Duties; Constitution; Citizenship



Accurate Explanation: Every citizen must cherish and follow the noble ideals that inspired India’s national struggle for freedom.



Simple Explanation: Citizens should value the democratic, freedom-oriented, equality-based, and justice-oriented ideals associated with India’s freedom movement.



Applies To: Every citizen.



Conditions: The Constitution does not provide a fixed statutory list of “noble ideals” for this clause.



Exceptions / Limitations: This provision does not create a separate criminal offence or a direct individual court remedy by itself.



Practical Relevance: Supports constitutional education, civic participation, democratic values, equality, and respect for liberty.



Related Articles: Preamble; Articles 14–32; Article 51A(a).



Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified by the Constitution.

Article 51A(c) — Sovereignty, unity and integrity of India

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active

Category: Fundamental Duties; Constitution; National Integrity



Accurate Explanation: Every citizen must uphold and protect the sovereignty, unity, and integrity of India.



Simple Explanation: Citizens should not act in ways that harm India’s independence, unity, or territorial integrity.



Applies To: Every citizen.



Conditions: Conduct affecting national security, unity, public order, or territorial integrity may also be regulated by valid criminal and public-order laws.



Exceptions / Limitations: This duty does not eliminate constitutional freedoms such as lawful speech, lawful association, or peaceful criticism. Restrictions on Article 19 freedoms must satisfy constitutional requirements.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to responsible civic conduct, public order, national security, and lawful democratic participation.



Related Articles: Preamble; Article 19(2), Article 19(3), Article 19(4), Article 355 and Article 51A(a).



Related Acts / Procedures: Relevant criminal, public-order, and national-security laws may apply depending on the specific conduct.

Article 51A(d) — Defence of the country and national service

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active

Category: Fundamental Duties; Constitution; National Service



Accurate Explanation: Every citizen must defend the country and render national service when called upon to do so.



Simple Explanation: Citizens have a duty to support the defence of India and provide national service if lawfully called upon.



Applies To: Every citizen.



Conditions: The duty to render national service arises when a citizen is lawfully called upon under an applicable constitutional or legal framework.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 51A(d) does not itself establish a general compulsory military-service system or set out the procedure for calling citizens to service.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to constitutional understanding of national defence, emergency service, and lawful calls for national service.



Related Articles: Articles 23(2), 33, 352 and 51A(c).



Related Acts / Procedures: Any applicable defence, emergency, or national-service law would determine the actual process.

Article 51A(e) — Harmony, brotherhood and dignity of women

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active

Category: Fundamental Duties; Equality; Women \& Children; Social Harmony



Accurate Explanation: Every citizen must promote harmony and the spirit of common brotherhood among all people of India, transcending religious, linguistic, regional, sectional, or diversities; and must renounce practices derogatory to the dignity of women.



Simple Explanation: Citizens should promote unity across religion, language, region, and community, and should reject practices that insult, harm, or reduce the dignity of women.



Applies To: Every citizen.



Conditions: Laws addressing discrimination, violence, harassment, and harmful practices may give enforceable legal consequences to particular conduct.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 51A(e) itself does not define every practice that is derogatory to women’s dignity or establish a separate complaint procedure.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to anti-discrimination awareness, women’s dignity, social harmony, prevention of gender-based harm, and respectful community conduct.



Related Articles: Articles 14, 15, 15(3), 17, 19, 21 and 51A(a).



Related Acts / Procedures: Relevant criminal laws, domestic-violence law, workplace-harassment law, and anti-discrimination protections may apply depending on facts.

Article 51A(f) — Preservation of composite culture

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active

Category: Fundamental Duties; Culture; Heritage



Accurate Explanation: Every citizen must value and preserve the rich heritage of India’s composite culture.



Simple Explanation: Citizens should respect and help preserve India’s diverse shared cultural heritage.



Applies To: Every citizen.



Conditions: Protection of a particular monument, heritage object, cultural practice, language, or site depends on applicable constitutional and statutory provisions.



Exceptions / Limitations: This duty does not authorise a citizen to interfere unlawfully with another person’s culture, religion, property, or lawful rights.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to heritage preservation, cultural respect, historical sites, languages, art, and shared national culture.



Related Articles: Articles 29, 49 and 51A(e).



Related Acts / Procedures: Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958, where applicable.

Article 51A(g) — Protection of the natural environment and compassion for living creatures

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active

Category: Fundamental Duties; Environment; Forests; Wildlife



Accurate Explanation: Every citizen must protect and improve the natural environment, including forests, lakes, rivers, and wildlife, and have compassion for living creatures.



Simple Explanation: Citizens should avoid harming nature, help protect forests, water bodies, wildlife, and show compassion toward animals.



Applies To: Every citizen.



Conditions: Specific environmental obligations, permissions, offences, and complaint routes arise from applicable environmental, forest, wildlife, pollution-control, and animal-protection laws.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 51A(g) does not itself create every environmental permit rule, wildlife offence, pollution standard, or animal-welfare procedure.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to pollution, waste disposal, protection of water bodies, forest and wildlife conservation, responsible animal treatment, and environmental awareness.



Related Articles: Articles 21, 48A, 51A(f), 243G and Eleventh Schedule.



Related Acts / Procedures: Environment (Protection) Act, 1986; Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972; Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980; Water and Air pollution-control legislation.

Article 51A(h) — Scientific temper, humanism, inquiry and reform

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active

Category: Fundamental Duties; Education; Citizenship



Accurate Explanation: Every citizen must develop scientific temper, humanism, and the spirit of inquiry and reform.



Simple Explanation: Citizens should think critically, value evidence and human dignity, ask questions, and support positive reform.



Applies To: Every citizen.



Conditions: This is a broad constitutional civic duty, not a detailed code of conduct.



Exceptions / Limitations: It does not allow a person to violate another person’s Fundamental Rights, religious freedom, dignity, or lawful interests in the name of reform or inquiry.



Practical Relevance: Supports evidence-based learning, education, scientific awareness, social reform, and informed civic participation.



Related Articles: Articles 19(1)(a), 21A, 25, 28, 41, 45 and 51A(b).



Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified by the Constitution.

Article 51A(i) — Safeguarding public property and abjuring violence

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active

Category: Fundamental Duties; Public Property; Public Order



Accurate Explanation: Every citizen must safeguard public property and abjure violence.



Simple Explanation: Citizens should not damage public property and should reject violence.



Applies To: Every citizen.



Conditions: Damage to government or public property, violence, rioting, and related conduct may be offences under applicable law.



Exceptions / Limitations: The duty does not prohibit lawful peaceful assembly, lawful protest, or legitimate criticism of the government. Article 19 rights remain subject to the Constitution and valid law.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to protests, public demonstrations, public infrastructure, transport, government buildings, and community safety.



Related Articles: Articles 19(1)(b), 19(2), 19(3), 21 and 51A(c).



Related Acts / Procedures: Prevention of Damage to Public Property Act, 1984; applicable criminal and public-order law.



Article 51A(j) — Striving for excellence

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active

Category: Fundamental Duties; Citizenship; Education



Accurate Explanation: Every citizen must strive toward excellence in all spheres of individual and collective activity so that the nation constantly rises to higher levels of endeavour and achievement.



Simple Explanation: Citizens should try to do their work and contribute to society as well as they can, individually and collectively.



Applies To: Every citizen.



Conditions: The provision is an aspirational civic duty.



Exceptions / Limitations: It does not establish a legal performance standard, employment obligation, examination rule, or direct court remedy.



Practical Relevance: Supports civic responsibility, educational effort, professional ethics, community participation, and national development.



Related Articles: Preamble; Articles 38, 41, 43 and 51A(b).



Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified by the Constitution.



Article 51A(k) — Educational opportunities for children aged 6–14

Provision Type: Fundamental Duty

Status: Active; inserted by the Constitution (Eighty-sixth Amendment) Act, 2002



Category: Fundamental Duties; Education; Women \& Children



Accurate Explanation: A parent or guardian must provide opportunities for education to their child or ward between the age of 6 and 14 years.



Simple Explanation: Parents and guardians should ensure that children aged 6–14 get the opportunity to attend school and receive education.



Applies To: Every citizen who is a parent or guardian of a child or ward aged 6–14 years.



Conditions: The child or ward must fall within the 6–14 age group.



Exceptions / Limitations: This duty should be read with Article 21A, which places the constitutional duty on the State to provide free and compulsory education for children aged 6–14. Article 51A(k) does not itself prescribe a separate penalty or procedure against every parent or guardian.



Practical Relevance: Encourages parents and guardians to enrol and support children in elementary education. It is directly connected to school-access and child-education awareness.



Related Articles: Articles 21A, 39(e), 39(f), 45 and 51A(h).



Related Acts / Procedures: Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act, 2009.



Part V — The Union

Part: Part V — The Union

Chapter I — The Executive: comprises the President and Vice-President (Articles 52–71), the President's power of pardon and the extent of Union executive power (Articles 72–73), the Council of Ministers (Articles 74–75), the Attorney-General for India (Article 76), and the conduct of Government business (Articles 77–78). This Chapter establishes India's parliamentary executive, in which the directly unelected President acts as constitutional Head of State while real executive power is exercised by a Council of Ministers responsible to the House of the People.



Article 52 — The President of India

Provision Type: Constitutional office — establishment

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President

Accurate Explanation: The Constitution establishes a single constitutional office of President of India, who is the Head of State and, under Article 53, in whom the executive power of the Union is formally vested.

Simple Explanation: India has one President, who is the formal Head of State of the country.

Applies To: The Union Government and all constitutional functions requiring Presidential action.

Conditions: None; this is a foundational, self-executing provision.

Exceptions / Limitations: This Article only creates the office; the President's actual powers, election, and qualifications are dealt with in the Articles that follow.

Practical Relevance: The President's assent, signature, or formal action is required for laws, ordinances, and various high constitutional appointments.

Related Articles: Articles 53, 54, 60, 74.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified by the Constitution.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Ministry of Law and Justice, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 53 — Executive power of the Union

Provision Type: Executive power — vesting provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President

Accurate Explanation: The executive power of the Union is vested in the President and is exercised by the President either directly or through officers subordinate to the President, in accordance with the Constitution. The President is also the Supreme Commander of the Defence Forces of the Union, and the exercise of this command is regulated by law. This Article does not prevent Parliament from conferring functions on authorities other than the President by law.

Simple Explanation: On paper, all executive authority of the central government flows from the President, but in practice it is exercised by the Council of Ministers and the administrative machinery on the President's behalf, as required by Article 74.

Applies To: The Union Government and its administrative and military functions.

Conditions: Must be read together with Article 74, under which the President acts on the aid and advice of the Council of Ministers.

Exceptions / Limitations: Does not by itself give the President personal or discretionary command of the armed forces; military command is regulated by law and exercised through the constitutional chain of ministerial responsibility. Parliament may by law vest functions in bodies other than the President.

Practical Relevance: Explains why executive decisions, notifications, and orders of the Union Government are issued "in the name of the President" even though the Council of Ministers takes the real decisions.

Related Articles: Articles 52, 74, 77.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified by the Constitution; military command regulated by ordinary law (e.g., service Acts).

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 54 — Election of President

Provision Type: Electoral provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President; Elections

Accurate Explanation: The President is elected by an electoral college consisting of the elected members of both Houses of Parliament and the elected members of the Legislative Assemblies of the States, and, by later constitutional amendment, of the Legislative Assemblies of the Union territories of Delhi and Puducherry as well. Nominated members of Parliament or State Legislative Assemblies do not participate in this election.

Simple Explanation: The President is not directly elected by all citizens; instead, elected MPs and elected MLAs across the country vote to choose the President.

Applies To: Elected Members of Parliament, elected MLAs of the States, and (by constitutional amendment) elected MLAs of the National Capital Territory of Delhi and of Puducherry.

Conditions: Voting follows the system of proportional representation by means of the single transferable vote, and voting is by secret ballot, as further detailed in Article 55.

Exceptions / Limitations: Nominated members of the Rajya Sabha, Lok Sabha, or State Assemblies, and MLAs of other Union territories without a Legislative Assembly, do not vote in a Presidential election.

Practical Relevance: Determines who is entitled to vote whenever a Presidential election is held, and explains why the Presidential election is indirect rather than a direct popular vote.

Related Articles: Articles 55, 58, 62, 71.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952; Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Rules, 1974; role of the Election Commission of India.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Election Commission of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 55 — Manner of election of President

Provision Type: Electoral provision — procedure

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President; Elections

Accurate Explanation: This Article requires, as far as practicable, uniformity in the scale of representation of the different States at the Presidential election, and a proper balance between the States and the Union as a whole. It lays down the formula for calculating the value of the vote of an elected MLA (linked to the population of the State and the number of elected members in its Assembly) and of an elected MP (based on the total value of all MLA votes divided by the number of elected MPs). The election is held in accordance with the system of proportional representation by means of the single transferable vote, and voting is by secret ballot.

Simple Explanation: This Article provides the mathematical formula that decides how much a single MLA's or MP's vote is worth in a Presidential election, so that all States are fairly represented regardless of their size.

Applies To: The mechanics of every Presidential election.

Conditions: The "population" figure used for these calculations has, since the Constitution (Eighty-fourth Amendment) Act, 2001, continued to be based on the 1971 census figures until the first census taken after the year 2026, so as not to disadvantage States that had controlled their population growth.

Exceptions / Limitations: This Article deals only with the value/weightage of votes, not with who is entitled to vote (which is Article 54) or with qualifications of candidates (Article 58).

Practical Relevance: Relevant to understanding why the Presidential election result is announced in terms of vote "value" rather than a simple headcount.

Related Articles: Articles 54, 82 (readjustment after census), 170.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952; Constitution (Eighty-fourth Amendment) Act, 2001; Constitution (Eighty-seventh Amendment) Act, 2003.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 56 — Term of office of President

Provision Type: Tenure provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President

Accurate Explanation: The President holds office for a term of five years from the date on which they enter upon their office. The President may resign by writing addressed to the Vice-President, may be removed by impeachment under Article 61, and continues in office beyond the five-year term until a successor enters upon office. The Vice-President must forthwith inform the Speaker of the Lok Sabha of a Presidential resignation.

Simple Explanation: The President serves for five years, but can resign, be removed through impeachment, and stays in office until the next President takes over, so there is no vacuum in the office.

Applies To: The sitting President of India.

Conditions: Resignation must be by writing addressed to the Vice-President; removal must follow the Article 61 impeachment process.

Exceptions / Limitations: A five-year term does not mean automatic continuation; re-election is required (see Article 57), and removal for constitutional violation is governed strictly by Article 61.

Practical Relevance: Explains the fixed tenure of the President and the constitutional mechanism for resignation, removal, and continuity of office.

Related Articles: Articles 57, 61, 62, 65.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified beyond the constitutional text.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 57 — Eligibility for re-election

Provision Type: Eligibility provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President

Accurate Explanation: A person who holds or has held office as President is, subject to the other provisions of the Constitution, eligible for re-election to that office. There is no constitutional limit on the number of terms a person may serve as President.

Simple Explanation: A former or sitting President can stand for the office again; the Constitution does not cap the number of times someone can be President.

Applies To: Any citizen who has previously held, or currently holds, the office of President.

Conditions: The person must still meet all the qualification requirements in Article 58 at the time of re-election.

Exceptions / Limitations: None specified in the constitutional text regarding the number of terms.

Practical Relevance: Confirms that India, unlike some countries, places no constitutional term limit on the Presidency.

Related Articles: Articles 56, 58, 62.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified by the Constitution.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026
