Article 58 — Qualifications for election as President

Provision Type: Eligibility / qualification provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President

Accurate Explanation: A person is not eligible for election as President unless they are a citizen of India, have completed 35 years of age, and are qualified for election as a member of the House of the People (Lok Sabha). A person is not eligible if they hold any office of profit under the Government of India, a State Government, or a local or other authority under the control of these Governments — except that holding the office of President, Vice-President, Governor, or Minister (Union or State) does not count as disqualifying "office of profit" for this purpose.

Simple Explanation: To become President, a person must be an Indian citizen, at least 35 years old, eligible to be a Lok Sabha MP, and must not be holding a government job or office of profit (though being President, Vice-President, Governor, or a Minister does not disqualify them).

Applies To: Every prospective candidate for the office of President.

Conditions: All qualifying conditions must be satisfied at the time of the election.

Exceptions / Limitations: The specific carve-outs (President, Vice-President, Governor, Minister) are expressly excluded from the "office of profit" bar.

Practical Relevance: Sets out the basic threshold for who may stand for the highest constitutional office in India.

Related Articles: Articles 54, 84 (qualification for Parliament membership), 102 (disqualifications).

Related Acts / Procedures: Representation of the People Act, 1951 (for Lok Sabha qualification standards referenced here).

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 59 — Conditions of President's office

Provision Type: Service conditions provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President

Accurate Explanation: The President must not be a member of either House of Parliament or of a House of a State Legislature; if such a member is elected President, they are deemed to have vacated that seat on the date they enter office. The President must not hold any other office of profit. The President is entitled, without payment of rent, to the use of official residences, and is entitled to such emoluments, allowances, and privileges as Parliament may by law determine, and until so determined, as specified in the Second Schedule; these emoluments and allowances cannot be diminished during the President's term of office.

Simple Explanation: While serving, the President cannot simultaneously be an MP or MLA or hold any other paid government post, gets official residences rent-free, and receives a salary and privileges fixed by Parliament (or, failing that, as set out in the Second Schedule) which cannot be reduced during the term.

Applies To: The sitting President of India.

Conditions: Applies for the entire duration of the term of office.

Exceptions / Limitations: The bar on "office of profit" here operates alongside, but is distinct from, the eligibility bar in Article 58.

Practical Relevance: Protects the independence and dignity of the office by insulating the President's remuneration from political pressure during the term.

Related Articles: Articles 58, 97, Second Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: The President's Emoluments and Pension Act, 1951, and related Parliamentary legislation on salary/allowances.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 60 — Oath or affirmation by the President

Provision Type: Procedural / ceremonial provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President

Accurate Explanation: Every President, and every person acting as President or discharging the President's functions, must, before entering upon office, make and subscribe an oath or affirmation before the Chief Justice of India (or, in the Chief Justice's absence, the senior-most available Judge of the Supreme Court), swearing to faithfully execute the office, and to the best of their ability, preserve, protect, and defend the Constitution and the law, and devote themselves to the service and well-being of the people of India.

Simple Explanation: Before taking charge, the President must formally swear (or affirm) to protect the Constitution and serve the people, with the Chief Justice of India administering the oath.

Applies To: Every incoming President, and any person temporarily discharging the President's functions.

Conditions: Must occur before the person actually starts performing Presidential functions.

Exceptions / Limitations: None; this is a mandatory constitutional formality without which office cannot be validly entered upon.

Practical Relevance: Marks the formal, legally significant moment when a person becomes vested with the powers of the Presidency.

Related Articles: Articles 56, 65, 70.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified beyond the oath text in the constitutional article itself.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 61 — Procedure for impeachment of the President

Provision Type: Removal / accountability provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President; Parliamentary Procedure

Accurate Explanation: When a President is to be impeached for violation of the Constitution, the charge is preferred by either House of Parliament. No such charge can be preferred unless the proposal is contained in a resolution moved after at least fourteen days' written notice signed by not less than one-fourth of the total membership of that House, and the resolution is passed by a majority of not less than two-thirds of the total membership of that House. Once a charge is so preferred, the other House investigates the charge (or causes it to be investigated), and the President has the right to appear and be represented at that investigation. If, as a result, a resolution declaring the charge sustained is passed by a majority of not less than two-thirds of the total membership of the investigating House, the President stands removed from office from the date the resolution is passed.

Simple Explanation: To remove the President, one House of Parliament must formally accuse the President of violating the Constitution with a two-thirds majority vote, after which the other House investigates; if that House also finds the charge proved by a two-thirds vote, the President is removed immediately.

Applies To: The sitting President of India.

Conditions: Requires action by both Houses of Parliament in sequence, each requiring a special two-thirds majority of total membership (not just members present and voting).

Exceptions / Limitations: This is a quasi-judicial parliamentary process, not a criminal trial; the Constitution does not itself define "violation of the Constitution." As of the last verification date, no Indian President has ever been impeached under this Article.

Practical Relevance: The sole constitutional mechanism for removing a sitting President against their will.

Related Articles: Articles 56, 60, 62.

Related Acts / Procedures: Procedure further regulated by the Rules of Procedure of the Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 62 — Time of holding election to fill vacancy in the office of President and the term of office of person elected to fill casual vacancy

Provision Type: Electoral timing provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President; Elections

Accurate Explanation: An election to fill a vacancy caused by the expiration of the President's term must be completed before the expiration of that term. An election to fill a vacancy caused by death, resignation, or removal, or otherwise, must be held as soon as possible after, and in no case later than six months from, the date of occurrence of the vacancy; the person elected to fill such a casual vacancy is entitled to hold office for a full new term of five years from the date of entering office, unless earlier removed, resigning, or dying.

Simple Explanation: A new President must be elected before the old President's term ends; if the office falls vacant suddenly (death, resignation, removal), a new election must happen within six months, and whoever is elected then serves a fresh full five-year term.

Applies To: The Election Commission and the electoral college under Article 54.

Conditions: The six-month outer limit applies specifically to casual (unplanned) vacancies.

Exceptions / Limitations: During any period when the office is vacant, the Vice-President discharges Presidential functions under Article 65, so the country is never without someone empowered to act as President.

Practical Relevance: Ensures continuity in the highest constitutional office and prevents prolonged vacancies.

Related Articles: Articles 56, 61, 65, 71.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 63 — The Vice-President of India

Provision Type: Constitutional office — establishment

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Vice-President

Accurate Explanation: The Constitution establishes a single office of Vice-President of India.

Simple Explanation: India has one Vice-President, a constitutional office distinct from the President.

Applies To: The Union Government.

Conditions: None; a foundational provision.

Exceptions / Limitations: This Article only creates the office; election, qualifications, and functions follow in later Articles.

Practical Relevance: Establishes the office that is, among other things, ex officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha and first in line to discharge Presidential functions during a vacancy.

Related Articles: Articles 64, 65, 66.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified by the Constitution.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 64 — The Vice-President to be ex officio Chairman of the Council of States

Provision Type: Functional provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Vice-President; Parliament

Accurate Explanation: The Vice-President is the ex officio Chairman of the Council of States (Rajya Sabha) and does not hold any other office of profit while functioning as Chairman.

Simple Explanation: By virtue of being Vice-President, the person automatically presides over the Rajya Sabha as its Chairman.

Applies To: The sitting Vice-President.

Conditions: This role is automatic upon becoming Vice-President; no separate election as Chairman is needed.

Exceptions / Limitations: When the Vice-President is acting as President or discharging Presidential functions under Article 65, they do not perform the duties of Rajya Sabha Chairman during that period.

Practical Relevance: Explains why the Vice-President, rather than an elected Speaker-equivalent, presides over Rajya Sabha proceedings.

Related Articles: Articles 63, 89, 90, 91.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in the Council of States.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 65 — The Vice-President to act as President or to discharge his functions during casual vacancies in the office, or during the absence, of President

Provision Type: Succession / continuity provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Vice-President; President

Accurate Explanation: If the office of President falls vacant due to death, resignation, removal, or otherwise, the Vice-President acts as President until a new President is elected under Article 62. When the President is unable to discharge functions owing to absence, illness, or any other cause, the Vice-President discharges those functions until the President resumes duties. While acting as President or discharging Presidential functions, the Vice-President has all the powers, immunities, and emoluments of the President and does not perform the duties of Rajya Sabha Chairman.

Simple Explanation: If the President cannot serve — whether because the post is vacant or the President is temporarily unavailable — the Vice-President steps in and exercises the President's powers during that period.

Applies To: The sitting Vice-President, in the specified contingencies.

Conditions: Applies only for the vacancy/absence period; ceases once a new President is elected or the sitting President resumes duties.

Exceptions / Limitations: If the Vice-President's own office is also vacant, Article 70 permits Parliament to make alternative arrangements.

Practical Relevance: A key continuity safeguard ensuring the President's functions are never left unperformed.

Related Articles: Articles 62, 64, 70.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified beyond the constitutional text.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 66 — Election of Vice-President

Provision Type: Electoral provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Vice-President; Elections

Accurate Explanation: The Vice-President is elected by members of an electoral college consisting of the members of both Houses of Parliament (elected and nominated), voting in accordance with the system of proportional representation by means of the single transferable vote, with voting by secret ballot. Unlike Presidential elections, State Legislative Assembly members do not participate. The qualifications for a Vice-President candidate are: citizenship of India, completion of 35 years of age, and qualification for election as a member of the Council of States (Rajya Sabha); the same "office of profit" bar and carve-outs that apply to the Presidency also apply here. A sitting member of either House of Parliament or a State Legislature who is elected Vice-President is deemed to have vacated that seat on entering office.

Simple Explanation: The Vice-President is elected only by MPs (both nominated and elected, from both Houses), not by MLAs, and must meet similar age, citizenship, and eligibility conditions as the President.

Applies To: Members of both Houses of Parliament, and every prospective candidate for Vice-President.

Conditions: The candidate must be qualified for Rajya Sabha membership; disputes about elections are dealt with under Article 71.

Exceptions / Limitations: No person shall be nominated for election as Vice-President as a representative of a State Legislature (unlike Presidential elections, where State Assemblies participate).

Practical Relevance: Explains why the Vice-President's electoral college is narrower than the President's, being confined to Parliament.

Related Articles: Articles 63, 71, 84, 102.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 67 — Term of office of Vice-President

Provision Type: Tenure provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Vice-President

Accurate Explanation: The Vice-President holds office for a term of five years from the date of entering office, subject to earlier resignation (by writing addressed to the President), removal by a Rajya Sabha resolution passed by a majority of all its then members and agreed to by the Lok Sabha (with at least fourteen days' advance notice of the resolution), and continuation in office beyond the five-year term until a successor enters upon office.

Simple Explanation: The Vice-President serves five years but can resign, be removed by a Rajya Sabha resolution agreed to by the Lok Sabha, and stays on until the next Vice-President takes charge.

Applies To: The sitting Vice-President of India.

Conditions: Removal requires an effective majority in the Rajya Sabha plus Lok Sabha concurrence, following at least fourteen days' notice — a different and comparatively simpler process than Presidential impeachment.

Exceptions / Limitations: No formal "impeachment" trial as under Article 61; removal is by ordinary resolution procedure of the two Houses.

Practical Relevance: Sets out how and when a Vice-President's term can end other than by completing the full five years.

Related Articles: Articles 63, 68, 90.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure of the Rajya Sabha and Lok Sabha governing such resolutions.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 68 — Time of holding election to fill vacancy in the office of Vice-President and the term of office of person elected to fill casual vacancy

Provision Type: Electoral timing provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Vice-President; Elections

Accurate Explanation: An election to fill a vacancy caused by expiration of the Vice-President's term must be completed before the expiration of the term. An election to fill a casual vacancy (death, resignation, removal, or otherwise) must be held as soon as possible after the vacancy occurs, and the person so elected is entitled to hold office for a full new term of five years from the date of entering office.

Simple Explanation: A new Vice-President is elected in time to succeed the outgoing one, or promptly if the post falls vacant unexpectedly, and then serves a fresh five-year term.

Applies To: The Election Commission and Parliament's electoral college for this office.

Conditions: No strict six-month outer limit is specified in the text for the Vice-President (unlike the President under Article 62), but the election must occur "as soon as possible."

Exceptions / Limitations: None specified beyond the timing requirement.

Practical Relevance: Ensures the office of Vice-President, including its Rajya Sabha chairmanship function, is not left vacant for long.

Related Articles: Articles 62, 65, 67.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 69 — Oath or affirmation by the Vice-President

Provision Type: Procedural / ceremonial provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Vice-President

Accurate Explanation: Every Vice-President must, before entering upon office, make and subscribe an oath or affirmation before the President or a person appointed by the President, swearing to bear true faith and allegiance to the Constitution and to faithfully discharge the duties of the office.

Simple Explanation: Before starting the job, the Vice-President must formally swear (or affirm) loyalty to the Constitution, with the President (or someone the President appoints) administering the oath.

Applies To: Every incoming Vice-President.

Conditions: Must occur before the person begins performing the functions of the office.

Exceptions / Limitations: None; a mandatory constitutional formality.

Practical Relevance: Marks the formal start of the Vice-President's term and legal authority.

Related Articles: Articles 60, 67.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified beyond the oath text in the constitutional article itself.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 70 — Discharge of President's functions in other contingencies

Provision Type: Residual / fallback provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President; Vice-President

Accurate Explanation: Parliament may make such provision as it thinks fit for the discharge of the functions of the President in any contingency not provided for elsewhere in Chapter I of Part V — for example, if both the President's and Vice-President's offices are simultaneously vacant or unavailable.

Simple Explanation: This is a safety-net provision letting Parliament decide what to do if an unusual situation arises that the other Articles do not directly cover — such as both top constitutional offices being empty at once.

Applies To: Parliament, in framing such contingency legislation.

Conditions: Only operates for situations genuinely not covered by Articles 56–69.

Exceptions / Limitations: In practice this power has rarely needed to be exercised, since Article 65 already covers the ordinary vacancy/absence scenario.

Practical Relevance: A constitutional safeguard against extreme, unforeseen gaps in executive continuity.

Related Articles: Articles 56, 62, 65, 67, 68.

Related Acts / Procedures: President (Discharge of Functions) Act, 1969.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 71 — Matters relating to, or connected with, the election of a President or Vice-President

Provision Type: Electoral dispute-resolution provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President; Vice-President; Elections

Accurate Explanation: All doubts and disputes arising out of or in connection with the election of a President or Vice-President are inquired into and decided exclusively by the Supreme Court, whose decision is final. If the election of a person as President or Vice-President is declared void by the Supreme Court, acts done by that person in the exercise of the powers of the office before the date of the Supreme Court's decision are not invalidated by that decision. Parliament may by law regulate matters relating to or connected with such elections, including grounds on which they may be questioned.

Simple Explanation: Only the Supreme Court can hear and decide challenges to a Presidential or Vice-Presidential election, and even if it later voids the election, official acts already done by that person remain valid.

Applies To: Any person challenging a Presidential or Vice-Presidential election, and the Supreme Court.

Conditions: The Supreme Court has exclusive original jurisdiction over such disputes; no other court can entertain them.

Exceptions / Limitations: The protection for prior official acts applies only up to the date the Supreme Court actually declares the election void.

Practical Relevance: Ensures certainty and stability of governance even while an election dispute is being litigated.

Related Articles: Articles 54, 66, 132.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Supreme Court of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 72 — Power of President to grant pardons, etc., and to suspend, remit or commute sentences in certain cases

Provision Type: Executive clemency power

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; President; Criminal Justice

Accurate Explanation: The President has the power to grant pardons, reprieves, respites, or remissions of punishment, or to suspend, remit, or commute the sentence of any person convicted of an offence in three categories of cases: (a) where the punishment or sentence is by a court martial; (b) where the punishment or sentence is for an offence against any law relating to a matter to which the Union's executive power extends; and (c) in all cases where the sentence is a sentence of death. This power does not affect powers conferred by any other law on the Governor, military, or other authorities, and, per settled Supreme Court interpretation, is exercised on the aid and advice of the Council of Ministers, subject to limited judicial review for arbitrariness or mala fide exercise.

Simple Explanation: The President can forgive or reduce the punishment of a convicted person in certain categories of cases, especially death sentences and court-martial or Union-law convictions, though in practice this decision is taken on the advice of the central government, not the President personally.

Applies To: Persons convicted under court martial, Union law, or sentenced to death anywhere in India.

Conditions: The power is exercised on ministerial advice; courts have held that it can be reviewed (though narrowly) if exercised arbitrarily, for extraneous reasons, or without application of mind.

Exceptions / Limitations: Distinct from and wider in one respect (death sentences under any law) than the Governor's parallel pardon power under Article 161, which does not extend to court-martial sentences and, per Supreme Court rulings, does not independently extend to death sentences beyond State law offences in the same way.

Practical Relevance: The final stage of relief available to a death-row convict or certain other convicts after exhausting judicial appeals.

Related Articles: Articles 74, 161.

Related Acts / Procedures: Code of Criminal Procedure / Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita provisions on mercy petitions; relevant Supreme Court decisions including Kehar Singh v. Union of India, Maru Ram v. Union of India, and Epuru Sudhakar v. Government of Andhra Pradesh.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Supreme Court of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 73 — Extent of executive power of the Union

Provision Type: Federal distribution provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Centre-State Relations

Accurate Explanation: Subject to the Constitution, the executive power of the Union extends to matters on which Parliament has power to make laws, and to the exercise of rights, authority, and jurisdiction exercisable by the Government of India under any treaty or agreement. However, in matters falling within the Concurrent List (List III), the executive power of the Union does not, except as expressly provided by the Constitution or by Parliament, extend to a State unless expressly provided.

Simple Explanation: The central government's executive authority generally matches the subjects Parliament can legislate on, but for subjects both the Centre and States can legislate on (the Concurrent List), the Centre does not automatically get to administer them in the States unless the Constitution or a specific law says so.

Applies To: The Union Government's exercise of executive functions across the country.

Conditions: Must be read with the Seventh Schedule's Union, State, and Concurrent Lists, and with Article 256–257 on Union directions to States.

Exceptions / Limitations: The Concurrent List carve-out preserves State executive autonomy in day-to-day administration of jointly-legislated subjects.

Practical Relevance: Central to understanding the federal division of executive authority between the Union and the States.

Related Articles: Articles 162, 256, 257, Seventh Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified by the Constitution beyond the Seventh Schedule framework.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 74 — Council of Ministers to aid and advise President

Provision Type: Core executive-accountability provision

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Council of Ministers; President

Accurate Explanation: There is a Council of Ministers with the Prime Minister at its head to aid and advise the President, who must act in accordance with such advice in the exercise of functions. The President may require the Council of Ministers to reconsider such advice, either generally or otherwise, but must act in accordance with the advice tendered after such reconsideration. The question whether, and if so what, advice was tendered by Ministers to the President cannot be inquired into by any court.

Simple Explanation: Although the President is the formal Head of State, real decision-making power lies with the Council of Ministers led by the Prime Minister; the President can ask the Council to reconsider advice once, but must then follow it, and courts cannot investigate what advice was actually given.

Applies To: The President and the Union Council of Ministers.

Conditions: Originally, before the Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976, it was debated whether the President was bound to follow ministerial advice; the binding nature was made explicit by that amendment, and the "reconsideration" proviso was added by the Constitution (Forty-fourth Amendment) Act, 1978.

Exceptions / Limitations: The bar on judicial inquiry into the advice (clause 2) protects the confidentiality of Cabinet deliberations, though the final decision/order itself remains subject to judicial review on other grounds.

Practical Relevance: The foundational provision establishing India's parliamentary system of government, under which the elected Council of Ministers, not the President, effectively governs.

Related Articles: Articles 53, 75, 78.

Related Acts / Procedures: Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976; Constitution (Forty-fourth Amendment) Act, 1978; relevant Supreme Court decisions including Shamsher Singh v. State of Punjab.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Supreme Court of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 75 — Other provisions as to Ministers

Provision Type: Composition, size, and accountability provision

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Council of Ministers; Prime Minister

Accurate Explanation: The Prime Minister is appointed by the President, and other Ministers are appointed by the President on the Prime Minister's advice. The total number of Ministers, including the Prime Minister, in the Union Council of Ministers cannot exceed fifteen per cent of the total membership of the House of the People (a limit inserted by the Constitution (Ninety-first Amendment) Act, 2003). A member of either House of Parliament who is disqualified from House membership for defection under the Tenth Schedule is also disqualified from being appointed a Minister for a specified period (also inserted by the Ninety-first Amendment). Ministers hold office during the pleasure of the President. The Council of Ministers is collectively responsible to the House of the People. Before entering office, a Minister takes oaths of office and secrecy per the Third Schedule. A Minister who is not a member of either House of Parliament for any continuous period of six months ceases to be a Minister at the end of that period. Ministers' salaries and allowances are as Parliament may from time to time by law determine, and, until so determined, as specified in the Second Schedule.

Simple Explanation: The Prime Minister is appointed by the President, and other Ministers on the PM's recommendation; there is now a hard cap (15% of Lok Sabha strength) on Cabinet size, a defector cannot be made a Minister, the whole Council answers collectively to the Lok Sabha, and a Minister must become an MP within six months of taking office or lose the post.

Applies To: The Prime Minister and all Union Ministers.

Conditions: The 15% ceiling and anti-defection disqualification are calculated against the current strength of the House of the People at the relevant time.

Exceptions / Limitations: "Pleasure of the President" in practice means Ministers can be removed on the Prime Minister's advice, in keeping with Article 74; collective responsibility means the whole Council can fall if it loses the confidence of the Lok Sabha.

Practical Relevance: Directly governs the size, composition, and constitutional accountability of the Union Cabinet and Council of Ministers, and was central to curbing "oversized" ministries after 2003.

Related Articles: Articles 74, 88, 164, Tenth Schedule, Second Schedule, Third Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Constitution (Ninety-first Amendment) Act, 2003; Salary, Allowances and Pension of Members of Parliament Act, 1954, and related Ministers' salaries legislation.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 76 — Attorney-General for India

Provision Type: Constitutional law-officer provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Legal Officers

Accurate Explanation: The President appoints a person qualified to be a Judge of the Supreme Court as Attorney-General for India, whose duty is to give advice to the Government of India on legal matters referred by the President, to perform other legal duties assigned by the President, and to discharge functions conferred by the Constitution or any other law. In performing these duties, the Attorney-General has the right of audience in all courts in India, and the right to speak in and otherwise take part in the proceedings of either House of Parliament or their committees (without the right to vote). The Attorney-General holds office during the pleasure of the President, and receives remuneration as the President determines.

Simple Explanation: The Attorney-General is the Union Government's top law officer, giving legal advice to the government, allowed to appear in any Indian court and to speak (but not vote) in Parliament, and serves at the President's pleasure (in practice, on the government's advice).

Applies To: The Union Government's legal representation and advice.

Conditions: Must meet the same qualifications required to be appointed a Supreme Court Judge.

Exceptions / Limitations: The Attorney-General is not a full-time government servant in the ordinary civil-service sense and may engage in private legal practice subject to conflict-of-interest restrictions, unlike the Solicitor-General and Additional Solicitors-General, who are statutory/administrative appointments, not constitutional ones under this Article.

Practical Relevance: The Attorney-General frequently represents the Union of India in significant constitutional litigation before the Supreme Court.

Related Articles: Articles 124, 88.

Related Acts / Procedures: Law Officers (Terms and Conditions) Rules; conventions governing the Attorney-General's private practice.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 77 — Conduct of business of the Government of India

Provision Type: Administrative / procedural provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Government Business

Accurate Explanation: All executive action of the Government of India is expressed to be taken in the name of the President. Orders and instruments made and executed in the President's name are authenticated in the manner specified in rules made by the President, and the validity of an order or instrument so authenticated cannot be questioned on the ground that it was not made or executed by the President personally. The President makes rules for the more convenient transaction of the business of the Government of India, and for the allocation of that business among Ministers.

Simple Explanation: Every official act of the central government is legally treated as an act "of the President," even though it is actually carried out by ministries and officials, and the President's own rules set out how government business is organised and divided among Ministers.

Applies To: All Union Government departments, Ministries, and officials acting in the President's name.

Conditions: Requires proper authentication under rules made under this Article — commonly the Government of India (Transaction of Business) Rules and Allocation of Business Rules.

Exceptions / Limitations: The requirement that action be taken "in the name of the President" is a legal formality, not a requirement of the President's personal involvement in each decision.

Practical Relevance: Explains why government notifications, orders, and gazette entries are worded "in the name of the President of India."

Related Articles: Articles 53, 74, 166 (parallel provision for States).

Related Acts / Procedures: Government of India (Transaction of Business) Rules, 1961; Government of India (Allocation of Business) Rules, 1961.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 78 — Duties of Prime Minister as respects the furnishing of information to the President, etc.

Provision Type: Information / accountability provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Executive; Prime Minister; President

Accurate Explanation: It is the duty of the Prime Minister to communicate to the President all decisions of the Council of Ministers relating to the administration of the affairs of the Union and proposals for legislation, and to furnish such information relating to the administration of the affairs of the Union and proposals for legislation as the President may call for. If the President so requires, the Prime Minister must submit for the consideration of the Council of Ministers any matter on which a decision has been taken by an individual Minister but which has not been considered by the Council.

Simple Explanation: The Prime Minister must keep the President informed of Cabinet decisions and legislative proposals, answer the President's questions about government administration, and, if the President asks, bring an individual Minister's decision before the full Cabinet for collective consideration.

Applies To: The Prime Minister of India, in relation to the President.

Conditions: The duty operates continuously through the Prime Minister's tenure and is triggered specifically when the President exercises the right to call for information or referral.

Exceptions / Limitations: This is an information/communication channel; it does not give the President power to reverse or veto Cabinet decisions, which remain governed by Article 74.

Practical Relevance: Ensures the President — who under Article 74 must generally act on ministerial advice — is nonetheless kept meaningfully informed of government functioning and can require full Cabinet consideration of significant individual ministerial decisions.

Related Articles: Articles 74, 75, 77.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified beyond the constitutional text and Cabinet Secretariat procedures.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026
Chapter II — Parliament

Part: Part V — The Union, Chapter II — Parliament

Scope: Covers the constitution and composition of Parliament (Articles 79–82), duration and qualifications (83–84), sessions and Presidential addresses (85–88), Officers of Parliament (89–98), conduct of business and disqualifications (99–104), powers/privileges/salaries (105–106), legislative procedure including Money Bills (107–111), financial procedure and the Union Budget (112–117), and procedure generally, including the bar on courts questioning parliamentary proceedings (118–122). Chapter III (Article 123) deals with the President's ordinance-making power.



Article 79 — Constitution of Parliament

Provision Type: Constitutional structure — establishment

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament

Accurate Explanation: There is a Parliament for the Union, consisting of the President and two Houses, known as the Council of States (Rajya Sabha) and the House of the People (Lok Sabha).

Simple Explanation: India's Parliament is not just the two Houses — the President is constitutionally part of Parliament too, alongside the Rajya Sabha and the Lok Sabha.

Applies To: The Union legislative structure as a whole.

Conditions: None; a foundational provision.

Exceptions / Limitations: The President does not sit or vote in either House but is essential to the legislative process (assent to Bills, summoning, proroguing, dissolving).

Practical Relevance: Explains why an Act of Parliament is not "law" until it also receives Presidential assent under Article 111.

Related Articles: Articles 80, 81, 111, 123.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified by the Constitution.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 80 — Composition of the Council of States

Provision Type: Composition provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Rajya Sabha

Accurate Explanation: The Rajya Sabha consists of not more than 250 members, of whom up to 12 are nominated by the President from persons having special knowledge or practical experience in literature, science, art, or social service, and not more than 238 are representatives of the States and Union territories. Representatives of each State are elected by the elected members of that State's Legislative Assembly in accordance with the system of proportional representation by means of the single transferable vote. Representation of Union territories in the Rajya Sabha is as Parliament may by law provide.

Simple Explanation: The Rajya Sabha is a maximum 250-member "Council of States," made up mostly of members elected by State Assemblies (not directly by the public), plus up to 12 members nominated by the President for their expertise in fields like literature, science, art, or social service.

Applies To: State Legislative Assemblies (as electors) and the President (for nominations).

Conditions: The actual seat allocation to each State/Union territory is fixed under the Fourth Schedule.

Exceptions / Limitations: Rajya Sabha members are not directly elected by voters, unlike Lok Sabha members.

Practical Relevance: Rajya Sabha functions as a body representing the States and is a permanent (non-dissolvable) chamber, distinguishing it structurally from the Lok Sabha.

Related Articles: Articles 83, 89, Fourth Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Representation of the People Act, 1950 and 1951.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 81 — Composition of the House of the People

Provision Type: Composition provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Lok Sabha

Accurate Explanation: The Lok Sabha consists of members chosen by direct election from territorial constituencies in the States (not more than 530 in total) and members representing Union territories (not more than 20), chosen in the manner Parliament provides by law. Seats are allocated among the States so that, as far as practicable, the ratio between a State's seats and its population is the same across States, and each State is divided into territorial constituencies so that, as far as practicable, the ratio between the population of each constituency and its seats is the same throughout the State. Under a constitutional freeze, the total number of seats allotted to each State (based on the 1971 Census) and the division of each State into territorial constituencies (based on the 2001 Census) remain unchanged until the relevant figures for the first Census taken after the year 2026 are published.

Simple Explanation: The Lok Sabha's roughly 543 current members are directly elected by voters across the country, with seat numbers per State currently still based on 1971 population figures — a freeze meant to avoid penalising States that controlled population growth — pending the next revision after the post-2026 Census.

Applies To: Voters and constituencies across India.

Conditions: The freeze mechanism was introduced by the Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976, and extended by the Constitution (Eighty-fourth Amendment) Act, 2001.

Exceptions / Limitations: A Government proposal in 2026 — the Constitution (One Hundred and Thirty-First Amendment) Bill, 2026 — sought to raise the Lok Sabha's maximum strength to 850 seats and delink delimitation from the post-2026 Census freeze (using the 2011 Census instead), but the Bill was voted down in the Lok Sabha on 17 April 2026 for want of the required two-thirds special majority; the pre-existing text of Articles 81 and 82, including the freeze until the first Census after 2026, therefore continues to be the operative constitutional position as of the last verification date.

Practical Relevance: Determines how many MPs each State currently sends to the Lok Sabha and is central to ongoing "delimitation" and women's-reservation debates.

Related Articles: Articles 82, 170, 330A, 334A.

Related Acts / Procedures: Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976; Constitution (Eighty-fourth Amendment) Act, 2001; Constitution (Eighty-seventh Amendment) Act, 2003; Delimitation Act, 2002; failed Constitution (One Hundred and Thirty-First Amendment) Bill, 2026.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; PRS Legislative Research (for 2026 Bill status).

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 82 — Readjustment after each census

Provision Type: Delimitation provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Elections; Delimitation

Accurate Explanation: After every Census, the allocation of Lok Sabha seats to the States and the division of States into territorial constituencies is to be readjusted by a law made by Parliament, in the manner Parliament may determine (typically through a Delimitation Commission), but subject to the freeze under Article 81 until the first Census after 2026, and subject to the requirement that such readjustment does not affect representation in the Lok Sabha until the dissolution of the then-existing House.

Simple Explanation: After each Census, Parliament is supposed to redraw constituency boundaries and reallocate seats to keep representation proportional to population, though the numeric allocation itself is currently frozen until after the next Census following 2026.

Applies To: The Delimitation Commission and Parliament.

Conditions: Readjustment is carried out through a Delimitation Act and an independent Delimitation Commission, not by direct parliamentary vote on individual boundaries.

Exceptions / Limitations: As with Article 81, the proposed 2026 constitutional amendment package to alter this delimitation timeline did not pass; the existing freeze framework continues to apply.

Practical Relevance: Governs when and how India's parliamentary map is redrawn, directly affecting political representation.

Related Articles: Articles 81, 170.

Related Acts / Procedures: Delimitation Act, 2002; Delimitation Commission Act framework.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; PRS Legislative Research.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 83 — Duration of Houses of Parliament

Provision Type: Tenure provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament

Accurate Explanation: The Rajya Sabha is a permanent body and is not subject to dissolution, but one-third of its members retire every second year in accordance with law made by Parliament. The Lok Sabha, unless sooner dissolved, continues for five years from its first meeting date, after which it automatically dissolves. While a Proclamation of Emergency under Article 352 is in operation, Parliament may by law extend the Lok Sabha's term beyond five years for a period not exceeding one year at a time, and in no case beyond six months after the Proclamation ceases to operate.

Simple Explanation: The Rajya Sabha never dissolves — a third of its members rotate out every two years — while the Lok Sabha normally serves a five-year term and then dissolves, though its term can be extended year-by-year during a national Emergency.

Applies To: Both Houses of Parliament.

Conditions: Lok Sabha extension is only possible during an operative Emergency under Article 352.

Exceptions / Limitations: This extension power was notably used during the 1975–77 Emergency; the Constitution (Forty-fourth Amendment) Act, 1978, tightened Emergency safeguards generally, indirectly limiting future misuse of this extension route.

Practical Relevance: Explains the structural permanence of the Rajya Sabha versus the periodic, election-based renewal of the Lok Sabha.

Related Articles: Articles 85, 352.

Related Acts / Procedures: Representation of the People Act, 1951 (for Rajya Sabha retirement cycles).

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 84 — Qualification for membership of Parliament

Provision Type: Eligibility provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Elections

Accurate Explanation: A person is not qualified to be chosen to fill a seat in Parliament unless they are a citizen of India, make and subscribe an oath or affirmation in the prescribed form before a person authorised by the Election Commission, are at least 30 years of age for the Council of States and at least 25 years of age for the House of the People, and possess such other qualifications as may be prescribed by or under any law made by Parliament.

Simple Explanation: To become an MP, a person must be an Indian citizen, take a prescribed oath before a designated official, be at least 30 (for Rajya Sabha) or 25 (for Lok Sabha) years old, and meet any additional conditions Parliament has laid down by law.

Applies To: Every prospective candidate for Parliament.

Conditions: Additional qualifications (such as being a registered elector) are prescribed under the Representation of the People Act, 1951.

Exceptions / Limitations: This Article deals only with basic qualification; disqualifications are separately dealt with under Article 102.

Practical Relevance: Sets the baseline eligibility bar for anyone seeking to become a Member of Parliament.

Related Articles: Articles 58, 66, 102.

Related Acts / Procedures: Representation of the People Act, 1951.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Election Commission of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 85 — Sessions of Parliament, prorogation and dissolution

Provision Type: Procedural / calendar provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Sessions

Accurate Explanation: The President summons each House of Parliament to meet at such time and place as considered fit, but six months must not intervene between the last sitting of one session and the date appointed for the first sitting of the next session. The President may from time to time prorogue either House, and may dissolve the Lok Sabha.

Simple Explanation: The President formally calls Parliament into session and can end a session (prorogation) or dissolve the Lok Sabha altogether, but there cannot be a gap of more than six months between two sessions.

Applies To: Both Houses of Parliament, through Presidential action taken on ministerial advice.

Conditions: The six-month maximum gap is the only fixed constitutional requirement on session frequency; the earlier practice of ensuring at least two or three sessions a year is a convention rather than a separate express constitutional mandate distinct from this gap rule.

Exceptions / Limitations: Summoning, prorogation, and dissolution are formally Presidential acts but are exercised on the aid and advice of the Council of Ministers under Article 74, except in narrow situations of a caretaker government.

Practical Relevance: Explains the constitutional basis for Parliament's Budget, Monsoon, and Winter sessions, and the process by which a Lok Sabha term ends before completing five years.

Related Articles: Articles 83, 74, 87.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in Lok Sabha/Rajya Sabha.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 86 — Right of President to address and send messages to Houses

Provision Type: Presidential parliamentary-engagement provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; President

Accurate Explanation: The President may address either House or both Houses assembled together, and may require the attendance of members for that purpose. The President may also send messages to either House, whether with respect to a Bill pending in Parliament or otherwise, and a House to which a message is sent must consider with due despatch any matter required by the message to be considered.

Simple Explanation: Besides the annual formal address under Article 87, the President can also speak to Parliament on other occasions or send it written messages that the House must take up promptly.

Applies To: Either or both Houses of Parliament.

Conditions: Exercised on the advice of the Council of Ministers in ordinary practice.

Exceptions / Limitations: This is distinct from the special address under Article 87, which is a specific, recurring constitutional obligation.

Practical Relevance: Provides a formal channel of communication from the executive Head of State to the legislature outside the routine law-making process.

Related Articles: Articles 85, 87.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified beyond the constitutional text.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 87 — Special address by the President

Provision Type: Presidential parliamentary-engagement provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; President

Accurate Explanation: At the commencement of the first session after each general election to the Lok Sabha, and at the commencement of the first session of each year, the President addresses both Houses assembled together and informs Parliament of the causes of its summons. Rules regulating the procedure of either House may provide for an allotment of time for discussion of the matters referred to in this address.

Simple Explanation: The President delivers a formal opening address to a joint sitting of Parliament at the start of a new Lok Sabha and at the start of each year's first session — commonly referred to as the "President's Address" — setting out the government's agenda, which is then debated.

Applies To: Both Houses of Parliament, assembled jointly for the address.

Conditions: The address content is prepared by the Union Government (in practice, the Council of Ministers), since the President acts on ministerial advice.

Exceptions / Limitations: This is a fixed, recurring constitutional obligation, unlike the discretionary addresses/messages under Article 86.

Practical Relevance: Marks the ceremonial and substantive opening of each parliamentary year and each new Lok Sabha, followed by the customary "Motion of Thanks" debate.

Related Articles: Articles 85, 86.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha (Motion of Thanks).

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 88 — Rights of Ministers and Attorney-General as respects Houses

Provision Type: Access / participation provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Council of Ministers; Attorney-General

Accurate Explanation: Every Minister and the Attorney-General for India have the right to speak in, and otherwise take part in the proceedings of, either House of Parliament, any joint sitting of the Houses, and any parliamentary committee of which they may be named a member, without being entitled to vote unless they are also a member of that House.

Simple Explanation: Ministers and the Attorney-General can attend and speak in either House of Parliament (even if they are only a member of one House, or of neither, as with some nominated law officers), but they can only vote in a House of which they are actually an elected/nominated member.

Applies To: All Union Ministers and the Attorney-General for India.

Conditions: The right to speak does not extend a right to vote beyond ordinary membership rules.

Exceptions / Limitations: A Minister who is not a member of either House for six consecutive months ceases to be a Minister under Article 75(5), independent of this Article's participation right.

Practical Relevance: Allows a Minister from the Rajya Sabha to answer questions or pilot a Bill in the Lok Sabha (and vice versa), and allows the Attorney-General to assist Parliament on legal matters directly.

Related Articles: Articles 75, 76, 105.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Officers of Parliament

Article 89 — The Chairman and Deputy Chairman of the Council of States

Provision Type: Presiding-officer provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Rajya Sabha; Officers of Parliament

Accurate Explanation: The Vice-President of India is the ex officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha (as also stated in Article 64). The Rajya Sabha chooses one of its own members to be Deputy Chairman, and, so often as the office falls vacant, the House chooses another member to fill it.

Simple Explanation: The Vice-President automatically presides over the Rajya Sabha as its Chairman, while the House itself elects one of its own members as Deputy Chairman to step in when needed.

Applies To: The Rajya Sabha and its presiding officers.

Conditions: The Deputy Chairman must be an elected/nominated member of the Rajya Sabha.

Exceptions / Limitations: Unlike the Chairman (who holds the post by virtue of being Vice-President), the Deputy Chairman is directly chosen by and from the House.

Practical Relevance: Provides for an alternate presiding officer to run Rajya Sabha proceedings when the Chairman is unavailable.

Related Articles: Articles 64, 90, 91, 92.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in the Council of States.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 90 — Vacation and resignation of, and removal from, the office of Deputy Chairman

Provision Type: Tenure / removal provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Rajya Sabha; Officers of Parliament

Accurate Explanation: The Deputy Chairman vacates office if they cease to be a member of the Rajya Sabha, may resign by writing addressed to the Chairman, and may be removed from office by a resolution of the Rajya Sabha passed by a majority of all its then members, provided at least fourteen days' notice of the intention to move the resolution has been given.

Simple Explanation: The Deputy Chairman loses the post automatically on losing Rajya Sabha membership, can quit by writing to the Chairman, or can be voted out by a majority of the full House after proper notice.

Applies To: The sitting Deputy Chairman of the Rajya Sabha.

Conditions: Removal requires an absolute majority of the House's total membership, not just members present and voting.

Exceptions / Limitations: The Deputy Chairman cannot preside over the House while a removal resolution against themself is under consideration, per Article 92.

Practical Relevance: Provides an accountable mechanism for changing the Rajya Sabha's Deputy Chairman.

Related Articles: Articles 89, 91, 92.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in the Council of States.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 91 — Power of the Deputy Chairman or other person to perform the duties of the office of, or to act as, Chairman

Provision Type: Continuity provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Rajya Sabha; Officers of Parliament

Accurate Explanation: While the office of Chairman is vacant, or while the Vice-President is acting as, or discharging the functions of, President, the duties of the office of Chairman are performed by the Deputy Chairman, or, if the office of Deputy Chairman is also vacant, by such member of the Rajya Sabha as the President may appoint for the purpose.

Simple Explanation: If the Chairman's post is empty (or the Vice-President is temporarily acting as President), the Deputy Chairman takes over Rajya Sabha proceedings, and if even that post is vacant, the President appoints another member to fill in.

Applies To: The Rajya Sabha and its presiding-officer arrangements.

Conditions: Applies only during the specified contingencies (vacancy in Chairman's office, or the Vice-President acting as President).

Exceptions / Limitations: This is purely a continuity mechanism, not a permanent transfer of the Chairmanship.

Practical Relevance: Ensures Rajya Sabha proceedings can continue smoothly even if both top presiding-officer posts are simultaneously affected.

Related Articles: Articles 65, 89, 90.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in the Council of States.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 92 — The Chairman or the Deputy Chairman not to preside while a resolution for his removal from office is under consideration

Provision Type: Procedural safeguard

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Rajya Sabha; Officers of Parliament

Accurate Explanation: While a resolution for the removal of the Vice-President (acting in capacity as Chairman) or the Deputy Chairman is under consideration in the Rajya Sabha, that person does not preside over the sitting, though they retain the right to be present and speak in, and take part in, the proceedings of the House during such consideration, and to vote in the first instance on such resolution or on any other matter during such proceedings, subject to the tie-breaking casting-vote rule under Article 100 not applying to them in that situation in the ordinary way.

Simple Explanation: If the House is debating a motion to remove its own presiding officer, that officer cannot chair the debate, though they can still attend, speak, and vote on the resolution itself, just not preside over it.

Applies To: The Vice-President (as Rajya Sabha Chairman) and the Deputy Chairman, specifically during their own removal proceedings.

Conditions: Applies only for the duration of the specific removal-resolution debate.

Exceptions / Limitations: This is a natural-justice safeguard preventing a presiding officer from controlling debate on their own removal.

Practical Relevance: Reflects the constitutional principle that no one should judge or control proceedings concerning their own removal.

Related Articles: Articles 67, 89, 90.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in the Council of States.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 93 — The Speaker and Deputy Speaker of the House of the People

Provision Type: Presiding-officer provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Lok Sabha; Officers of Parliament

Accurate Explanation: The Lok Sabha chooses two of its own members to be Speaker and Deputy Speaker, and, whenever either office falls vacant, the House chooses another member to fill it.

Simple Explanation: Unlike the Rajya Sabha (whose Chairman is the Vice-President by default), the Lok Sabha elects its own Speaker and Deputy Speaker from among its members.

Applies To: The Lok Sabha and its presiding officers.

Conditions: Both must be sitting members of the Lok Sabha at the time of election to these posts.

Exceptions / Limitations: The Speaker's office has some continuity protections not applicable to the Deputy Speaker; see Article 94.

Practical Relevance: The Speaker is the principal presiding authority over Lok Sabha proceedings, rulings on procedure, and, notably, certification of Money Bills under Article 110.

Related Articles: Articles 94, 95, 96, 110.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in Lok Sabha.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 94 — Vacation and resignation of, and removal from, the offices of Speaker and Deputy Speaker

Provision Type: Tenure / removal provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Lok Sabha; Officers of Parliament

Accurate Explanation: The Speaker or Deputy Speaker vacates office if they cease to be a member of the Lok Sabha; either may resign by writing addressed to the other (the Speaker resigns to the Deputy Speaker, and vice versa); either may be removed by a resolution of the Lok Sabha passed by a majority of all its then members, provided at least fourteen days' notice has been given. However, the Speaker does not vacate office upon dissolution of the Lok Sabha and continues in office until immediately before the first meeting of the next Lok Sabha.

Simple Explanation: Both officers lose the post if they stop being MPs, can resign to each other, or can be removed by a majority vote of the whole House after notice — but uniquely, the Speaker keeps functioning even after the Lok Sabha is dissolved, right up until a new Speaker is chosen by the newly elected House.

Applies To: The sitting Speaker and Deputy Speaker of the Lok Sabha.

Conditions: Removal requires an absolute majority of total Lok Sabha membership.

Exceptions / Limitations: The Speaker's continuity beyond dissolution is a special safeguard not extended in the same terms to the Deputy Speaker.

Practical Relevance: Ensures there is always a functioning presiding authority for the Lok Sabha, even during the gap between one Lok Sabha's dissolution and the next one's formation.

Related Articles: Articles 93, 95, 96.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in Lok Sabha.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 95 — Power of the Deputy Speaker or other person to perform the duties of the office of, or to act as, Speaker

Provision Type: Continuity provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Lok Sabha; Officers of Parliament

Accurate Explanation: While the office of Speaker is vacant, the duties of the office are performed by the Deputy Speaker, or, if that office is also vacant, by such member of the Lok Sabha as the President may appoint for the purpose. The Deputy Speaker (or such other member) also performs the Speaker's duties during any period the Speaker is absent from a sitting.

Simple Explanation: If the Speaker's post is empty or the Speaker is away from a sitting, the Deputy Speaker (or another member appointed by the President, if needed) takes over presiding duties.

Applies To: The Lok Sabha and its presiding-officer arrangements.

Conditions: Applies during vacancy of the Speaker's office or the Speaker's temporary absence from a sitting.

Exceptions / Limitations: A continuity mechanism only; it does not amount to a permanent transfer of the Speakership.

Practical Relevance: Ensures Lok Sabha sittings can proceed without interruption even if the Speaker is unavailable.

Related Articles: Articles 93, 94, 96.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in Lok Sabha.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 96 — The Speaker or the Deputy Speaker not to preside while a resolution for his removal from office is under consideration

Provision Type: Procedural safeguard

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Lok Sabha; Officers of Parliament

Accurate Explanation: While a resolution for the removal of the Speaker or Deputy Speaker is under consideration, that person does not preside at the sitting of the Lok Sabha, though they have the right to be present and speak in, and take part in, the proceedings of the House during such consideration, and to vote in the first instance on such resolution or other matters, though not to exercise a casting vote in the case of equality of votes on the resolution itself.

Simple Explanation: An officer facing a removal motion cannot chair that particular debate, though they can still be present, speak, and vote on the underlying resolution.

Applies To: The Speaker and Deputy Speaker, specifically during their own removal proceedings.

Conditions: Applies only for the duration of the specific removal-resolution debate.

Exceptions / Limitations: A natural-justice safeguard analogous to Article 92 for the Rajya Sabha.

Practical Relevance: Prevents a presiding officer from controlling the proceedings on their own removal.

Related Articles: Articles 92, 93, 94.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in Lok Sabha.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 97 — Salaries and allowances of the Chairman and Deputy Chairman and the Speaker and Deputy Speaker

Provision Type: Remuneration provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Officers of Parliament

Accurate Explanation: The Chairman and Deputy Chairman of the Rajya Sabha, and the Speaker and Deputy Speaker of the Lok Sabha, are paid such salaries and allowances as Parliament may by law determine, and, until so determined, such salaries and allowances as specified in the Second Schedule.

Simple Explanation: These four presiding officers receive salaries fixed by parliamentary law, with the Second Schedule providing a fallback figure if Parliament has not separately legislated.

Applies To: The Chairman, Deputy Chairman, Speaker, and Deputy Speaker.

Conditions: In practice, Parliament has enacted dedicated salary legislation for these offices.

Exceptions / Limitations: Distinct from ordinary MPs' salaries under Article 106.

Practical Relevance: Ensures these key constitutional offices have a legally secure and defined remuneration structure.

Related Articles: Articles 89, 93, 106, Second Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Salaries and Allowances of Officers of Parliament Act, 1953.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 98 — Secretariat of Parliament

Provision Type: Administrative / staffing provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Administration

Accurate Explanation: Each House of Parliament has a separate secretarial staff, though Parliament may by law provide for the extent to which any common posts may be created and staffed for both Houses. Parliament may by law regulate the recruitment and conditions of service of persons appointed to the secretarial staff of either House.

Simple Explanation: The Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha each have their own independent administrative staff (the Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha Secretariats), separate from the executive government's civil service, with their recruitment and service conditions governed by parliamentary law.

Applies To: The administrative and secretarial staff of both Houses of Parliament.

Conditions: Governed by parliamentary law and internal service rules framed under it.

Exceptions / Limitations: This keeps Parliament's own staff institutionally independent of the executive branch's civil service control.

Practical Relevance: Underpins the operational independence of Parliament's day-to-day functioning, including committee support and legislative drafting assistance.

Related Articles: Articles 79, 118.

Related Acts / Procedures: Parliament (Prevention of Disqualification) Act and Lok Sabha/Rajya Sabha Secretariat service rules made under parliamentary law.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Conduct of Business

Article 99 — Oath or affirmation by members

Provision Type: Procedural / ceremonial provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Members

Accurate Explanation: Every member of either House of Parliament must, before taking their seat, make and subscribe before the President, or a person appointed by the President for that purpose, an oath or affirmation in the form set out in the Third Schedule.

Simple Explanation: Before an MP can officially start functioning as a member, they must take a prescribed oath (or affirmation) in the form laid down in the Third Schedule.

Applies To: Every newly elected or nominated Member of Parliament.

Conditions: Must occur before the member takes their seat in the House.

Exceptions / Limitations: A member who sits or votes before taking this oath is liable to the penalty specified in Article 104.

Practical Relevance: A mandatory formality that legally activates a person's status as a functioning Member of Parliament.

Related Articles: Articles 60, 69, 104, Third Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified beyond the oath text in the Third Schedule.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 100 — Voting in Houses, power of Houses to act notwithstanding vacancies and quorum

Provision Type: Procedural provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Procedure

Accurate Explanation: Except as otherwise provided in the Constitution, all questions in either House (or joint sitting) are decided by a majority of votes of members present and voting, other than the Speaker or presiding officer, who does not vote in the first instance but has and exercises a casting vote in the case of equality of votes. Either House may act notwithstanding any vacancy in its membership, and proceedings remain valid even if it is later discovered that a person who sat, voted, or otherwise participated was not entitled to do so. The quorum to constitute a sitting of either House is one-tenth of the total number of members of the House; if there is no quorum, it is the duty of the presiding officer to adjourn the House or suspend the sitting until there is a quorum.

Simple Explanation: Ordinary decisions in Parliament are made by simple majority of those present and voting, the presiding officer normally doesn't vote except to break a tie, business isn't invalidated just because some seats are vacant, and a minimum of one-tenth of a House's total membership must be present for proceedings to continue.

Applies To: Both Houses of Parliament and joint sittings.

Conditions: "Otherwise provided in the Constitution" refers to matters requiring special/absolute majorities, such as constitutional amendments under Article 368 or certain removal resolutions.

Exceptions / Limitations: This general voting rule yields to any specific majority requirement elsewhere in the Constitution.

Practical Relevance: Sets the default voting and quorum rules governing everyday parliamentary business.

Related Articles: Articles 96, 105, 118, 368.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Disqualifications of Members

Article 101 — Vacation of seats

Provision Type: Membership-termination provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Members; Disqualifications

Accurate Explanation: No person can be a member of both Houses of Parliament at the same time; Parliament may by law make provisions for vacating the seat of a person chosen as a member of both Houses. No person can be a member both of Parliament and of a State Legislature; if such a person is elected to both, their seat in Parliament becomes vacant unless they resign the State Legislature seat within the time prescribed by rules made by the President. A member's seat becomes vacant if they incur any disqualification under Article 102. A member may resign their seat by writing addressed to the Chairman/Speaker, and the seat becomes vacant on acceptance of the resignation (or, in certain cases, automatically if the resignation is not voluntary or genuine, subject to verification). If a member is absent from all sittings of a House for sixty days without the House's permission, the House may declare their seat vacant, though periods during which the House is prorogued or adjourned for more than four consecutive days are excluded from this sixty-day count.

Simple Explanation: An MP cannot simultaneously hold a seat in both Houses of Parliament, or in Parliament and a State Legislature at once; incurring a disqualification, resigning in writing, or being absent from the House for sixty continuous days without permission (excluding certain breaks) will cause the seat to fall vacant.

Applies To: Every sitting Member of Parliament.

Conditions: Absence-based vacation requires the House's own determination, not automatic operation of law.

Exceptions / Limitations: The Speaker/Chairman must be satisfied a resignation is voluntary and genuine before accepting it, per settled practice and rulings.

Practical Relevance: Governs how and when an MP's seat can fall vacant other than through the end of their electoral term.

Related Articles: Articles 102, 190 (parallel State provision).

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha; Representation of the People Act, 1951 (bye-election procedure).

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 102 — Disqualifications for membership

Provision Type: Eligibility / disqualification provision

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Members; Disqualifications

Accurate Explanation: A person is disqualified for being chosen as, and for being, a member of either House of Parliament if they: hold any office of profit under the Government of India or a State Government (other than an office excluded by Parliament by law); are of unsound mind and so declared by a competent court; are an undischarged insolvent; are not a citizen of India, or have voluntarily acquired citizenship of a foreign State, or are under any acknowledgement of allegiance or adherence to a foreign State; or are disqualified under any law made by Parliament. A person is also disqualified if disqualified under the Tenth Schedule (anti-defection law), a clause inserted by the Constitution (Fifty-second Amendment) Act, 1985.

Simple Explanation: An MP can be disqualified for holding certain government offices of profit, being declared of unsound mind, being an undischarged bankrupt, issues of citizenship or foreign allegiance, disqualification under election-related laws (such as for corrupt practices or certain criminal convictions), or for defecting from their party under the Tenth Schedule.

Applies To: Every Member of Parliament and every prospective candidate.

Conditions: "Office of profit" disqualification is subject to specific statutory exemptions Parliament has enacted for numerous offices.

Exceptions / Limitations: Disqualification under laws made by Parliament (such as the Representation of the People Act, 1951) covers matters like conviction for certain offences and corrupt election practices.

Practical Relevance: Frequently invoked in "office of profit" controversies and anti-defection disqualification cases against sitting legislators.

Related Articles: Articles 101, 103, 191, Tenth Schedule.
