Related Acts / Procedures: Representation of the People Act, 1951; Parliament (Prevention of Disqualification) Act, 1959; Tenth Schedule (anti-defection law, inserted by the 52nd Amendment, 1985).

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Election Commission of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 103 — Decision on questions as to disqualifications of members

Provision Type: Adjudicatory provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Members; Disqualifications

Accurate Explanation: If any question arises whether a sitting member of either House has become subject to any of the disqualifications under Article 102, the question is referred for the decision of the President, whose decision is final. Before giving any decision, the President is required to obtain the opinion of the Election Commission and to act according to that opinion.

Simple Explanation: Disputes about whether a sitting MP has become disqualified are decided by the President, but only after — and effectively in line with — the Election Commission's opinion, which the President is bound to follow.

Applies To: Sitting Members of Parliament facing disqualification questions (post-election disputes; pre-election candidacy disputes are handled differently under election law).

Conditions: The Election Commission's opinion is a mandatory precondition to the President's decision, and Presidential practice treats it as binding.

Exceptions / Limitations: This Article covers disqualification under Article 102 generally; disqualification specifically under the Tenth Schedule (anti-defection) is instead decided by the Chairman or Speaker of the relevant House, subject to judicial review, not by this Article 103 process.

Practical Relevance: Provides the constitutional mechanism for resolving "office of profit" and similar post-election disqualification disputes against sitting MPs.

Related Articles: Articles 102, 192 (parallel State provision), Tenth Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Election Commission of India procedures for such references.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Election Commission of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 104 — Penalty for sitting and voting before making oath or affirmation under Article 99 or when not qualified or when disqualified

Provision Type: Penal provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Members; Disqualifications

Accurate Explanation: If a person sits or votes as a member of either House of Parliament before complying with the oath/affirmation requirement of Article 99, or when they know they are not qualified, or are disqualified for membership, or are barred from sitting/voting by any parliamentary law, they are liable, for every day they so sit or vote, to a penalty (specified by parliamentary law) recoverable as a debt due to the Union.

Simple Explanation: An MP who sits or votes without taking the required oath, or while knowingly unqualified or disqualified, can be fined for each day they wrongly did so, and the government can recover that fine like any other debt owed to it.

Applies To: Any Member of Parliament acting outside their constitutional authority to sit or vote.

Conditions: Liability requires the member's knowledge of their disqualification or lack of qualification, per the constitutional text.

Exceptions / Limitations: The specific penalty amount is fixed by parliamentary law, not the Constitution itself.

Practical Relevance: A deterrent and enforcement mechanism backing up the qualification and oath requirements of Articles 84, 99, and 102.

Related Articles: Articles 84, 99, 102.

Related Acts / Procedures: Parliament (Prevention of Disqualification) Act, 1959, and related parliamentary penalty legislation.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Powers, Privileges and Immunities of Parliament and its Members

Article 105 — Powers, privileges, etc., of the Houses of Parliament and of the members and committees thereof

Provision Type: Parliamentary privilege provision

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Privileges

Accurate Explanation: Subject to the Constitution and parliamentary rules, there is freedom of speech in Parliament. No member of Parliament is liable to any court proceedings in respect of anything said or any vote given by them in Parliament or any committee thereof, and no person is liable for the publication, by or under the authority of a House, of any report, paper, votes, or proceedings. In other respects, the powers, privileges, and immunities of each House, its members, and its committees are as may from time to time be defined by Parliament by law, and, until so defined, are those of that House, its members, and its committees immediately before the coming into force of Section 15 of the Constitution (Forty-fourth Amendment) Act, 1978. These provisions also apply to Ministers and the Attorney-General in respect of their right to speak in, and take part in the proceedings of, either House or its committees.

Simple Explanation: MPs enjoy freedom of speech inside Parliament and cannot be sued or prosecuted in court for anything they say or how they vote there, and Parliament's other powers/privileges are defined by parliamentary law rather than, as originally, by reference to the UK House of Commons.

Applies To: Members of Parliament, parliamentary committees, and (for the speech/participation privileges) Ministers and the Attorney-General.

Conditions: The original text (before the 44th Amendment) tied undefined privileges to those of the UK House of Commons "at the commencement of the Constitution"; this reference was removed and replaced with the current formulation.

Exceptions / Limitations: Privilege does not immunise conduct outside the actual proceedings of the House/committee, and courts have clarified (e.g., regarding bribery for votes/speech) that certain criminal conduct connected to voting is not shielded merely because it relates to a vote — this remains a debated and evolving area of constitutional interpretation following Supreme Court rulings such as Sita Soren v. Union of India (2023), which held that legislators do not enjoy immunity for bribery even if linked to a vote or speech in the House.

Practical Relevance: Central to debates on parliamentary conduct, disciplinary action against members, and the limits of legislative immunity from judicial and criminal process.

Related Articles: Articles 88, 122, 194 (parallel State provision).

Related Acts / Procedures: Constitution (Forty-fourth Amendment) Act, 1978; relevant Supreme Court decisions including P.V. Narasimha Rao v. State (1998) and Sita Soren v. Union of India (2023).

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Supreme Court of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 106 — Salaries and allowances of members

Provision Type: Remuneration provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Members

Accurate Explanation: Members of either House of Parliament are entitled to receive such salaries and allowances as Parliament may from time to time by law determine.

Simple Explanation: MPs' salaries and allowances are fixed by Parliament through ordinary legislation, not by the Constitution's own text.

Applies To: All Members of Parliament.

Conditions: The applicable law may be amended by Parliament from time to time.

Exceptions / Limitations: Distinct from the specific salary provisions for presiding officers under Article 97.

Practical Relevance: The constitutional basis for the statute governing MPs' pay, allowances, and pension.

Related Articles: Articles 97, 105.

Related Acts / Procedures: Salary, Allowances and Pension of Members of Parliament Act, 1954.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Legislative Procedure

Article 107 — Provisions as to introduction and passing of Bills

Provision Type: Legislative-procedure provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Legislative Procedure

Accurate Explanation: Subject to Articles 109 (Money Bills) and 117 (Financial Bills), a Bill may originate in either House of Parliament, and is not deemed passed unless agreed to by both Houses, with or without amendments. A Bill pending in Parliament does not lapse by reason of prorogation of the Houses. A Bill pending in the Rajya Sabha which has not been passed by the Lok Sabha does not lapse on dissolution of the Lok Sabha. A Bill which is pending in the Lok Sabha, or which has been passed by the Lok Sabha and is pending in the Rajya Sabha, lapses on dissolution of the Lok Sabha.

Simple Explanation: Ordinary Bills can start in either House and need approval from both to become law; proroguing a session doesn't kill a pending Bill, but dissolving the Lok Sabha does kill any Bill that was still with the Lok Sabha (or had reached the Rajya Sabha only after the Lok Sabha passed it) — though a Bill sitting only in the Rajya Sabha survives dissolution of the Lok Sabha.

Applies To: All ordinary (non-Money, non-Financial) Bills introduced in Parliament.

Conditions: Money Bills can only originate in the Lok Sabha (Article 109); Financial Bills involving expenditure need Presidential recommendation (Article 117).

Exceptions / Limitations: The Rajya Sabha's permanence (it is never dissolved) is precisely why Bills pending only there survive a Lok Sabha dissolution.

Practical Relevance: Explains why some pending Bills are re-introduced after each new Lok Sabha while others are not.

Related Articles: Articles 108, 109, 117, 111.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 108 — Joint sitting of both Houses in certain cases

Provision Type: Deadlock-resolution provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Legislative Procedure

Accurate Explanation: If a Bill (other than a Money Bill or a Bill required to be passed with a special majority under a constitutional-amendment procedure) passed by one House is rejected by the other, or the Houses have finally disagreed on amendments, or more than six months elapse from the Bill's receipt by the other House without its being passed (excluding periods of prorogation or adjournment beyond four consecutive days), the President may notify their intention to summon both Houses to meet in a joint sitting for the purpose of deliberating and voting on the Bill. Unless the Lok Sabha has been dissolved in the meantime, the President may summon such a joint sitting, at which the Bill is decided by a majority of the total members of both Houses present and voting.

Simple Explanation: If the two Houses cannot agree on an ordinary Bill, the President can call a joint sitting where all MPs from both Houses vote together, and because the Lok Sabha is much larger than the Rajya Sabha, this mechanism tends to favour the Lok Sabha's position.

Applies To: Ordinary Bills stuck in an inter-House deadlock.

Conditions: Does not apply to Money Bills (which follow the special Article 109 procedure) or to Bills requiring the special constitutional-amendment majority under Article 368.

Exceptions / Limitations: This mechanism has been used sparingly in India's history (for example, for the Dowry Prohibition Act, 1961, the Banking Service Commission (Repeal) Bill, 1978, and the Prevention of Terrorism Bill, 2002).

Practical Relevance: The primary constitutional tool for resolving a genuine legislative deadlock between the Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha on an ordinary Bill.

Related Articles: Articles 107, 109, 111.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure for joint sittings, made under Article 118.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 109 — Special procedure in respect of Money Bills

Provision Type: Special legislative-procedure provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Legislative Procedure; Money Bills

Accurate Explanation: A Money Bill cannot be introduced in the Rajya Sabha and can only be introduced in the Lok Sabha, on the recommendation of the President. After the Lok Sabha passes a Money Bill, it is transmitted to the Rajya Sabha for its recommendations; the Rajya Sabha must return the Bill to the Lok Sabha within fourteen days of receipt, with or without recommendations. The Lok Sabha may accept or reject any or all of the Rajya Sabha's recommendations. If the Rajya Sabha does not return the Bill within the fourteen-day period, the Bill is deemed to have been passed by both Houses at the expiration of that period, in the form in which it was passed by the Lok Sabha.

Simple Explanation: Only the Lok Sabha can introduce a Money Bill, the Rajya Sabha gets just fourteen days to suggest changes (which the Lok Sabha can accept or ignore), and if the Rajya Sabha does nothing within that window, the Bill automatically counts as passed exactly as the Lok Sabha sent it.

Applies To: Bills certified as Money Bills under Article 110.

Conditions: The Presidential recommendation is required specifically for introduction of the Money Bill in the Lok Sabha.

Exceptions / Limitations: The Rajya Sabha effectively has no power to block or substantially alter a genuine Money Bill, which is precisely why classification disputes under Article 110 (discussed below) are constitutionally significant.

Practical Relevance: Explains why some major legislation is passed through the Lok Sabha alone using the Money Bill route, generating ongoing constitutional controversy about the scope of "only" in Article 110(1).

Related Articles: Articles 107, 110, 117.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 110 — Definition of "Money Bills"

Provision Type: Definitional provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Legislative Procedure; Money Bills

Accurate Explanation: A Bill is a Money Bill if it contains only provisions dealing with one or more of: imposition, abolition, remission, alteration, or regulation of any tax; regulation of Union Government borrowing or guarantees; custody of the Consolidated Fund of India or Contingency Fund of India, and payments into or withdrawals from such Funds; appropriation of money out of the Consolidated Fund of India; declaring any expenditure to be charged on the Consolidated Fund of India, or increasing such charged expenditure; receipt of money on account of the Consolidated Fund of India or the public account of India, custody or issue of such money, or audit of Union or State accounts; or any matter incidental to any of these. A Bill is not a Money Bill merely because it provides for the imposition of fines or other pecuniary penalties, demands or payment of fees for licences or services rendered, or the imposition, abolition, remission, alteration, or regulation of any tax by a local authority for local purposes. If any question arises whether a Bill is a Money Bill, the decision of the Speaker of the Lok Sabha is final, and every such Bill, when transmitted to the Rajya Sabha or presented to the President for assent, bears the Speaker's certificate.

Simple Explanation: Article 110 lists the specific financial matters that make a Bill a "Money Bill"; the Speaker of the Lok Sabha alone decides whether a Bill qualifies, and that certification is stated by the Constitution to be final and conclusive.

Applies To: Bills dealing with taxation, government funds, and related financial matters.

Conditions: For a Bill to be a Money Bill, it must deal only with the listed matters — a Bill containing significant non-financial provisions alongside financial ones may not qualify.

Exceptions / Limitations: Although Article 110(3) declares the Speaker's decision "final," the Supreme Court has held (Rojer Mathew v. South Indian Bank, 2019) that this finality does not completely bar judicial review, though the scope of review is narrow; the correctness of the earlier Aadhaar judgment (K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India, 2018, which upheld the Aadhaar Act's certification as a Money Bill by a 4:1 majority) on the meaning of the word "only" in Article 110(1) has been referred to a larger seven-judge Constitution Bench, whose ruling remains pending as of the last verification date.

Practical Relevance: This is one of the most consequential and currently unsettled areas of Indian constitutional law, since Money Bill classification determines whether the Rajya Sabha's role in a piece of legislation is largely bypassed.

Related Articles: Articles 109, 117, 122.

Related Acts / Procedures: Relevant Supreme Court decisions including K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India (2018) and Rojer Mathew v. South Indian Bank Ltd. (2019); pending seven-judge Bench reference.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Supreme Court of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 111 — Assent to Bills

Provision Type: Final legislative-approval provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; President; Legislative Procedure

Accurate Explanation: When a Bill has been passed by both Houses of Parliament, it is presented to the President, who may either assent to the Bill, withhold assent, or, in the case of a Bill other than a Money Bill, return the Bill (if not a Money Bill) with a message requesting the Houses to reconsider it or any specified provisions, and, in particular, to consider the desirability of introducing any amendments. If the Houses pass the Bill again, with or without amendments, and present it to the President again, the President is required to give assent to the Bill.

Simple Explanation: After Parliament passes a Bill, the President can sign it into law, withhold assent, or (except for Money Bills) send it back once for reconsideration — but if Parliament passes it again, the President must then sign it.

Applies To: All Bills passed by both Houses of Parliament and presented for Presidential assent.

Conditions: The "return for reconsideration" option is not available for Money Bills, which the President may only assent to or withhold assent from.

Exceptions / Limitations: The Constitution does not specify a time limit within which the President must act on a Bill, an ambiguity sometimes called the "pocket veto" issue; unlike the express timelines the Supreme Court has, in recent years, read into the parallel Governor's power over State Bills under Article 200, no equivalent binding timeline has been definitively imposed on the President's power under this Article as of the last verification date.

Practical Relevance: The final constitutional step by which a parliamentary Bill becomes an Act of Parliament and enforceable law.

Related Articles: Articles 79, 109, 200 (parallel State provision).

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified beyond the constitutional text; relevant judicial commentary in State of Tamil Nadu v. Governor of Tamil Nadu and related Article 200 litigation, to the extent it discusses analogous assent-related principles.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Supreme Court of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Procedure in Financial Matters

Article 112 — Annual financial statement

Provision Type: Budgetary provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Finance; Union Budget

Accurate Explanation: The President causes to be laid before both Houses of Parliament, for each financial year, a statement of the estimated receipts and expenditure of the Government of India for that year, referred to as the "annual financial statement" (commonly called the Union Budget). This statement must distinguish expenditure charged on the Consolidated Fund of India (such as the President's and Judges' salaries) from other expenditure proposed to be met from the Consolidated Fund of India, and must show separately capital and revenue expenditure.

Simple Explanation: Every year, the government presents Parliament with a detailed statement of expected income and spending — the Union Budget — clearly separating spending that is automatically "charged" and not voted on from spending that Parliament must actually approve.

Applies To: The Union Government's annual budgeting process.

Conditions: Must be laid before both Houses; presented typically at the start of the Budget Session.

Exceptions / Limitations: Charged expenditure (e.g., under Article 112 read with Articles 125, 146, 148, 202) may be discussed by Parliament but is not subject to a vote.

Practical Relevance: The constitutional foundation of India's annual Union Budget process.

Related Articles: Articles 113, 114, 202 (parallel State provision).

Related Acts / Procedures: General Financial Rules; Budget Manual of the Ministry of Finance.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Ministry of Finance.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 113 — Procedure in Parliament with respect to estimates

Provision Type: Budgetary-procedure provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Finance; Union Budget

Accurate Explanation: Estimates of expenditure charged on the Consolidated Fund of India are not submitted to the vote of Parliament, though they may be discussed. Other estimates are submitted to the Lok Sabha in the form of demands for grants, and the Lok Sabha has the power to assent to, refuse to assent to, or assent to any demand subject to a reduction of the amount specified; no demand for a grant can be made except on the recommendation of the President.

Simple Explanation: Non-charged budget items are placed before the Lok Sabha as "demands for grants" that MPs can approve, reject, or cut, while automatically charged items can only be debated, not voted down, and the President must formally recommend every demand before it is even placed for consideration.

Applies To: The Union Budget approval process in the Lok Sabha.

Conditions: Only the Lok Sabha votes on demands for grants; the Rajya Sabha discusses but does not vote on them.

Exceptions / Limitations: The Presidential-recommendation requirement is a formality exercised on ministerial advice, but is constitutionally mandatory for validity.

Practical Relevance: The mechanism through which the Lok Sabha exercises the "power of the purse" over the government's discretionary annual spending.

Related Articles: Articles 112, 114, 117.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure of Lok Sabha (Demands for Grants, Cut Motions).

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 114 — Appropriation Bills

Provision Type: Budgetary-enactment provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Finance; Union Budget

Accurate Explanation: As soon as possible after the grants under Article 113 have been made by the Lok Sabha, a Bill is introduced to provide for the appropriation out of the Consolidated Fund of India of all money required to meet those grants and the expenditure charged on the Consolidated Fund of India. No amendment can be proposed to such a Bill in either House that would vary the amount or alter the destination of any grant so made, or vary the amount of any charged expenditure. No money can be withdrawn from the Consolidated Fund of India except under an appropriation made by law passed in accordance with this Article.

Simple Explanation: Once the Lok Sabha approves the various spending demands, Parliament passes a single "Appropriation Bill" that legally authorises the government to actually draw that money from the Consolidated Fund, and MPs cannot amend it to change the amounts already voted.

Applies To: The final legal authorisation stage of the annual Union Budget process.

Conditions: Passage follows the ordinary Article 107 procedure but with this Article's specific amendment restriction.

Exceptions / Limitations: This restriction preserves the integrity of the grants already voted by the Lok Sabha, preventing them from being reopened during the Appropriation Bill stage.

Practical Relevance: No government expenditure from the Consolidated Fund is lawful without a corresponding Appropriation Act.

Related Articles: Articles 112, 113, 266.

Related Acts / Procedures: Annual Appropriation Acts.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 115 — Supplementary, additional or excess grants

Provision Type: Budgetary-adjustment provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Finance; Union Budget

Accurate Explanation: If the amount authorised for a service in the current financial year is found to be insufficient, or a need arises for additional expenditure on a new service not contemplated in the original annual financial statement, or money has been spent in a financial year in excess of the amount granted for a service, the President causes a supplementary, additional, or excess-demand statement to be laid before both Houses, and the same procedure as for the original demands for grants and Appropriation Bill applies to such statements and expenditure.

Simple Explanation: If the original Budget turns out to be insufficient, or the government needs to spend on something new, or it has already overspent, Parliament is asked to separately approve that additional or "excess" spending through the same voting and Appropriation Bill process used for the main Budget.

Applies To: Mid-year or after-the-fact budgetary corrections and additions.

Conditions: Excess-grant statements (for money already spent beyond the sanctioned grant) require post-facto Parliamentary regularisation, typically after examination by the Public Accounts Committee.

Exceptions / Limitations: This does not permit the government to spend without eventual parliamentary sanction; it only structures how additional/excess spending is later authorised.

Practical Relevance: The constitutional basis for "Supplementary Demands for Grants" regularly presented in Parliament.

Related Articles: Articles 112, 113, 114.

Related Acts / Procedures: Public Accounts Committee procedures; Comptroller and Auditor-General reporting under Article 151.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 116 — Votes on account, votes of credit and exceptional grants

Provision Type: Interim-financing provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Finance; Union Budget

Accurate Explanation: The Lok Sabha has the power to make a grant in advance for part of a financial year pending completion of the full budgetary approval process (a "vote on account"), to make a grant for meeting an unexpected demand upon India's resources whose magnitude or indefinite character cannot be stated with the usual budgetary detail (a "vote of credit"), and to make an exceptional grant which forms no part of the current service of any financial year. Parliament may by law authorise the withdrawal of money from the Consolidated Fund of India for the purposes of such grants, and these votes/grants are otherwise dealt with in the same manner as ordinary demands for grants and the corresponding Appropriation Act.

Simple Explanation: Parliament can approve a temporary "vote on account" to keep government spending going while the full Budget is still being debated, a flexible "vote of credit" for unforeseen large needs, or a one-off "exceptional grant" outside the usual annual pattern.

Applies To: Interim or extraordinary financing needs of the Union Government.

Conditions: Most commonly used before general elections, when a full-year Budget cannot be passed before the outgoing government's term ends (an interim Budget/vote on account).

Exceptions / Limitations: A vote on account is meant only to cover routine, ongoing expenditure for a short period, not to introduce new taxation or major policy changes, per longstanding parliamentary convention.

Practical Relevance: The constitutional basis for "interim Budgets" typically presented in an election year.

Related Articles: Articles 112, 113, 114.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure of Lok Sabha regarding votes on account.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 117 — Special provisions as to financial Bills

Provision Type: Special legislative-procedure provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Legislative Procedure; Finance

Accurate Explanation: A Bill or amendment making provision for any of the matters specified in Article 110(1)(a)-(f) (the Money Bill subject-matters) cannot be introduced or moved except on the recommendation of the President, and such a Bill dealing specifically with taxation cannot be introduced in the Rajya Sabha; however, no Presidential recommendation is required for moving an amendment merely providing for the reduction or abolition of any tax. A Bill that, if enacted, would involve expenditure from the Consolidated Fund of India cannot be passed by either House unless the President has recommended consideration of the Bill to that House.

Simple Explanation: "Financial Bills" that touch on Money Bill-type subjects need the President's recommendation to even be introduced (and tax Bills specifically cannot start in the Rajya Sabha), and any Bill that would involve government spending needs Presidential recommendation before either House can actually pass it, though a proposal only to cut or remove a tax doesn't need this recommendation.

Applies To: "Financial Bills" as distinct from Money Bills — Bills with some, but not exclusively, financial provisions.

Conditions: This governs Bills broader in scope than pure Money Bills (which are covered by Articles 109-110) but that still touch financial subject-matter.

Exceptions / Limitations: Unlike genuine Money Bills, these Financial Bills can originate in and be substantively considered by the Rajya Sabha (except the specific taxation-only category), and are not subject to the Article 109 fast-track procedure.

Practical Relevance: Clarifies the procedural distinction between a "Money Bill" (Article 110, Rajya Sabha largely bypassed) and a broader "Financial Bill" (this Article, Rajya Sabha retains a fuller role).

Related Articles: Articles 109, 110, 112.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha regarding Financial Bills.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Procedure Generally

Article 118 — Rules of procedure

Provision Type: Internal rule-making provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Procedure

Accurate Explanation: Each House of Parliament may make rules for regulating, subject to the Constitution, its own procedure and the conduct of its business. Parliament may also by law regulate the procedure of Parliament with respect to joint sittings of, and communications between, the two Houses. Until such rules are made, President, after consultation with the Speaker and Chairman, may make rules regulating procedure for joint sittings and matters connected therewith. Rules regulating procedure in financial business require the President's recommendation or previous sanction, as the case may be.

Simple Explanation: Each House writes its own detailed rulebook for how it conducts business, subject to the Constitution's overall framework, and there are special rules (with Presidential involvement) governing joint sittings and financial business.

Applies To: The internal procedural framework of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha.

Conditions: Financial-business rules specifically need Presidential recommendation or sanction, reflecting the executive's constitutional role in initiating money matters.

Exceptions / Limitations: These "Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business" are subordinate to, and must be consistent with, the Constitution itself.

Practical Relevance: The source of the detailed day-to-day Rules of Procedure that actually govern how bills are debated, questions are asked, and committees function in Indian Parliament.

Related Articles: Articles 108, 119, 122.

Related Acts / Procedures: Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in Lok Sabha; Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in the Council of States.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 119 — Regulation by law of procedure in Parliament in relation to financial business

Provision Type: Financial-procedure provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Procedure; Finance

Accurate Explanation: Parliament may, for the purpose of the timely completion of financial business, regulate by law the procedure of, and the conduct of business in, each House with respect to any financial matter or to any Bill for the appropriation of money out of the Consolidated Fund of India; where such a law is inconsistent with any rule made under Article 118, or with any Rule of Procedure having effect under Article 118, that law prevails.

Simple Explanation: Parliament can pass a special law to speed up or streamline how financial business (like the Budget) is handled, and if such a law conflicts with the ordinary internal Rules of Procedure, the law takes priority.

Applies To: Financial legislative business, particularly time-sensitive Budget-related procedures.

Conditions: Requires a specific parliamentary law, distinct from the ordinary internally-made Rules of Procedure.

Exceptions / Limitations: This power has been used comparatively rarely, since the Rules of Procedure under Article 118 already govern most financial-business timelines in practice.

Practical Relevance: Provides Parliament with a legislative override mechanism to ensure Budget-related business is not held up by procedural rules.

Related Articles: Articles 112, 118.

Related Acts / Procedures: None widely enacted as a standalone statute; largely operates through the Rules of Procedure framework in practice.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 120 — Language to be used in Parliament

Provision Type: Language provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Official Language

Accurate Explanation: Business in Parliament is transacted in Hindi or in English, provided that the Chairman of the Rajya Sabha or the Speaker of the Lok Sabha (or a person acting in that capacity) may permit a member who cannot adequately express themselves in either language to address the House in their mother tongue. The provisions of Article 348 (regarding the authoritative English text of laws, Bills, and certain other instruments) apply to Parliament as they apply to the corresponding matters for State Legislatures.

Simple Explanation: Parliament's official business is conducted in Hindi or English, but the presiding officer can allow a member who struggles in both to speak in their own mother tongue, and the special rules on the authoritative English legal text still apply.

Applies To: All proceedings and business conducted in Parliament.

Conditions: The mother-tongue permission is a discretionary accommodation granted by the presiding officer, not an automatic right.

Exceptions / Limitations: This governs the language of parliamentary proceedings specifically, distinct from the broader Official Language provisions in Part XVII (Articles 343-351).

Practical Relevance: Reflects India's constitutional multilingual accommodation within the core institutions of national legislative business.

Related Articles: Articles 343, 348, 210 (parallel State provision).

Related Acts / Procedures: Official Languages Act, 1963, and related rules.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 121 — Restriction on discussion in Parliament

Provision Type: Judicial-independence safeguard

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Judiciary; Procedure

Accurate Explanation: No discussion takes place in Parliament with respect to the conduct of any Judge of the Supreme Court or of a High Court in the discharge of their duties, except upon a motion for presenting an address to the President praying for the Judge's removal, in the manner provided under Article 124(4) (and the corresponding provisions for High Court Judges via Article 218).

Simple Explanation: MPs generally cannot debate or criticise a sitting judge's conduct in office on the floor of Parliament — the only exception is a formal motion aimed at actually removing that judge through the constitutional impeachment-like process.

Applies To: Discussion and debate within either House of Parliament regarding named Judges.

Conditions: The exception applies only when a formal removal motion, following the specific procedural requirements of Article 124(4), is actually before the House.

Exceptions / Limitations: This restricts floor discussion in Parliament specifically; it does not prevent judicial conduct from being discussed in other appropriate forums, such as through in-house judicial mechanisms.

Practical Relevance: Protects judicial independence by insulating individual judges from routine political criticism within the legislature.

Related Articles: Articles 124, 218.

Related Acts / Procedures: Judges (Inquiry) Act, 1968 (governing the removal-motion investigation process).

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 122 — Courts not to inquire into proceedings of Parliament

Provision Type: Judicial-restraint / separation-of-powers provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Judiciary; Procedure

Accurate Explanation: The validity of any proceedings in Parliament cannot be called in question in any court on the ground of any alleged irregularity of procedure. No officer or member of Parliament in whom powers are vested for regulating procedure, conduct of business, or maintaining order in Parliament is subject to the jurisdiction of any court in respect of the exercise of those powers.

Simple Explanation: Courts generally cannot strike down a Parliament proceeding just because of a procedural irregularity, and presiding officers exercising their procedural/disciplinary powers within the House are largely shielded from being taken to court over how they exercised those powers.

Applies To: Judicial review of internal parliamentary proceedings and the conduct of presiding officers.

Conditions: The bar specifically covers "irregularity of procedure," which courts have distinguished from substantive violations of the Constitution itself.

Exceptions / Limitations: Courts have clarified that this Article does not bar review of a proceeding that violates a mandatory constitutional provision (as opposed to a mere internal procedural rule) — this distinction is central to the ongoing Money Bill litigation discussed under Article 110.

Practical Relevance: Defines the boundary between legitimate judicial review of parliamentary action and impermissible interference with Parliament's internal autonomy.

Related Articles: Articles 105, 110, 212 (parallel State provision).

Related Acts / Procedures: Relevant Supreme Court decisions interpreting the "procedural irregularity" versus "constitutional illegality" distinction, including in the Money Bill line of cases.

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Supreme Court of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Chapter III — Legislative Powers of the President

Article 123 — Power of President to promulgate Ordinances during recess of Parliament

Provision Type: Emergency legislative power

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; President; Ordinances; Legislative Procedure

Accurate Explanation: If, at any time when both Houses of Parliament are not in session, the President is satisfied that circumstances exist that render it necessary to take immediate action, the President may promulgate such Ordinances as the circumstances appear to require. An Ordinance so promulgated has the same force and effect as an Act of Parliament, but must be laid before both Houses of Parliament when they reassemble, and ceases to operate at the expiration of six weeks from the date of reassembly unless a resolution disapproving it is passed by both Houses before that period expires, or it may cease to operate earlier if both Houses pass resolutions disapproving it. The President may also withdraw an Ordinance at any time. If and so far as an Ordinance makes any provision which Parliament would not, under the Constitution, be competent to enact as an Act, such provision is void.

Simple Explanation: When Parliament is not in session and the President (acting on ministerial advice) is satisfied urgent action is needed, the government can issue an Ordinance that works just like a law immediately — but it is temporary and must be approved by Parliament within six weeks of reassembly or it lapses, and it can never contain anything Parliament itself could not have validly enacted.

Applies To: Situations requiring urgent legislative-type action when Parliament is not sitting.

Conditions: Requires the President's (i.e., the Council of Ministers') satisfaction that immediate action is necessary; exercised on ministerial advice under Article 74.

Exceptions / Limitations: The Supreme Court has held that the President's satisfaction is justiciable in limited circumstances (A.K. Roy v. Union of India), that repeatedly re-promulgating the same Ordinance without placing it before the legislature is a "fraud on the Constitution" and unconstitutional (D.C. Wadhwa v. State of Bihar, 1987), and, in Krishna Kumar Singh v. State of Bihar (2017), that Ordinances are subject to judicial review, that rights and liabilities created under a lapsed Ordinance do not automatically survive merely because the Ordinance briefly had legal force, and that courts may examine the public-interest impact of the Ordinance's ceasing to operate.

Practical Relevance: Frequently used and frequently controversial, since repeated Ordinance-making has at times been used to bypass full parliamentary debate; the constitutional safeguards (six-week limit, prohibition on repeated re-promulgation, judicial review) are central to keeping this executive law-making power within constitutional bounds.

Related Articles: Articles 74, 85, 107, 213 (parallel State provision for Governors).

Related Acts / Procedures: Relevant Supreme Court decisions including A.K. Roy v. Union of India (1982), D.C. Wadhwa v. State of Bihar (1987), and Krishna Kumar Singh v. State of Bihar (2017).

Official Source(s): Legislative Department, Government of India; India Code; Supreme Court of India.

Last Verified: 20 August 2026

Article 124 — Establishment and constitution of Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Constitutional Bodies



Accurate Explanation: There shall be a Supreme Court of India consisting of a Chief Justice of India and, unless Parliament by law prescribes a larger number, not more than seven other Judges. Parliament has increased the sanctioned strength by ordinary law. Every Supreme Court Judge is appointed by the President by warrant under the President’s hand and seal, after consultation with such Judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts as the President considers necessary. For appointment of a Judge other than the Chief Justice, consultation with the Chief Justice of India is mandatory under the constitutional text. A Judge must be an Indian citizen and must have been a High Court Judge for at least five years, a High Court advocate for at least ten years, or, in the President’s opinion, a distinguished jurist.



Simple Explanation: The Supreme Court is India’s highest court. The President appoints its judges. A Supreme Court Judge must be an Indian citizen with substantial judicial, legal-practice, or recognised legal expertise.



Applies To: The President, Supreme Court Judges, High Court Judges, advocates, Parliament, and persons eligible for appointment.



Conditions: A Supreme Court Judge holds office until age 65, unless the Judge resigns or is removed according to the Constitution. A Judge may resign by writing to the President.



Exceptions / Limitations: A Supreme Court Judge can be removed only through the constitutional removal procedure in Article 124(4) and Article 124(5). Appointment practice and consultation have been substantially shaped by Supreme Court judicial decisions; those decisions are interpretation, not the wording of Article 124 itself.



Practical Relevance: Explains how the highest constitutional court is formed, who can become a Judge, and why judicial independence is constitutionally protected.



Historical Note: Articles 124A, 124B and 124C concerning the National Judicial Appointments Commission were inserted by the Constitution (Ninety-ninth Amendment) Act, 2014. The Supreme Court declared the 99th Amendment and the NJAC Act unconstitutional in 2015. They should be displayed as historical/invalidated provisions, not active appointment law.



Related Articles: Articles 50, 125–147, 217, 222, 368.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Judges (Salaries and Conditions of Service) Act, 1958; Judges (Inquiry) Act, 1968.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 124

; Supreme Court judgment on NJAC, 2015.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 124A — National Judicial Appointments Commission

Provision Type: Judiciary; historical constitutional provision

Status: Historical; invalidated by the Supreme Court

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court



Accurate Explanation: Article 124A was inserted by the Constitution (Ninety-ninth Amendment) Act, 2014 to establish the National Judicial Appointments Commission.



Simple Explanation: This Article attempted to create a new body for appointing higher-court judges.



Applies To: Historically, the proposed National Judicial Appointments Commission and higher-judiciary appointments.



Conditions: The Article was part of the 99th Amendment framework.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Supreme Court declared the Constitution (Ninety-ninth Amendment) Act, 2014 unconstitutional in 2015. Article 124A is not an active basis for judicial appointments.



Practical Relevance: LAWOUD should show this only as historical context when explaining the current judicial-appointment system.



Related Articles: Articles 124, 124B, 124C, 217.

Related Acts / Procedures: National Judicial Appointments Commission Act, 2014 — invalidated with the constitutional amendment.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India

; Supreme Court NJAC judgment, 2015.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 124B — Functions of Commission

Provision Type: Judiciary; historical constitutional provision

Status: Historical; invalidated by the Supreme Court

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court



Accurate Explanation: Article 124B proposed functions for the National Judicial Appointments Commission, including recommending persons for appointment as Chief Justice of India, Supreme Court Judges, Chief Justices of High Courts, and High Court Judges, and recommending transfers of High Court Chief Justices and Judges.



Simple Explanation: This Article would have given the proposed NJAC a major role in appointing and transferring senior judges.



Applies To: Historically, the proposed NJAC.



Conditions: It depended on the validity and operation of the Constitution (Ninety-ninth Amendment) Act, 2014.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Supreme Court invalidated the 99th Amendment. Article 124B is not active law.



Practical Relevance: Historical context only; do not use it to describe the present judicial-appointment process.



Related Articles: Articles 124, 124A, 124C, 217 and 222.

Related Acts / Procedures: National Judicial Appointments Commission Act, 2014 — invalidated.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India

; Supreme Court NJAC judgment, 2015.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 124C — Power of Parliament to make law

Provision Type: Judiciary; historical constitutional provision

Status: Historical; invalidated by the Supreme Court

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Parliament



Accurate Explanation: Article 124C proposed to empower Parliament to make law regulating the procedure for appointing the Chief Justice of India, Supreme Court Judges, High Court Chief Justices, and High Court Judges, and to confer functions on the proposed NJAC.



Simple Explanation: It would have allowed Parliament to make laws about the proposed NJAC appointment process.



Applies To: Historically, Parliament and the proposed NJAC framework.



Conditions: It depended on the 99th Amendment.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Supreme Court invalidated the 99th Amendment. Article 124C is not active law.



Practical Relevance: Historical context only.



Related Articles: Articles 124, 124A, 124B, 217 and 222.

Related Acts / Procedures: National Judicial Appointments Commission Act, 2014 — invalidated.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India

; Supreme Court NJAC judgment, 2015.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 125 — Salaries, etc., of Judges

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Finance



Accurate Explanation: Supreme Court Judges receive salaries, allowances, rights relating to leave and pension, and other conditions of service as Parliament may determine by law. Until Parliament so provides, the Second Schedule applies. Their privileges, allowances, rights relating to leave or pension, and other conditions of service cannot be varied to their disadvantage after appointment.



Simple Explanation: Parliament decides Supreme Court Judges’ pay and service conditions, but a Judge’s benefits cannot be reduced unfairly after appointment.



Applies To: Chief Justice of India and other Supreme Court Judges.



Conditions: Actual salary and service conditions are governed by law.



Exceptions / Limitations: This Article concerns judicial service conditions; it does not decide the appointment, removal, or jurisdiction of Judges.



Practical Relevance: Protects judicial independence by preventing the government from reducing a sitting Judge’s service benefits to their disadvantage.



Related Articles: Articles 124, 126–128, 146; Second Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Judges (Salaries and Conditions of Service) Act, 1958.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 125

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 126 — Appointment of acting Chief Justice

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court



Accurate Explanation: When the office of Chief Justice of India is vacant, or when the Chief Justice is unable to perform duties because of absence or otherwise, the President may appoint a Judge of the Supreme Court to perform the duties of Chief Justice.



Simple Explanation: If the Chief Justice of India’s post is vacant or the Chief Justice cannot work temporarily, the President can appoint another Supreme Court Judge to act as Chief Justice.



Applies To: The President and Supreme Court Judges.



Conditions: A vacancy or inability of the Chief Justice of India to perform duties must exist.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Article concerns temporary performance of duties; it does not itself prescribe the ordinary process of permanently appointing a Chief Justice.



Practical Relevance: Ensures that the Supreme Court’s leadership and administrative functioning continue without interruption.



Related Articles: Articles 124, 125, 127 and 146.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court administrative procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 126

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 127 — Appointment of ad hoc Judges

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court



Accurate Explanation: If the Supreme Court lacks a quorum of permanent Judges to hold or continue a sitting, the Chief Justice of India may, with the previous consent of the President and after consultation with the Chief Justice of the concerned High Court, request in writing a qualified High Court Judge to sit and act as an ad hoc Judge of the Supreme Court. The Judge must be qualified for appointment as a Supreme Court Judge and must attend unless unable because of duties or otherwise.



Simple Explanation: If too few permanent Supreme Court Judges are available, an eligible High Court Judge may temporarily sit in the Supreme Court.



Applies To: Chief Justice of India, President, High Court Chief Justices, eligible High Court Judges.



Conditions: The Supreme Court must lack the required quorum of permanent Judges. Presidential consent and High Court consultation are required.



Exceptions / Limitations: An ad hoc Judge serves only for the required sitting or period and does not become a permanent Supreme Court Judge through Article 127.



Practical Relevance: Helps prevent court work from stopping when the Supreme Court does not have enough available Judges.



Related Articles: Articles 124, 126, 128, 145, 217.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules and judicial administrative procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 127

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 128 — Attendance of retired Judges at sittings of the Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court



Accurate Explanation: The Chief Justice of India may, at any time, with the previous consent of the President, request a former Supreme Court Judge, a former Federal Court Judge, or a former High Court Judge qualified for appointment to the Supreme Court, to sit and act as a Judge of the Supreme Court. Such a person is entitled to allowances determined by the President and has the jurisdiction, powers, and privileges of a Supreme Court Judge while so sitting.



Simple Explanation: A qualified retired Judge may be asked to temporarily sit in the Supreme Court when needed.



Applies To: Chief Justice of India, President, retired eligible Judges.



Conditions: Prior consent of the President and consent of the retired Judge are required.



Exceptions / Limitations: The retired Judge does not resume permanent office merely by sitting under Article 128.



Practical Relevance: Allows experienced former Judges to assist the Supreme Court where constitutionally required.



Related Articles: Articles 124, 127, 145 and 146.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules and judicial administration.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 128

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 129 — Supreme Court to be a court of record

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Legal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: The Supreme Court is a court of record and has all powers of such a court, including the power to punish for contempt of itself.



Simple Explanation: The Supreme Court’s official records have special legal authority, and the Court can punish conduct that unlawfully interferes with or undermines the Court’s work.



Applies To: Supreme Court, litigants, advocates, parties, officials, and persons whose conduct may amount to contempt.



Conditions: Contempt jurisdiction is exercised according to constitutional authority, legislation, rules, and judicial procedure.



Exceptions / Limitations: Criticism of a court is not automatically contempt. Whether conduct amounts to contempt depends on law, facts, and judicial determination.



Practical Relevance: Protects the ability of the Supreme Court to function effectively and preserve the authority of judicial proceedings.



Related Articles: Articles 141, 142, 144, 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: Contempt of Courts Act, 1971; Supreme Court Rules.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 129

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 130 — Seat of Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court



Accurate Explanation: The Supreme Court sits in Delhi or in another place or places as the Chief Justice of India may, with the approval of the President, appoint.



Simple Explanation: The Supreme Court is based in Delhi, but the Constitution permits sittings elsewhere if the Chief Justice of India chooses a place with Presidential approval.



Applies To: Chief Justice of India, President, Supreme Court.



Conditions: Presidential approval is required for a place other than Delhi.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Article does not create a citizen’s automatic right to require a Supreme Court bench in a particular city.



Practical Relevance: Explains the constitutional basis for the location of Supreme Court sittings.



Related Articles: Articles 124, 145 and 146.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court administrative arrangements.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 130

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 131 — Original jurisdiction of the Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court jurisdiction

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Union-State Relations



Accurate Explanation: Subject to the Constitution, the Supreme Court has exclusive original jurisdiction in disputes between the Government of India and one or more States; between the Government of India and one or more States on one side and one or more other States on the other; or between two or more States, if the dispute involves a legal right whose existence or extent depends on a question of law or fact.



Simple Explanation: Certain legal disputes between the Union Government and States, or between States, must go directly to the Supreme Court.



Applies To: Union Government and State Governments.



Conditions: The dispute must involve a legal right and fall within one of the constitutionally listed Union-State or State-State categories.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 131 does not cover every political disagreement. It excludes disputes arising from certain pre-Constitution treaties, agreements, covenants, engagements, sanads, or similar instruments that continue to operate or specifically exclude Supreme Court jurisdiction.



Practical Relevance: Protects the constitutional balance between the Union and States by giving the Supreme Court a direct role in specified federal disputes.



Related Articles: Articles 131A, 136, 143, 246, 262, 263, 300.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules; special constitutional or statutory procedures where applicable.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 131

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 131A — Exclusive jurisdiction of the Supreme Court in regard to questions as to constitutional validity of Central laws

Provision Type: Judiciary; historical constitutional provision

Status: Omitted; repealed by the Constitution (Forty-third Amendment) Act, 1977

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Historical Provisions



Accurate Explanation: Article 131A had given the Supreme Court exclusive jurisdiction over questions concerning the constitutional validity of Central laws.



Simple Explanation: This Article once reserved challenges to Central laws for the Supreme Court alone.



Applies To: Historical context only.



Conditions: The Article no longer operates.



Exceptions / Limitations: It was omitted by the Constitution (Forty-third Amendment) Act, 1977. Do not use it as current law.



Practical Relevance: LAWOUD should display this as historical context and not as an active restriction on High Court jurisdiction.



Related Articles: Articles 32, 131, 226, 136 and 139A.

Related Acts / Procedures: None currently under Article 131A.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India

;

Constitution (Forty-third Amendment) Act, 1977

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 132 — Appellate jurisdiction of Supreme Court in appeals from High Courts in certain cases

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court jurisdiction

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Constitutional Remedies



Accurate Explanation: An appeal lies to the Supreme Court from a High Court judgment, decree, or final order in civil, criminal, or other proceedings if the High Court certifies under Article 134A that the case involves a substantial question of law regarding interpretation of the Constitution.



Simple Explanation: If a High Court case raises an important question about what the Constitution means, the case can be appealed to the Supreme Court if the High Court grants the required certificate.



Applies To: Parties to High Court proceedings.



Conditions: A High Court certificate under Article 134A is required for an Article 132 appeal.



Exceptions / Limitations: It is not an automatic appeal in every case involving a constitutional argument. The constitutional question must be substantial and require interpretation.



Practical Relevance: Provides a route to the Supreme Court for important constitutional-interpretation issues decided by High Courts.



Related Articles: Articles 132, 134A, 136, 141 and 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules; High Court certification procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 132

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 133 — Appellate jurisdiction of Supreme Court in appeals from High Courts in regard to civil matters

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court jurisdiction

Status: Active; substituted

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Legal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: An appeal lies to the Supreme Court from a High Court judgment, decree, or final order in a civil proceeding if the High Court certifies under Article 134A that the case involves a substantial question of law of general importance and that, in the High Court’s opinion, the question needs to be decided by the Supreme Court.



Simple Explanation: A civil case may reach the Supreme Court when it raises an important legal question affecting more than just the parties and the Supreme Court should decide it.



Applies To: Parties to civil proceedings decided by a High Court.



Conditions: A certificate under Article 134A is required.



Exceptions / Limitations: An adverse High Court civil decision does not automatically create a right of appeal under Article 133.



Practical Relevance: Allows the Supreme Court to settle important legal questions arising from civil disputes.



Related Articles: Articles 132, 134A, 136, 141 and 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules; High Court certificate procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 133

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 134 — Appellate jurisdiction of Supreme Court in regard to criminal matters

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court jurisdiction

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Criminal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: An appeal lies to the Supreme Court from a High Court judgment, final order, or sentence in a criminal proceeding if the High Court reversed an acquittal and sentenced the accused to death; withdrew a case from a subordinate court, convicted the accused, and sentenced the person to death; or certifies under Article 134A that the case is fit for appeal to the Supreme Court. Parliament may confer further criminal appellate jurisdiction on the Supreme Court.



Simple Explanation: Certain serious criminal cases, including specified death-sentence cases and cases certified by the High Court as fit for appeal, can be appealed to the Supreme Court.



Applies To: Accused persons, prosecution, High Courts, and Supreme Court.



Conditions: The appeal must meet one of the listed constitutional situations, or a statutory expansion made by Parliament.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 134 does not make every criminal High Court order automatically appealable to the Supreme Court.



Practical Relevance: Provides a constitutional appeal route in the most serious criminal matters and in cases the High Court certifies as appropriate.



Related Articles: Articles 132, 134A, 136, 145 and 21.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules; Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023, where relevant to criminal procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 134

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 134A — Certificate for appeal to the Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court jurisdiction

Status: Active; inserted

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Legal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: Every High Court passing a judgment, decree, final order, or sentence referred to in Articles 132(1), 133(1), or 134(1) must determine, either on its own motion or on an oral application made immediately after the decision, whether a certificate described in those Articles may be given. If the High Court considers that such a certificate may be given, it must do so.



Simple Explanation: After certain High Court decisions, a party can immediately ask the High Court for a certificate to appeal to the Supreme Court.



Applies To: High Courts and parties in covered proceedings.



Conditions: The oral application must be made immediately after the High Court passes the relevant judgment, decree, final order, or sentence.



Exceptions / Limitations: The certificate is available only where the constitutional requirements of Articles 132, 133, or 134 are met.



Practical Relevance: Explains an important procedural route for seeking a regular constitutional appeal to the Supreme Court.



Related Articles: Articles 132, 133, 134, 136 and 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: High Court procedure; Supreme Court Rules.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 134A

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 135 — Jurisdiction and powers of the Federal Court under existing law to be exercisable by the Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; transitional constitutional provision

Status: Active in text; transitional in nature

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Transitional Provisions



Accurate Explanation: Until Parliament provides otherwise, the Supreme Court has the jurisdiction and powers that the Federal Court had immediately before the Constitution commenced under existing law, in matters not covered by Articles 133 or 134.



Simple Explanation: This Article transferred certain old Federal Court powers to the Supreme Court when the Constitution began.



Applies To: Supreme Court and matters historically governed by Federal Court jurisdiction.



Conditions: Operates subject to Parliament making another provision.



Exceptions / Limitations: It is mainly transitional and has limited practical use in ordinary modern citizen queries.



Practical Relevance: Explains constitutional continuity between the former Federal Court and the Supreme Court.



Related Articles: Articles 124, 133, 134, 136 and 374.

Related Acts / Procedures: Historical Federal Court-related law, where relevant.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 135

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 136 — Special leave to appeal by the Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court jurisdiction

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Constitutional Remedies; Legal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: The Supreme Court may, in its discretion, grant special leave to appeal from any judgment, decree, determination, sentence, or order in any cause or matter passed or made by any court or tribunal in India. This does not apply to judgments, determinations, sentences, or orders made by a court or tribunal constituted under a law relating to the Armed Forces.



Simple Explanation: In exceptional cases, the Supreme Court may allow an appeal from a decision of almost any Indian court or tribunal. This is called a Special Leave Petition, or SLP.



Applies To: Persons affected by decisions of courts or tribunals in India, subject to the Armed Forces exception.



Conditions: Granting special leave is entirely discretionary. An applicant must follow Supreme Court filing rules and show why the Court should intervene.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 136 is not an automatic final appeal and is not a routine substitute for ordinary appeals or High Court remedies. It excludes Armed Forces court or tribunal matters covered by the constitutional exception.



Practical Relevance: A major route for seeking Supreme Court review after a court or tribunal decision, but it should be used with qualified legal advice due to strict procedure and discretionary jurisdiction.



Related Articles: Articles 132–135, 136, 141, 142 and 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules; Special Leave Petition procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 136

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 137 — Review of judgments or orders by the Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court procedure

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Legal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: Subject to any law made by Parliament and rules made under Article 145, the Supreme Court may review any judgment pronounced or order made by it.



Simple Explanation: The Supreme Court can reconsider its own judgment or order through a review process.



Applies To: Supreme Court and parties seeking review.



Conditions: Review is governed by Supreme Court Rules and is not an ordinary re-hearing of the entire case.



Exceptions / Limitations: A review is limited and does not give every dissatisfied party a right to argue the whole case again.



Practical Relevance: Provides a narrow constitutional basis for asking the Supreme Court to reconsider an error in its own decision.



Related Articles: Articles 136, 141, 142 and 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules; review-petition procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 137

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 138 — Enlargement of the jurisdiction of the Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court jurisdiction

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Parliament



Accurate Explanation: Parliament may by law confer on the Supreme Court further jurisdiction and powers with respect to matters in the Union List. Parliament may also provide for conferring further jurisdiction and powers with respect to other matters if the Government of India and the Government of the concerned State agree.



Simple Explanation: Parliament can give the Supreme Court additional powers through law, especially in Union subjects. For some State-related matters, the Union and the State must agree.



Applies To: Parliament, Union Government, State Governments, Supreme Court.



Conditions: Additional jurisdiction requires a law made by Parliament. For matters outside the Union List, Government of India and the relevant State Government must agree.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 138 does not itself enlarge jurisdiction; Parliament must enact a law.



Practical Relevance: Explains how the Supreme Court’s legal powers can be expanded beyond the jurisdiction expressly listed in the Constitution.



Related Articles: Articles 131–137, 139, 140, 246 and Seventh Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Parliament may enact laws under Article 138.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 138

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 139 — Conferment on the Supreme Court of powers to issue certain writs

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court jurisdiction

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Constitutional Remedies



Accurate Explanation: Parliament may by law confer on the Supreme Court power to issue directions, orders, or writs, including habeas corpus, mandamus, prohibition, quo warranto, and certiorari, for purposes other than enforcement of Fundamental Rights.



Simple Explanation: Parliament can give the Supreme Court power to issue constitutional writs for legal purposes beyond enforcing Fundamental Rights.



Applies To: Parliament and Supreme Court.



Conditions: The power must be conferred by a law made by Parliament.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 139 itself does not grant an unrestricted new writ jurisdiction. Article 32 remains the direct constitutional basis for Supreme Court writs enforcing Fundamental Rights.



Practical Relevance: Helps distinguish Article 32 writs for Fundamental Rights from additional writ powers that Parliament may confer.



Related Articles: Articles 32, 136, 138, 139A, 226 and 246.

Related Acts / Procedures: Any parliamentary law made under Article 139; Supreme Court Rules.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 139

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 139A — Transfer of certain cases

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court jurisdiction

Status: Active; inserted

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Constitutional Remedies



Accurate Explanation: Where cases involving the same or substantially the same questions of law are pending before the Supreme Court and one or more High Courts, or before two or more High Courts, and the Supreme Court is satisfied that the questions are substantial questions of general importance, it may withdraw the High Court case or cases and dispose of them itself. It may also transfer cases, appeals, or other proceedings from one High Court to another if expedient for the ends of justice.



Simple Explanation: If similar important legal issues are pending in different High Courts, the Supreme Court can bring the cases together or move a case to another High Court to ensure fair and consistent decisions.



Applies To: Supreme Court, High Courts, and parties in relevant proceedings.



Conditions: The Supreme Court must be satisfied about substantial common legal questions of general importance, or that transfer is needed for the ends of justice.



Exceptions / Limitations: Transfer is discretionary; parties cannot demand it automatically.



Practical Relevance: Prevents conflicting High Court decisions on the same major legal issue and can help ensure a fair hearing.



Related Articles: Articles 131, 136, 141, 142, 144 and 226.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules; transfer-petition procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 139A

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 140 — Ancillary powers of Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court jurisdiction

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Parliament



Accurate Explanation: Parliament may by law give the Supreme Court supplementary powers necessary or desirable to enable it more effectively to exercise its jurisdiction, including powers relating to contempt, subject to the Constitution.



Simple Explanation: Parliament can give the Supreme Court extra supporting powers needed to help it perform its constitutional work effectively.



Applies To: Parliament and Supreme Court.



Conditions: Additional powers must be conferred by parliamentary law and must be consistent with the Constitution.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 140 does not itself create a particular new power; a law is required.



Practical Relevance: Supports the effective functioning of the Supreme Court when supplementary legal powers are necessary.



Related Articles: Articles 129, 138, 141, 142 and 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: Parliament may enact supporting legislation.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 140

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 141 — Law declared by Supreme Court to be binding on all courts

Provision Type: Judiciary; precedent

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Legal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: The law declared by the Supreme Court is binding on all courts within the territory of India.



Simple Explanation: When the Supreme Court explains what the law means, other Indian courts must follow that legal interpretation.



Applies To: All courts in India.



Conditions: The binding element is the legal principle or rule declared by the Supreme Court in a decision.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 141 does not mean every observation in every judgment has identical binding force. Determining the binding legal rule can require legal analysis.



Practical Relevance: Promotes consistency in the legal system. A Supreme Court interpretation of the Constitution or a statute generally guides High Courts and lower courts.



Related Articles: Articles 129, 136, 137, 142, 144 and 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: Judicial precedent and court procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 141

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 142 — Enforcement of decrees and orders of Supreme Court and orders as to discovery, etc.

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court powers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Constitutional Remedies; Legal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: The Supreme Court may pass any decree or make any order necessary for doing complete justice in any cause or matter pending before it. Such decrees or orders are enforceable throughout India in the manner prescribed by Parliament or, until Parliament provides otherwise, in the manner prescribed by the President through order. Subject to parliamentary law, the Supreme Court has power to make orders for securing attendance of persons, discovery or production of documents, and investigation or punishment of contempt of itself.



Simple Explanation: In a case before it, the Supreme Court can make orders needed to do complete justice, and its orders can be enforced anywhere in India.



Applies To: Supreme Court, parties before it, authorities responsible for enforcing its orders.



Conditions: The power applies to a cause or matter pending before the Supreme Court and remains subject to the Constitution and applicable law.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 142 is not a general route for every person to bypass normal courts or legal procedure. It does not permit an order contrary to the Constitution or binding statutory limits.



Practical Relevance: Important for enforcement of Supreme Court orders, nationwide effect of decrees, and the Court’s ability to issue effective relief in matters properly before it.



Related Articles: Articles 129, 136, 141, 144 and 145.
