Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules; Contempt of Courts Act, 1971; applicable enforcement procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 142

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 143 — Power of President to consult Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; advisory jurisdiction

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; President



Accurate Explanation: If at any time it appears to the President that a question of law or fact has arisen, or is likely to arise, which is of such public importance that it is expedient to obtain the Supreme Court’s opinion, the President may refer the question to the Court. The Supreme Court may report its opinion after such hearing as it thinks fit. The President may also refer disputes of the kind mentioned in the Article 131 proviso; in that situation, the Supreme Court must report its opinion.



Simple Explanation: The President can ask the Supreme Court for its opinion on an important public legal or factual question.



Applies To: President and Supreme Court.



Conditions: The President must make a constitutional reference. The question must be of public importance in the Article 143(1) situation.



Exceptions / Limitations: An Article 143 opinion is advisory jurisdiction, not an ordinary appeal between private parties. The Supreme Court may decline to answer an Article 143(1) reference.



Practical Relevance: Explains how the President may seek the Supreme Court’s constitutional opinion on nationally important issues.



Related Articles: Articles 131, 136, 141, 142 and 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential reference procedure; Supreme Court Rules.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 143

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 144 — Civil and judicial authorities to act in aid of the Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; enforcement provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Government Institutions



Accurate Explanation: All authorities, civil and judicial, in the territory of India must act in aid of the Supreme Court.



Simple Explanation: Government authorities and courts across India must assist the Supreme Court in carrying out its lawful orders and functions.



Applies To: Civil authorities and judicial authorities throughout India.



Conditions: The duty arises in relation to lawful Supreme Court functions, proceedings, and orders.



Exceptions / Limitations: It does not allow authorities to act outside their lawful powers; they must act consistently with the Constitution and applicable law.



Practical Relevance: Supports nationwide implementation of Supreme Court decisions and orders.



Related Articles: Articles 129, 141, 142 and 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: Applicable court-enforcement and administrative procedures.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 144

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 144A — Special provisions as to disposal of questions relating to constitutional validity of laws

Provision Type: Judiciary; historical constitutional provision

Status: Omitted; repealed by the Constitution (Forty-third Amendment) Act, 1977

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Historical Provisions



Accurate Explanation: Article 144A had imposed special requirements for Supreme Court decisions declaring Central or State laws constitutionally invalid.



Simple Explanation: This Article once required special procedures before the Supreme Court could strike down certain laws.



Applies To: Historical context only.



Conditions: The Article no longer operates.



Exceptions / Limitations: It was omitted by the Constitution (Forty-third Amendment) Act, 1977. Do not treat it as active constitutional procedure.



Practical Relevance: Historical context only; it should not be used to describe the present process for constitutional review of legislation.



Related Articles: Articles 13, 32, 131A, 141 and 145.

Related Acts / Procedures: None currently under Article 144A.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India

;

Constitution (Forty-third Amendment) Act, 1977

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 145 — Rules of Court, etc.

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court administration

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Legal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: Subject to parliamentary law, the Supreme Court may make rules regulating its practice and procedure, including rules regarding persons practising before it, hearing appeals, proceedings enforcing Fundamental Rights, transfer of cases, and other matters. The Constitution requires at least five Judges to sit for cases involving a substantial question of constitutional interpretation, Article 143 references, and other matters required by rules. Judgments must be delivered in open court, and no judgment may be delivered except with the concurrence of a majority of Judges present. Judges may deliver dissenting opinions.



Simple Explanation: The Supreme Court makes rules for how cases are filed, heard, and decided. Important constitutional cases are normally heard by benches of at least five Judges.



Applies To: Supreme Court, advocates, litigants, parties, and persons practising before the Court.



Conditions: Rules are subject to parliamentary law. The President’s approval is required for rules made under Article 145(1) concerning court fees.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Constitution sets a minimum bench requirement for specified constitutional matters; it does not require every case to be heard by five Judges.



Practical Relevance: Explains Supreme Court filing, hearing, bench composition, open-court judgments, and procedural rule-making.



Related Articles: Articles 32, 129, 136, 137, 139A, 143 and 146.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court Rules, 2013, as amended; Advocates Act, 1961.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 145

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 146 — Officers and servants and the expenses of the Supreme Court

Provision Type: Judiciary; Supreme Court administration

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court; Finance



Accurate Explanation: Appointments of Supreme Court officers and servants are made by the Chief Justice of India or another Judge or officer directed by the Chief Justice. Service conditions may be regulated by rules made by the Chief Justice or authorised person, subject to parliamentary law. Rules relating to salaries, allowances, leave, or pensions require Presidential approval. Administrative expenses of the Supreme Court, including salaries, allowances, and pensions, are charged on the Consolidated Fund of India.



Simple Explanation: The Chief Justice of India manages Supreme Court staff. The Court’s essential expenses are paid from the Consolidated Fund of India.



Applies To: Chief Justice of India, Supreme Court staff, President, Parliament.



Conditions: Service rules remain subject to parliamentary law, and specified financial rules require Presidential approval.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Article concerns court administration and staff, not appointment or removal of Supreme Court Judges.



Practical Relevance: Supports administrative and financial independence of the Supreme Court.



Related Articles: Articles 125, 126, 145, 112 and 266.

Related Acts / Procedures: Supreme Court staff-service rules; financial and administrative procedures.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 146

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 147 — Interpretation

Provision Type: Judiciary; interpretation provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Supreme Court



Accurate Explanation: For Chapter IV of Part V and Chapter V of Part VI, references to a substantial question of law concerning interpretation of the Constitution include references to a substantial question concerning interpretation of the Government of India Act, 1935, including enactments amending or supplementing it, and Orders in Council or orders made thereunder, or of the Indian Independence Act, 1947, or orders made thereunder.



Simple Explanation: For certain Supreme Court and High Court appeal provisions, important questions about older constitutional laws are treated as constitutional-interpretation questions.



Applies To: Supreme Court, High Courts, and appeals involving the historical constitutional instruments specified in the Article.



Conditions: Applies to the interpretation provisions in the relevant chapters of Parts V and VI.



Exceptions / Limitations: It is mainly historical and interpretive; it does not create a general modern right of appeal.



Practical Relevance: Helps courts handle surviving legal questions connected with pre-Constitution constitutional instruments.



Related Articles: Articles 132, 134A, 145, 214–231 and 372.

Related Acts / Procedures: Historical legal instruments specified in Article 147.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 147

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 148 — Comptroller and Auditor-General of India

Provision Type: Constitutional Body; Finance and Audit

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Constitutional Bodies; Finance; Public Accountability



Accurate Explanation: There shall be a Comptroller and Auditor-General of India, appointed by the President by warrant under the President’s hand and seal. The CAG may be removed only in the same manner and on the same grounds as a Supreme Court Judge. Before entering office, the CAG must make and subscribe an oath or affirmation before the President or a person appointed by the President. The CAG’s salary, allowances, leave, pension, and other service conditions are determined by Parliament, but cannot be varied to the CAG’s disadvantage after appointment. After leaving office, the CAG cannot hold further office under the Government of India or a State Government. Administrative expenses of the CAG’s office are charged on the Consolidated Fund of India.



Simple Explanation: The CAG is an independent constitutional auditor who checks how the Union and State Governments use public money. The government cannot easily remove the CAG or reduce service conditions after appointment.



Applies To: President, CAG, Parliament, Union and State public-finance systems.



Conditions: Removal follows the same constitutional standard and process used for a Supreme Court Judge.



Exceptions / Limitations: The CAG does not replace police investigations, courts, Parliament, State Legislatures, or departmental decision-making. Audit observations are not automatically criminal findings or court judgments.



Practical Relevance: The CAG supports transparency and public accountability by auditing government expenditure, receipts, accounts, and specified public bodies.



Related Articles: Articles 112, 148–151, 266, 279, 280, 293 and 300.

Related Acts / Procedures: Comptroller and Auditor-General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 148

;

CAG constitutional provisions

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 149 — Duties and powers of the Comptroller and Auditor-General

Provision Type: Constitutional Body; Finance and Audit

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Constitutional Bodies; Finance; Public Accountability



Accurate Explanation: The CAG performs duties and exercises powers relating to accounts of the Union, States, and other authorities or bodies as Parliament may prescribe by law. Until Parliament makes such provision, the CAG’s duties and powers are those exercisable immediately before the Constitution commenced in relation to accounts of the Dominion of India and Provinces.



Simple Explanation: Parliament decides through law what government accounts and bodies the CAG can audit and what audit powers the CAG has.



Applies To: CAG, Parliament, Union Government, State Governments, and audited public authorities or bodies.



Conditions: The detailed audit mandate comes from parliamentary law.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 149 does not itself list every audit duty or every audited entity. The scope depends on the law made by Parliament.



Practical Relevance: Explains why detailed CAG audit powers come from the CAG’s governing legislation rather than Article 149 alone.



Related Articles: Articles 148, 150, 151, 266 and 280.

Related Acts / Procedures: Comptroller and Auditor-General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971.



Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 149

;

CAG Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 150 — Form of accounts of the Union and of the States

Provision Type: Finance; Public Accounts

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Finance; Public Accountability; Constitutional Bodies



Accurate Explanation: The accounts of the Union and States must be kept in such form as the President may prescribe on the advice of the CAG.



Simple Explanation: Government accounts must follow a standard format set by the President after taking the CAG’s advice.



Applies To: Union Government, State Governments, President, CAG, and public accounting authorities.



Conditions: The President prescribes the form of accounts; the CAG’s advice is constitutionally required.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 150 does not itself contain the detailed accounting formats or audit procedures.



Practical Relevance: Helps ensure that government financial records are prepared in a consistent form that can be audited and examined.



Related Articles: Articles 148, 149, 151, 266 and 283.

Related Acts / Procedures: Government accounting rules and financial procedures.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 150

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 151 — Audit reports

Provision Type: Finance; Public Accountability

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Constitutional Bodies; Finance; Public Accountability; Parliament; State Government



Accurate Explanation: CAG audit reports relating to Union accounts must be submitted to the President, who must cause them to be laid before each House of Parliament. CAG audit reports relating to State accounts must be submitted to the Governor of the State, who must cause them to be laid before the State Legislature.



Simple Explanation: The CAG’s audit findings on Union finances go to Parliament through the President. Audit findings on State finances go to the State Legislature through the Governor.



Applies To: CAG, President, Parliament, Governors, State Legislatures, Union Government, State Governments.



Conditions: The constitutional process requires submission to the President or Governor and laying before the relevant legislature.



Exceptions / Limitations: A CAG report is an audit report, not by itself a court decision, criminal conviction, or automatic finding of individual wrongdoing. Legislative committees and authorities may examine issues further according to law and procedure.



Practical Relevance: Citizens, journalists, civil-society groups, and legislators can use publicly laid audit reports to understand possible irregularities, waste, weak controls, or financial-management issues in government spending.



Related Articles: Articles 148–150, 112, 202, 266, 280 and 283.

Related Acts / Procedures: Comptroller and Auditor-General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971; parliamentary and State legislative committee procedures.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 151

;

CAG constitutional provisions

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026

Article 152 — Definition

Provision Type: Constitutional Provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government



Accurate Explanation: In Part VI, unless the context otherwise requires, “State” does not include the State of Jammu and Kashmir.



Simple Explanation: This is a special definition used for Part VI of the Constitution. It does not itself describe the present administration of Jammu and Kashmir.



Applies To: Interpretation of Part VI.



Conditions: Applies unless the context requires a different meaning.



Exceptions / Limitations: This is a definition clause, not a power, right, or administrative arrangement.



Practical Relevance: Helps interpret State-government provisions in Part VI.



Related Articles: Articles 1, 152–237, 239A, 239AA, 370.

Related Acts / Procedures: None directly specified.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 152

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 153 — Governors of States

Provision Type: State Executive; Governor

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor



Accurate Explanation: There shall be a Governor for each State. The same person may be appointed as Governor for two or more States.



Simple Explanation: Every State has a Governor. One Governor may serve more than one State.



Applies To: States, President, Governors.



Conditions: The Governor is appointed under Article 155.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Constitution permits a shared Governor; it does not require a different individual for every State.



Practical Relevance: Explains the constitutional office of Governor in each State.



Related Articles: Articles 154–162, 163–167.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential appointment process.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 153

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 154 — Executive power of State

Provision Type: State Executive

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor



Accurate Explanation: The executive power of a State is vested in the Governor and may be exercised directly or through officers subordinate to the Governor, in accordance with the Constitution. This does not transfer functions already conferred by law on another authority.



Simple Explanation: State executive authority formally belongs to the Governor, but State government work is normally carried out through ministers, departments, and authorised officers.



Applies To: Governor, State Government, State officers.



Conditions: Exercise of executive power must follow the Constitution and applicable law.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Governor’s formal executive role does not remove powers legally given to other authorities.



Practical Relevance: Explains why State government orders and executive actions are constitutionally issued in the Governor’s name.



Related Articles: Articles 162, 163, 166, 167.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Rules of Business.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 154

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 155 — Appointment of Governor

Provision Type: State Executive; Governor

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor



Accurate Explanation: The Governor of a State is appointed by the President by warrant under the President’s hand and seal.



Simple Explanation: The President appoints a State Governor.



Applies To: President and Governor.



Conditions: Appointment is made by Presidential warrant.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Constitution does not provide for direct election of Governors by citizens.



Practical Relevance: Clarifies that the Governor is a constitutional appointee, not an elected State office-holder.



Related Articles: Articles 153, 156–159.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential appointment procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 155

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 156 — Term of office of Governor

Provision Type: State Executive; Governor

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor



Accurate Explanation: A Governor holds office during the pleasure of the President. A Governor may resign by writing addressed to the President. Subject to the President’s pleasure, the normal term is five years from entering office, but the Governor continues until a successor enters office.



Simple Explanation: A Governor normally has a five-year term but serves while the President permits and may remain until the next Governor takes charge.



Applies To: Governors and President.



Conditions: Resignation must be addressed to the President.



Exceptions / Limitations: The five-year period is not an absolute guarantee because the Governor holds office during the President’s pleasure.



Practical Relevance: Explains how long a Governor may serve and how a vacancy or transition is handled.



Related Articles: Articles 155, 157–160.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential appointment and resignation procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 156

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 157 — Qualifications for appointment as Governor

Provision Type: State Executive; Governor

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor



Accurate Explanation: A person must be a citizen of India and must have completed 35 years of age to be eligible for appointment as Governor.



Simple Explanation: A Governor must be an Indian citizen who is at least 35 years old.



Applies To: Persons considered for appointment as Governor.



Conditions: Both citizenship and minimum-age requirements must be met.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 157 states minimum constitutional qualifications; it does not prescribe an election process.



Practical Relevance: Explains the basic eligibility criteria for this constitutional office.



Related Articles: Articles 155, 158, 159.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential appointment procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 157

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 158 — Conditions of Governor’s office

Provision Type: State Executive; Governor

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor



Accurate Explanation: A Governor cannot be a member of either House of Parliament or of a State Legislature. If such a member is appointed Governor, the seat is treated as vacant on entering office. A Governor cannot hold another office of profit. The Governor is entitled to official residence, allowances, emoluments, and privileges determined by Parliament; if one person is Governor of multiple States, the President determines allocation. Emoluments and allowances cannot be reduced during the Governor’s term.



Simple Explanation: A Governor cannot simultaneously be an MP, MLA, MLC, or hold another paid government office.



Applies To: Governors and persons appointed as Governors.



Conditions: Service conditions are governed by the Constitution and applicable law.



Exceptions / Limitations: This Article does not regulate ordinary political activity outside the constitutional conditions of office.



Practical Relevance: Helps preserve the independence and full-time nature of the Governor’s office.



Related Articles: Articles 153, 155–159; Second Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Governor’s service-condition provisions and financial rules.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 158

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 159 — Oath or affirmation by the Governor

Provision Type: State Executive; Governor

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor



Accurate Explanation: Before entering office, the Governor must make and subscribe an oath or affirmation before the Chief Justice of the High Court exercising jurisdiction in relation to that State, or, in the Chief Justice’s absence, the senior-most available Judge of that High Court. The oath includes faithfully executing the office and preserving, protecting, and defending the Constitution and law.



Simple Explanation: Before taking office, a Governor must formally promise to protect the Constitution and serve the people of the State.



Applies To: Governor and relevant High Court authority.



Conditions: Oath or affirmation must occur before entering office.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Article sets the constitutional oath; it does not itself regulate all Governor actions after appointment.



Practical Relevance: Confirms that the Governor’s office is constitutionally bound to the Constitution and law.



Related Articles: Articles 155–158, 164, 188.

Related Acts / Procedures: Oath-taking procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 159

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 160 — Discharge of functions of the Governor in certain contingencies

Provision Type: State Executive; Governor

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor



Accurate Explanation: The President may make any provision considered appropriate for discharge of the Governor’s functions in a contingency not provided for in Part VI.



Simple Explanation: If an unusual situation arises and the Constitution has not specifically explained how the Governor’s work should continue, the President can make arrangements.



Applies To: President, Governor, State Government.



Conditions: The contingency must not already be covered by another Part VI provision.



Exceptions / Limitations: This is a contingency provision; it does not give unlimited power to change the constitutional structure of State government.



Practical Relevance: Helps prevent a constitutional gap when the Governor cannot perform functions for an unforeseen reason.



Related Articles: Articles 153, 156, 159, 356.

Related Acts / Procedures: Presidential order in an appropriate contingency.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 160

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 161 — Power of Governor to grant pardons, etc., and to suspend, remit or commute sentences in certain cases

Provision Type: State Executive; Governor; Clemency Power

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor; Criminal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: The Governor may grant pardons, reprieves, respites, or remissions of punishment, or suspend, remit, or commute a sentence, where a person has been convicted of an offence against a law relating to a matter within the State’s executive power.



Simple Explanation: In appropriate State-law criminal cases, the Governor can reduce, suspend, change, or forgive a sentence through constitutional clemency powers.



Applies To: Governor and persons convicted under laws within the State’s executive sphere.



Conditions: The offence must concern a matter to which the State’s executive power extends.



Exceptions / Limitations: Article 161 is distinct from the President’s Article 72 power. It does not replace appeals, bail, review, parole, remission rules, or court procedure. Clemency decisions are fact-specific.



Practical Relevance: Relevant to mercy petitions and sentence-remission issues in eligible State-law matters.



Related Articles: Articles 72, 162, 163, 72 and 161; Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023.

Related Acts / Procedures: State remission and mercy-petition procedures; criminal procedure law.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 161

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 162 — Extent of executive power of State

Provision Type: State Executive

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Union-State Relations



Accurate Explanation: Subject to the Constitution, a State’s executive power extends to matters on which the State Legislature can make laws. In Concurrent List matters, State executive power is subject to the Constitution and any Union law or power expressly conferred on Union authorities.



Simple Explanation: A State government can generally act on subjects on which its Legislature can make laws, but Union law may limit the State’s role in subjects shared by the Union and States.



Applies To: State Government, Governor, Union Government.



Conditions: Must operate subject to the Constitution and applicable parliamentary law.



Exceptions / Limitations: In Concurrent List subjects, State executive power may be restricted where the Constitution or a Union law gives authority to the Union.



Practical Relevance: Explains the constitutional boundary between State executive functions and Union powers.



Related Articles: Articles 73, 154, 246, 254; Seventh Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Union and State laws within their respective legislative fields.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 162

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 163 — Council of Ministers to aid and advise Governor

Provision Type: State Executive; Council of Ministers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor; Chief Minister



Accurate Explanation: There shall be a Council of Ministers with the Chief Minister at its head to aid and advise the Governor, except where the Constitution requires the Governor to act in discretion. If a question arises whether a matter is within the Governor’s discretion, the Governor’s decision is final. Courts cannot inquire into advice tendered by Ministers to the Governor.



Simple Explanation: The Governor normally acts with the advice of the Chief Minister and Council of Ministers. In limited matters where the Constitution gives the Governor discretion, the Governor may act differently.



Applies To: Governor, Chief Minister, State Council of Ministers.



Conditions: Discretion exists only where the Constitution requires the Governor to exercise it.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Governor does not have a general unrestricted personal discretion in all State matters. Courts cannot ask what advice Ministers gave the Governor, but constitutional action can still be examined according to law.



Practical Relevance: Explains the normal relationship between an elected State government and the Governor.



Related Articles: Articles 154, 164, 166, 167, 200, 213, 356.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Rules of Business.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 163

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 164 — Other provisions as to Ministers

Provision Type: State Executive; Council of Ministers

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; State Government; Chief Minister; Governor



Accurate Explanation: The Chief Minister is appointed by the Governor, and other Ministers are appointed by the Governor on the Chief Minister’s advice. Ministers hold office during the Governor’s pleasure, and the Council of Ministers is collectively responsible to the Legislative Assembly. A Minister must take oath before entering office. A person who is not a member of the State Legislature for six consecutive months ceases to be a Minister. Ministerial salaries and allowances are determined by the State Legislature. The total number of Ministers, including the Chief Minister, cannot exceed 15% of the total strength of the Legislative Assembly and cannot be below 12. A member disqualified under the Tenth Schedule is also disqualified from appointment as a Minister for the stated constitutional period. The Article also requires a Tribal Welfare Minister in Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh and Odisha.



Simple Explanation: The Governor appoints the Chief Minister and, on the Chief Minister’s advice, other Ministers. The Ministry must retain the confidence of the elected Legislative Assembly.



Applies To: Governor, Chief Minister, Ministers, Legislative Assembly members.



Conditions: Collective responsibility is to the Legislative Assembly. A non-member Minister must become a State Legislator within six consecutive months.



Exceptions / Limitations: Holding office during the Governor’s pleasure operates within the parliamentary system and collective responsibility framework. The 15% ceiling is constitutionally binding.



Practical Relevance: Explains how State governments are formed, why a Ministry may have to resign after losing Assembly support, and why ministerial numbers are limited.



Related Articles: Articles 163, 164(1A), 164(1B), 164(2)–(5), 173, 191, Tenth Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: State ministerial oath and salary law; anti-defection procedure under the Tenth Schedule.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 164

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 165 — Advocate-General for the State

Provision Type: State Executive; Constitutional Office

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Constitutional Offices; Legal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: The Governor appoints an Advocate-General for the State. The person must be qualified to be appointed a High Court Judge. The Advocate-General holds office during the Governor’s pleasure and receives remuneration determined by the Governor.



Simple Explanation: Every State has an Advocate-General, its highest constitutional legal adviser.



Applies To: Governor, Advocate-General, State Government.



Conditions: The Advocate-General must have the qualifications required for High Court Judgeship.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Advocate-General is not a Judge and does not independently make State policy.



Practical Relevance: The Advocate-General advises the State Government and represents it in important legal and constitutional matters.



Related Articles: Articles 177, 217, 165–167.

Related Acts / Procedures: State legal-department procedures.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 165

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 166 — Conduct of business of the Government of a State

Provision Type: State Executive; Government Administration

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Government Administration



Accurate Explanation: All executive action of a State Government must be expressed to be taken in the Governor’s name. Orders and instruments made in the Governor’s name must be authenticated according to rules made by the Governor. The Governor must make rules for more convenient transaction of State business and allocation among Ministers.



Simple Explanation: State government orders are formally issued in the Governor’s name, but government work is divided among Ministers and departments through Rules of Business.



Applies To: Governor, State Council of Ministers, State departments and officers.



Conditions: Authentication must follow rules made under this Article.



Exceptions / Limitations: Formal use of the Governor’s name does not mean the Governor personally makes every department decision.



Practical Relevance: Helps citizens identify valid State executive orders and understand how department responsibilities are allocated.



Related Articles: Articles 154, 163, 164, 167.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Rules of Business; State authentication rules.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 166

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 167 — Duties of Chief Minister as respects the furnishing of information to Governor, etc.

Provision Type: State Executive; Chief Minister

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Chief Minister; Governor



Accurate Explanation: The Chief Minister must communicate to the Governor all decisions of the Council of Ministers relating to State administration and legislative proposals; provide information the Governor calls for concerning State administration and legislative proposals; and, if the Governor requires, submit for consideration of the Council a matter decided by a Minister but not considered by the Council.



Simple Explanation: The Chief Minister must keep the Governor informed about important government decisions and proposed laws.



Applies To: Chief Minister, Governor, Council of Ministers.



Conditions: The Governor may require information or require a Minister’s decision to be considered by the full Council.



Exceptions / Limitations: This does not make the Governor the ordinary day-to-day decision-maker of the elected government.



Practical Relevance: Supports constitutional communication between the Governor and the elected State Ministry.



Related Articles: Articles 163, 164, 166.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Rules of Business.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 167

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 168 — Constitution of Legislatures in States

Provision Type: State Legislature

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature



Accurate Explanation: Every State has a Legislature consisting of the Governor and either one House or two Houses. In bicameral States, the Houses are the Legislative Assembly and Legislative Council. In unicameral States, the Legislature consists of the Governor and Legislative Assembly.



Simple Explanation: Every State has an elected Legislative Assembly. Some States also have a Legislative Council.



Applies To: States, Governor, Legislative Assembly, Legislative Council where applicable.



Conditions: Whether a State has a Legislative Council depends on the constitutional process in Article 169.



Exceptions / Limitations: A Legislative Council is not compulsory for every State.



Practical Relevance: Explains the structure of State law-making bodies.



Related Articles: Articles 169–177.

Related Acts / Procedures: State legislative rules and election law.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 168

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 169 — Abolition or creation of Legislative Councils in States

Provision Type: State Legislature

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Legislative Council; Parliament



Accurate Explanation: Parliament may by law create or abolish a Legislative Council in a State if the Legislative Assembly passes a resolution to that effect by a majority of the total membership and by not less than two-thirds of members present and voting. Such a law is not treated as a constitutional amendment for Article 368 purposes.



Simple Explanation: A State Assembly can ask Parliament to create or remove its Legislative Council, but Parliament must pass a law to make it happen.



Applies To: State Legislative Assembly, Parliament, States with or seeking a Legislative Council.



Conditions: The Assembly resolution requires the special majority stated in the Article.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Assembly cannot itself create or abolish a Council; Parliament must enact the law.



Practical Relevance: Explains how States can move between one-House and two-House legislatures.



Related Articles: Articles 168, 171, 368.

Related Acts / Procedures: Parliamentary legislation under Article 169.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 169

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 170 — Composition of the Legislative Assemblies

Provision Type: State Legislature; Elections

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; State Government; Legislative Assembly; Elections



Accurate Explanation: A Legislative Assembly consists of members chosen by direct election from territorial constituencies. Subject to constitutional exceptions, an Assembly has not more than 500 and not fewer than 60 members. Constituencies should, as far as practicable, have equal population-to-seat ratios across the State. Readjustment following census is governed by the Article’s constitutional delimitation framework.



Simple Explanation: Citizens elect MLAs directly from local constituencies. Most State Assemblies must have between 60 and 500 elected members.



Applies To: State Legislative Assemblies, voters, Election Commission, delimitation authorities.



Conditions: Constituencies are territorial; their population representation must be kept as equal as practicable.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Constitution contains special population and delimitation provisions. Exact constituency boundaries and seats are decided through the constitutionally authorised delimitation process, not by Article 170 alone.



Practical Relevance: Explains why citizens vote for MLAs from Assembly constituencies and how seats are distributed.



Related Articles: Articles 171, 172, 324, 327, 332, 334A; Delimitation provisions.

Related Acts / Procedures: Delimitation Act; Representation of the People Acts, 1950 and 1951.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 170

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 171 — Composition of the Legislative Councils

Provision Type: State Legislature; Elections

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Legislative Council; Elections



Accurate Explanation: A Legislative Council cannot have more than one-third of the total Assembly membership and cannot have fewer than 40 members. As nearly as practicable, one-third are elected by local authorities, one-twelfth by graduates, one-twelfth by teachers, one-third by Assembly members from persons who are not Assembly members, and the remainder are nominated by the Governor for special knowledge or practical experience in literature, science, art, the co-operative movement, or social service.



Simple Explanation: Legislative Councils are partly elected by special groups and partly nominated by the Governor. They are not directly elected in the same way as Assemblies.



Applies To: States with Legislative Councils, Governor, electors in specified constituencies.



Conditions: Council composition must follow the stated categories as nearly as practicable.



Exceptions / Limitations: A Council cannot permanently block an ordinary Bill passed by the Assembly; Article 197 limits its role in ordinary-Bill procedure.



Practical Relevance: Explains the composition of an MLC House in bicameral States.



Related Articles: Articles 168, 169, 172, 173, 197.

Related Acts / Procedures: Representation of the People Act, 1950; election rules.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 171

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 172 — Duration of State Legislatures

Provision Type: State Legislature

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Emergency



Accurate Explanation: Every Legislative Assembly continues for five years from its first meeting unless sooner dissolved. During a National Emergency, Parliament may extend its term by law for up to one year at a time, but not beyond six months after the Emergency has ceased. A Legislative Council is permanent and is not subject to dissolution; as nearly as possible, one-third of its members retire every second year.



Simple Explanation: An Assembly normally lasts five years unless dissolved earlier. A Legislative Council does not end all at once; members retire in stages.



Applies To: State Assemblies, Legislative Councils, Parliament.



Conditions: Extension of an Assembly during National Emergency requires a law made by Parliament.



Exceptions / Limitations: Emergency extension is temporary and limited by the Constitution.



Practical Relevance: Explains State election cycles and why Council membership changes gradually.



Related Articles: Articles 83, 174, 352, 356.

Related Acts / Procedures: Election procedure and emergency-related parliamentary law.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 172

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 173 — Qualification for membership of the State Legislature

Provision Type: State Legislature; Elections

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Elections; Legislative Assembly; Legislative Council



Accurate Explanation: A person must be an Indian citizen, make and subscribe the prescribed oath or affirmation before a person authorised by the Election Commission, and meet the minimum age requirement: 25 years for the Legislative Assembly and 30 years for the Legislative Council. Parliament may prescribe additional qualifications by law.



Simple Explanation: To contest for MLA, a person must be an Indian citizen and at least 25. To contest for MLC, the person must be at least 30.



Applies To: Candidates for State Legislature elections.



Conditions: The prescribed oath or affirmation must be made; statutory qualifications may also apply.



Exceptions / Limitations: Meeting Article 173 does not remove disqualifications under Article 191 or election law.



Practical Relevance: Provides basic constitutional eligibility for contesting State elections.



Related Articles: Articles 84, 188, 191, 192, 324, 327.

Related Acts / Procedures: Representation of the People Act, 1951.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 173

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 174 — Sessions of the State Legislature, prorogation and dissolution

Provision Type: State Legislature

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor; State Legislature



Accurate Explanation: The Governor summons each House of the State Legislature to meet at a time and place considered appropriate, but no more than six months may intervene between the last sitting in one session and the first sitting in the next. The Governor may prorogue either House and may dissolve the Legislative Assembly.



Simple Explanation: The Governor formally calls State Legislature sessions. An Assembly cannot go more than six months without meeting.



Applies To: Governor, State Legislature.



Conditions: The six-month maximum gap applies between sessions.



Exceptions / Limitations: Only the Assembly can be dissolved; a Legislative Council is permanent.



Practical Relevance: Ensures that elected State Legislatures meet regularly and can conduct government business.



Related Articles: Articles 172, 175, 176, 200, 213.

Related Acts / Procedures: State legislative procedure and Rules of Business.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 174

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 175 — Right of Governor to address and send messages to the House or Houses

Provision Type: State Legislature; Governor

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor; State Legislature



Accurate Explanation: The Governor may address the Legislative Assembly, or both Houses assembled together in a bicameral State, and may require members’ attendance. The Governor may also send messages to either House regarding a pending Bill or otherwise, and the House must consider the matter with all convenient dispatch.



Simple Explanation: The Governor can speak to the State Legislature and send it messages that the House must consider promptly.



Applies To: Governor, Legislative Assembly, Legislative Council.



Conditions: A House receiving a Governor’s message must consider the matter without unnecessary delay.



Exceptions / Limitations: This is not the same as the special constitutional address required under Article 176.



Practical Relevance: Enables formal communication between the Governor and State Legislature.



Related Articles: Articles 174, 176, 200.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 175

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 176 — Special address by the Governor

Provision Type: State Legislature; Governor

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor; State Legislature



Accurate Explanation: At the first session after every general election to the Legislative Assembly and at the first session of each year, the Governor must address the Legislative Assembly, or both Houses assembled together in a bicameral State, and inform the Legislature of the reasons for its summons. Rules must provide time for discussion of matters referred to in the address.



Simple Explanation: After an Assembly election and at the first session each year, the Governor gives an address explaining the State government’s agenda.



Applies To: Governor and State Legislature.



Conditions: Occurs after each general Assembly election and at the first session every year.



Exceptions / Limitations: The address is part of constitutional legislative procedure; it does not itself enact policy or law.



Practical Relevance: Citizens can use the address to understand the government’s stated priorities and legislative plans.



Related Articles: Articles 174, 175, 208.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 176

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 177 — Rights of Ministers and Advocate-General as respects the Houses

Provision Type: State Legislature; Government Administration

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Advocate-General



Accurate Explanation: Every Minister and the Advocate-General for a State may speak in, and otherwise take part in, proceedings of the Legislative Assembly and, where there is one, the Legislative Council and any committee of the Legislature of which they may be named a member. They have no right to vote unless they are members of the House.



Simple Explanation: Ministers and the Advocate-General may participate in legislative discussions, but cannot vote unless they are members of that House.



Applies To: State Ministers, Advocate-General, State Legislature and committees.



Conditions: Voting requires membership of the relevant House.



Exceptions / Limitations: Participation does not automatically give a non-member Minister or Advocate-General voting rights.



Practical Relevance: Lets State legal and executive representatives explain government Bills and policy in the Legislature.



Related Articles: Articles 164, 165, 188, 189.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 177

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 178 — The Speaker and Deputy Speaker of the Legislative Assembly

Provision Type: State Legislature; Legislative Officers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Legislative Assembly



Accurate Explanation: Every Legislative Assembly must choose two members to be Speaker and Deputy Speaker, as soon as may be after the Assembly first meets. The Assembly must fill either office when vacant.



Simple Explanation: MLAs elect a Speaker and Deputy Speaker to conduct Assembly proceedings.



Applies To: Legislative Assembly and its members.



Conditions: The office-holders must be Assembly members.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Article establishes the offices; removal, resignation, and temporary performance are governed by later Articles.



Practical Relevance: The Speaker plays a central role in managing Assembly debates, voting, and procedural decisions.



Related Articles: Articles 179–181, 189, 191, Tenth Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Assembly Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 178

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 179 — Vacation and resignation of, and removal from, the offices of Speaker and Deputy Speaker

Provision Type: State Legislature; Legislative Officers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Legislative Assembly



Accurate Explanation: The Speaker or Deputy Speaker vacates office on ceasing to be an Assembly member. The Speaker may resign to the Deputy Speaker; the Deputy Speaker may resign to the Speaker. Either may be removed by an Assembly resolution passed by a majority of all then members, provided at least 14 days’ notice is given. The Speaker continues after Assembly dissolution until immediately before the first meeting of the new Assembly.



Simple Explanation: The Assembly can remove its Speaker or Deputy Speaker through a properly notified majority resolution.



Applies To: Speaker, Deputy Speaker, Assembly members.



Conditions: Removal requires a majority of all then Assembly members and 14 days’ notice.



Exceptions / Limitations: A simple majority of members present and voting is not enough for removal.



Practical Relevance: Provides a constitutional accountability process for Assembly presiding officers.



Related Articles: Articles 178, 180, 181, 189.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Assembly Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 179

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 180 — Power of Deputy Speaker or other person to perform the duties of the office of, or to act as, Speaker

Provision Type: State Legislature; Legislative Officers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Legislative Assembly



Accurate Explanation: When the Speaker’s office is vacant, the Deputy Speaker performs the Speaker’s duties. If both offices are vacant, a member appointed by the Governor performs the duties. During absence from a sitting, the Deputy Speaker, or another person determined by Assembly rules, or otherwise determined by the Assembly, acts as Speaker.



Simple Explanation: If the Speaker is unavailable, the Deputy Speaker or another authorised member presides over the Assembly.



Applies To: Speaker, Deputy Speaker, Governor, Legislative Assembly.



Conditions: The applicable arrangement depends on whether the office is vacant or the Speaker is merely absent.



Exceptions / Limitations: An acting presiding officer performs the role temporarily and does not automatically become Speaker.



Practical Relevance: Prevents Assembly proceedings from stopping when the Speaker is unavailable.



Related Articles: Articles 178, 179, 181, 208.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Assembly Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 180

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 181 — The Speaker or Deputy Speaker not to preside while a resolution for removal from office is under consideration

Provision Type: State Legislature; Legislative Officers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Legislative Assembly



Accurate Explanation: When an Assembly resolution to remove the Speaker is under consideration, the Speaker cannot preside; Article 180 rules apply. The Speaker may speak and otherwise participate and may vote in the first instance, but has no casting vote. The same applies to the Deputy Speaker when a removal resolution against the Deputy Speaker is considered.



Simple Explanation: A Speaker facing a removal motion cannot chair that debate but can defend themselves and vote normally.



Applies To: Speaker, Deputy Speaker, Legislative Assembly.



Conditions: A resolution for removal must be under consideration.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Speaker has no casting vote in this situation.



Practical Relevance: Promotes fairness when the Assembly considers removal of its presiding officer.



Related Articles: Articles 179, 180, 189.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Assembly Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 181

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 182 — The Chairman and Deputy Chairman of the Legislative Council

Provision Type: State Legislature; Legislative Officers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Legislative Council



Accurate Explanation: Every Legislative Council must choose two members as Chairman and Deputy Chairman, as soon as may be after the Council first meets, and must fill vacancies in those offices.



Simple Explanation: MLCs choose a Chairman and Deputy Chairman to conduct Council proceedings.



Applies To: Legislative Councils and their members.



Conditions: Applies only in States having a Legislative Council.



Exceptions / Limitations: Does not apply in unicameral States without a Council.



Practical Relevance: Establishes the presiding officers of the Legislative Council.



Related Articles: Articles 168, 171, 183–185.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Council Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 182

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 183 — Vacation and resignation of, and removal from, the offices of Chairman and Deputy Chairman

Provision Type: State Legislature; Legislative Officers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Legislative Council



Accurate Explanation: The Chairman or Deputy Chairman vacates office on ceasing to be a Council member. The Chairman may resign to the Deputy Chairman; the Deputy Chairman may resign to the Chairman. Either may be removed by a Council resolution passed by a majority of all then Council members, with at least 14 days’ notice.



Simple Explanation: A Legislative Council can remove its Chairman or Deputy Chairman through a properly notified majority resolution.



Applies To: Chairman, Deputy Chairman, Legislative Council members.



Conditions: Removal requires majority of all then members and at least 14 days’ notice.



Exceptions / Limitations: A Council is permanent, unlike an Assembly; this Article does not involve Assembly dissolution rules.



Practical Relevance: Provides accountability of Council presiding officers.



Related Articles: Articles 182, 184, 185, 189.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Council Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 183

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 184 — Power of Deputy Chairman or other person to perform the duties of the office of, or to act as, Chairman

Provision Type: State Legislature; Legislative Officers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Legislative Council



Accurate Explanation: When the Chairman’s office is vacant, the Deputy Chairman performs the Chairman’s duties. If both offices are vacant, a Council member appointed by the Governor performs the duties. During absence from a sitting, the Deputy Chairman or another person determined by Council rules, or otherwise determined by the Council, acts as Chairman.



Simple Explanation: If the Council Chairman is unavailable, the Deputy Chairman or another authorised member can preside.



Applies To: Chairman, Deputy Chairman, Governor, Legislative Council.



Conditions: Depends on vacancy or temporary absence.



Exceptions / Limitations: Applies only where a Legislative Council exists.



Practical Relevance: Keeps Council proceedings functioning when the Chairman cannot preside.



Related Articles: Articles 182, 183, 185, 208.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Council Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 184

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 185 — The Chairman or Deputy Chairman not to preside while a resolution for removal from office is under consideration

Provision Type: State Legislature; Legislative Officers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Legislative Council



Accurate Explanation: When a Council resolution for removal of the Chairman is under consideration, the Chairman cannot preside. The Chairman may speak and otherwise participate, and may vote in the first instance but not exercise a casting vote. The same applies to the Deputy Chairman when a removal resolution against that office is under consideration.



Simple Explanation: A Council presiding officer facing removal cannot chair that debate but can participate and vote normally.



Applies To: Chairman, Deputy Chairman, Legislative Council.



Conditions: A removal resolution must be under consideration.



Exceptions / Limitations: No casting vote is available in such a proceeding.



Practical Relevance: Helps ensure procedural fairness in removal proceedings.



Related Articles: Articles 183, 184, 189.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Council Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 185

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 186 — Salaries and allowances of the Speaker and Deputy Speaker and the Chairman and Deputy Chairman

Provision Type: State Legislature; Financial Provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Finance



Accurate Explanation: The Speaker and Deputy Speaker of the Assembly, and the Chairman and Deputy Chairman of the Council, receive salaries and allowances determined by the State Legislature by law, or until such law is made, as specified in the Second Schedule.



Simple Explanation: State law decides the salary and allowances of Assembly and Council presiding officers.



Applies To: Speaker, Deputy Speaker, Chairman, Deputy Chairman.



Conditions: Subject to State law.



Exceptions / Limitations: Applies only to legislative presiding officers, not all MLAs or MLCs.



Practical Relevance: Establishes the constitutional basis for payment of these constitutional office-holders.



Related Articles: Articles 178, 182, 195; Second Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Relevant State salary and allowance law.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 186

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 187 — Secretariat of State Legislature

Provision Type: State Legislature; Government Administration

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Public Services



Accurate Explanation: Each House of a State Legislature has a separate secretarial staff. The State Legislature may regulate staff recruitment and service conditions by law. Until then, the Governor may make rules after consulting the Speaker or Chairman, as applicable.



Simple Explanation: State Legislatures have their own staff to help them function independently from ordinary government departments.



Applies To: State Legislature, Governor, Speaker, Chairman, legislative staff.



Conditions: Rules made by the Governor require consultation with the relevant presiding officer.



Exceptions / Limitations: Legislative staffing remains subject to State law.



Practical Relevance: Supports independent functioning of the Assembly and Council.



Related Articles: Articles 146, 187, 309.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislature Secretariat service rules.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 187

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 188 — Oath or affirmation by members

Provision Type: State Legislature; Membership

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Elections



Accurate Explanation: Every member of a State Legislative Assembly or Council must, before taking their seat, make and subscribe an oath or affirmation before the Governor or a person appointed by the Governor.



Simple Explanation: An elected or chosen MLA or MLC must take the constitutional oath before participating as a member.



Applies To: Members of State Assemblies and Legislative Councils.



Conditions: Oath or affirmation is required before taking a seat.



Exceptions / Limitations: A member who sits or votes before taking oath may incur the constitutional penalty under Article 193.



Practical Relevance: Confirms formal acceptance of constitutional duties by legislators.



Related Articles: Articles 84, 99, 173, 193.

Related Acts / Procedures: Prescribed forms of oath or affirmation; State Legislature procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 188

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 189 — Voting in Houses, power of Houses to act notwithstanding vacancies and quorum

Provision Type: State Legislature; Legislative Procedure

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Legislative Procedure



Accurate Explanation: Questions in a House are decided by majority of members present and voting, excluding the presiding officer’s ordinary vote. The presiding officer has a casting vote in the event of equality. A House may act despite vacancies, and proceedings remain valid despite later discovery that an ineligible person participated. Quorum is ten members or one-tenth of total House membership, whichever is greater.



Simple Explanation: Most decisions are made by a majority of members who are present and vote. A minimum number of members must be present for the House to conduct business.



Applies To: Legislative Assembly, Legislative Council, presiding officers and members.



Conditions: Quorum must be met; if not, the presiding officer must adjourn or suspend the meeting.



Exceptions / Limitations: The casting-vote rule does not apply where Articles 181 or 185 remove that power during removal proceedings.



Practical Relevance: Explains how State Legislatures vote and why attendance matters.



Related Articles: Articles 181, 185, 208.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 189

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 190 — Vacation of seats

Provision Type: State Legislature; Membership

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Elections



Accurate Explanation: A person cannot be a member of both Houses of a State Legislature, and Parliament may make law to prevent simultaneous membership in Parliament and a State Legislature. A member may resign in writing to the Speaker or Chairman; the resignation is not accepted if the presiding officer finds it not voluntary or genuine. A member’s seat may be declared vacant if absent from all meetings for 60 days without House permission.



Simple Explanation: A person cannot keep multiple incompatible legislative seats. An MLA or MLC may resign, but the presiding officer must ensure the resignation is genuine.



Applies To: MPs, MLAs, MLCs, Speaker, Chairman, State Legislature.



Conditions: The 60-day absence calculation excludes periods when the House is prorogued or adjourned for more than four consecutive days.



Exceptions / Limitations: The House may give permission for absence; genuine voluntary resignation is required.



Practical Relevance: Explains legislative-seat vacancies, resignation scrutiny, and prolonged absence rules.



Related Articles: Articles 101, 190, 191, 192.

Related Acts / Procedures: Representation of the People Act, 1951; State Legislative Rules.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 190

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 191 — Disqualifications for membership

Provision Type: State Legislature; Elections

Status: Active; amended

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Elections



Accurate Explanation: A person is disqualified from State Legislature membership if they hold an office of profit under Union or State Government, unless exempted by State law; are declared by a competent court to be of unsound mind; are an undischarged insolvent; are not an Indian citizen, have voluntarily acquired foreign citizenship, or acknowledge allegiance to a foreign State; are disqualified by parliamentary law; or are disqualified under the Tenth Schedule.



Simple Explanation: A person can lose or be barred from a State legislative seat for conflicts of interest, insolvency, foreign allegiance, specified legal disqualifications, or defection.



Applies To: Candidates and members of State Legislatures.



Conditions: Specific statutory disqualifications are prescribed by Parliament; party-defection matters are governed by the Tenth Schedule.



Exceptions / Limitations: Not every paid role is automatically an office of profit; applicable law and facts matter. State law may exempt specified offices.



Practical Relevance: Important for election eligibility, disqualification petitions, and anti-defection matters.



Related Articles: Articles 102, 173, 190, 192; Tenth Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Representation of the People Act, 1951; Tenth Schedule procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 191

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 192 — Decision on questions as to disqualifications of members

Provision Type: State Legislature; Elections

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; Governor; Elections



Accurate Explanation: If a question arises whether a State Legislature member has become disqualified under Article 191(1), it is referred to the Governor, whose decision is final. Before deciding, the Governor must obtain the Election Commission’s opinion and act according to that opinion.



Simple Explanation: If there is a question about whether an MLA or MLC has become disqualified, the Governor decides after obtaining and following the Election Commission’s opinion.



Applies To: Governor, Election Commission, State Legislature members.



Conditions: The question must concern disqualification under Article 191(1).



Exceptions / Limitations: Defection disqualification under the Tenth Schedule follows the specific mechanism in that Schedule, generally involving the Speaker or Chairman.



Practical Relevance: Explains the constitutional procedure for many membership-disqualification disputes.



Related Articles: Articles 191, 192, 324; Tenth Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Election Commission procedure; Representation of the People Act, 1951.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 192

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 193 — Penalty for sitting and voting before making oath or affirmation under Article 188 or when not qualified or when disqualified

Provision Type: State Legislature; Membership

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Elections



Accurate Explanation: A person who sits or votes as a member before taking the oath or affirmation under Article 188, or knowing that they are not qualified, are disqualified, or are prohibited by law from sitting or voting, is liable to a penalty of ₹500 for each day of such sitting or voting, recoverable as a debt due to the State.



Simple Explanation: A person who knowingly sits or votes unlawfully in a State Legislature can be required to pay ₹500 for every such day.



Applies To: Persons unlawfully sitting or voting in a State Legislature.



Conditions: The person must sit or vote before oath, while knowingly unqualified/disqualified, or when legally prohibited.



Exceptions / Limitations: This penalty does not replace other consequences that may arise under election or criminal law.



Practical Relevance: Supports integrity of State legislative membership and voting.



Related Articles: Articles 173, 188, 191, 192.

Related Acts / Procedures: Recovery procedure under State law.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 193

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 194 — Powers, privileges and immunities of State Legislatures and their members and committees

Provision Type: State Legislature; Legislative Privileges

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Legal Procedure



Accurate Explanation: Subject to the Constitution and State legislative rules, freedom of speech in the State Legislature is protected. No member is liable in a court for anything said or any vote given in the Legislature or its committees. No person is liable for publication of a report, paper, vote, or proceeding under House authority. Other powers, privileges, and immunities remain as defined by law; until defined, they continue as existing immediately before the Forty-fourth Amendment’s commencement.



Simple Explanation: MLAs and MLCs can speak and vote freely inside the Legislature, subject to its rules, without being sued in court for that speech or vote.



Applies To: State Legislatures, members, committees, authorised publishers.



Conditions: Freedom of speech inside the House remains subject to the Constitution and House rules.



Exceptions / Limitations: This protection applies to legislative proceedings; it does not give immunity for all acts outside the Legislature.



Practical Relevance: Protects legislative debate and enables members to raise public issues without ordinary civil or criminal liability for their House speech.



Related Articles: Articles 105, 118, 208, 212.

Related Acts / Procedures: State Legislative Rules of Procedure.

Official Sources:

Constitution of India — Article 194

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Article 195 — Salaries and allowances of members

Provision Type: State Legislature; Financial Provision

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; State Government; State Legislature; Finance



Accurate Explanation: Members of a State Legislative Assembly or Legislative Council receive salaries and allowances as determined from time to time by the State Legislature by law, or until then, as specified in the Second Schedule.



Simple Explanation: State law decides the salary and allowances of MLAs and MLCs.



Applies To: MLAs and MLCs.



Conditions: Determined by State legislation.



Exceptions / Limitations: The Article does not itself state the current amount payable.



Practical Relevance: Provides the constitutional basis for State legislator remuneration.



Related Articles: Articles 186, 195; Second Schedule.

Related Acts / Procedures: Relevant State salary and allowance law.

Article 214 — High Courts for States

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: There shall be a High Court for each State.



Simple: Every State has a High Court, though Article 231 permits a common High Court for two or more States or for States and Union Territories.



Related: Articles 214–231; Article 231.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 215 — High Courts to be courts of record

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: Every High Court is a court of record and has all powers of such a court, including power to punish for contempt of itself.



Simple: High Court records have special legal authority, and a High Court can punish conduct that unlawfully interferes with its work.



Related: Articles 129, 215, 225, 226.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 216 — Constitution of High Courts

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: Every High Court consists of a Chief Justice and such other Judges as the President may consider necessary.



Simple: Each High Court has a Chief Justice and other Judges. The President determines how many Judges are needed.



Related: Articles 217, 222, 224, 231.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 217 — Appointment and conditions of the office of a Judge of a High Court

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: High Court Judges are appointed by the President after consultation with the Chief Justice of India, the Governor of the State, and, for a Judge other than the Chief Justice, the Chief Justice of that High Court. A Judge must be an Indian citizen and have held judicial office in India for at least ten years or been an advocate of a High Court for at least ten years. A Judge normally retires at age 62.



Simple: The President appoints High Court Judges after required constitutional consultations. A Judge must be an experienced Indian judicial officer or advocate and normally retires at 62.



Related: Articles 124, 217, 219, 222, 224; Judges (Inquiry) Act, 1968.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 218 — Application of certain provisions relating to Supreme Court to High Courts

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: Article 124(4) and Article 124(5), concerning removal of Supreme Court Judges, apply to High Court Judges.



Simple: A High Court Judge can be removed only through the same strict constitutional process used for Supreme Court Judges.



Related: Articles 124(4), 124(5), 217, 218.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 219 — Oath or affirmation by Judges of High Courts

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: Before entering office, a High Court Judge must make and subscribe an oath or affirmation before the Governor or a person appointed by the Governor.



Simple: Before beginning work, every High Court Judge formally promises to perform judicial duties faithfully and uphold the Constitution and law.



Related: Articles 217, 219; Third Schedule.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 220 — Restriction on practice after being a permanent Judge

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Legal Profession



Law: A person who has held office as a permanent High Court Judge cannot plead or act in any court or before any authority in India, except the Supreme Court, other High Courts, and certain specified tribunals or authorities.



Simple: After retiring, a permanent High Court Judge cannot practise before ordinary courts in the same broad way as an advocate. Limited practice before the Supreme Court and other High Courts is permitted.



Related: Articles 124, 217, 220, 224A.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 221 — Salaries, etc., of Judges

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Finance



Law: High Court Judges receive salaries, allowances, leave, pension, and service conditions determined by Parliament. Their privileges, allowances, leave, pension, and other service conditions cannot be changed to their disadvantage after appointment.



Simple: Parliament decides High Court Judges’ pay and benefits, but those service benefits cannot be reduced unfairly after appointment.



Related: Articles 125, 217, 221; Second Schedule.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 222 — Transfer of a Judge from one High Court to another

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: The President may transfer a High Court Judge to another High Court after consultation with the Chief Justice of India. A transferred Judge is entitled to compensatory allowance as Parliament may determine by law.



Simple: A High Court Judge may be transferred to another High Court by the President after consultation with the Chief Justice of India.



Related: Articles 217, 222, 224, 231.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 223 — Appointment of acting Chief Justice

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: When the office of Chief Justice of a High Court is vacant, or the Chief Justice cannot perform duties because of absence or another reason, the President may appoint another High Court Judge to perform those duties.



Simple: If a High Court Chief Justice is unavailable, another Judge may temporarily act as Chief Justice.



Related: Articles 216, 217, 223, 224.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 224 — Appointment of additional and acting Judges

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: The President may appoint duly qualified persons as additional Judges for a temporary period not exceeding two years to deal with a temporary increase in High Court business or arrears. Acting Judges may be appointed when a permanent Judge is absent or temporarily acting as Chief Justice. A person aged 62 cannot be appointed under this Article.



Simple: Extra or temporary High Court Judges may be appointed to deal with workload or fill short-term vacancies.



Related: Articles 217, 223, 224, 224A.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 224A — Appointment of retired Judges at sittings of High Courts

Type: Judiciary; High Courts
