Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: The Chief Justice of a High Court may, with the previous consent of the President, request a former High Court Judge to sit and act as a Judge of that High Court. The retired Judge must consent and receives allowances as determined by the President.



Simple: A retired High Court Judge may temporarily return to assist the High Court, with consent and Presidential approval.



Related: Articles 217, 220, 224, 224A.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 225 — Jurisdiction of existing High Courts

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts



Law: Subject to the Constitution and laws made by the appropriate Legislature, existing High Courts retain their pre-Constitution jurisdiction, powers, and authority, including court rules and administration of justice.



Simple: High Courts generally continue their established powers unless the Constitution or a valid law changes them.



Related: Articles 214, 225, 226, 227, 372.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 226 — Power of High Courts to issue certain writs

Type: Judiciary; Constitutional Remedies



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Constitutional Remedies



Law: Every High Court may issue directions, orders, or writs—including habeas corpus, mandamus, prohibition, quo warranto, and certiorari—for enforcement of Fundamental Rights and “for any other purpose,” meaning other legal rights. A High Court may act where the cause of action wholly or partly arises within its territorial jurisdiction. Interim orders in certain cases require notice and opportunity to be heard.



Simple: A High Court can protect Fundamental Rights and other legal rights. It can order release from unlawful detention, direct public authorities to perform legal duties, stop unlawful proceedings, cancel unlawful orders, or question an unlawful public appointment.



Related: Articles 32, 136, 139, 226, 227, 300A.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 226A — Constitutional validity of Central laws not to be considered in proceedings under Article 226

Type: Judiciary; Historical Constitutional Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 226A restricted High Courts from considering the constitutional validity of Central laws in Article 226 proceedings.



Simple: This restriction no longer exists.



Related: Articles 13, 32, 226, 226A, 131A.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 227 — Power of superintendence over all courts by the High Court

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Subordinate Courts



Law: Every High Court has superintendence over all courts and tribunals within its territorial jurisdiction, except courts or tribunals constituted under laws relating to the Armed Forces. It may call for returns, make rules, prescribe forms, and regulate practice and proceedings of subordinate courts.



Simple: High Courts supervise lower courts and most tribunals in their State or territory to help ensure they work lawfully and properly.



Related: Articles 226, 227, 233–237.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 228 — Transfer of certain cases to High Court

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Constitutional Remedies



Law: A High Court may withdraw a case from a subordinate court if it involves a substantial question of law regarding interpretation of the Constitution. The High Court may decide the case itself or decide the constitutional question and return the case to the subordinate court with directions.



Simple: If a lower-court case raises an important constitutional question, the High Court can take the case or decide that constitutional question.



Related: Articles 132, 226, 227, 228, 228A.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 228A — Special provisions as to disposal of questions relating to constitutional validity of State laws

Type: Judiciary; Historical Constitutional Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 228A had imposed special requirements on High Courts when deciding the constitutional validity of State laws.



Simple: This Article is no longer in force and should not be used to describe current High Court procedure.



Related: Articles 13, 226, 228, 228A.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 229 — Officers and servants and the expenses of High Courts

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Finance



Law: High Court officers and servants are appointed by the Chief Justice or an authorised Judge or officer. Service conditions may be regulated by rules made by the Chief Justice, subject to law and specified approval requirements. High Court administrative expenses are charged on the Consolidated Fund of the State.



Simple: The Chief Justice manages High Court staff, and the Court’s essential administrative costs are paid from the State’s Consolidated Fund.



Related: Articles 146, 221, 229, 266.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 230 — Extension of jurisdiction of High Courts to Union Territories

Type: Judiciary; High Courts; Union Territories



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Union Territories



Law: Parliament may by law extend the jurisdiction of a High Court to, or exclude its jurisdiction from, any Union Territory.



Simple: Parliament can decide which High Court has authority over a Union Territory.



Related: Articles 214, 226, 230, 241.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 231 — Establishment of a common High Court for two or more States

Type: Judiciary; High Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Union-State Relations



Law: Parliament may by law establish a common High Court for two or more States or for two or more States and a Union Territory. Related provisions apply with necessary changes, including appointments and allocation of expenses.



Simple: Two or more States, or States and a Union Territory, may share one High Court if Parliament provides so by law.



Related: Articles 214, 217, 221, 230, 231.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 232 — Omitted

Type: Historical Constitutional Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 232 was omitted by the Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Simple: This Article is no longer part of the active constitutional framework.



Related: Articles 214–231; Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 233 — Appointment of district judges

Type: Judiciary; Subordinate Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Subordinate Courts; State Government



Law: District Judges are appointed, posted, and promoted by the Governor in consultation with the High Court. A person not already in Union or State service is eligible only if they have been an advocate or pleader for at least seven years and are recommended by the High Court.



Simple: District Judges are appointed by the Governor after consultation with the High Court. Direct recruits need at least seven years of legal practice and a High Court recommendation.



Related: Articles 233–237, 309, 234.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 234 — Recruitment of persons other than district judges to the judicial service

Type: Judiciary; Subordinate Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Subordinate Courts; State Government



Law: Recruitment to the judicial service of a State, other than District Judge posts, is made according to State rules framed by the Governor after consultation with the State Public Service Commission and the High Court.



Simple: Lower-level judicial officers are recruited under State rules made after consultation with the High Court and State Public Service Commission.



Related: Articles 233, 234, 235, 309, 315.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 235 — Control over subordinate courts

Type: Judiciary; Subordinate Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Subordinate Courts



Law: Control over district courts and courts subordinate to a High Court, including posting, promotion, and grant of leave to persons in the State judicial service, is vested in the High Court. Existing appeal rights and service-rule rights are preserved.



Simple: High Courts supervise and control the careers and administration of lower-court judges in their jurisdiction.



Related: Articles 227, 233, 234, 235.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 236 — Interpretation

Type: Judiciary; Subordinate Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Subordinate Courts



Law: For this Chapter, “district judge” includes specified judicial offices such as city civil court judges, additional district judges, sessions judges, chief judges of small causes courts, and other listed equivalent posts. “Judicial service” means service consisting exclusively of persons intended to fill the post of district judge and other civil judicial posts inferior to district judge.



Simple: This Article explains which judicial posts count as District Judge posts and what “judicial service” means.



Related: Articles 233–237.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 237 — Application of the provisions of this Chapter to certain class or classes of magistrates

Type: Judiciary; Subordinate Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Subordinate Courts; State Government



Law: The Governor may, by public notification, direct that Articles 233–236 and related provisions apply to specified classes of magistrates in a State, subject to exceptions and modifications stated in the notification.



Simple: A Governor can extend the constitutional framework for subordinate judges to certain magistrates through an official notification.



Related: Articles 233–237, 50.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 238 — Omitted

Type: Historical Constitutional Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 238 was omitted by the Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Simple: This Article is not current law and should be shown only as historical constitutional context.



Related: Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956; Articles 152, 239.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 239 — Administration of Union Territories

Type: Union Territory Administration



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Union Territories; Government Administration



Law: Every Union Territory is administered by the President through an Administrator appointed by the President, with a designation the President specifies. The President may appoint a State Governor as Administrator of an adjoining Union Territory; in that role, the Governor acts independently of the State Council of Ministers.



Simple: Union Territories are administered by the President through an Administrator or Lieutenant Governor.



Related: Articles 239A–239AB, 240, 241.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 239A — Creation of local Legislatures or Council of Ministers or both for certain Union Territories

Type: Union Territory Administration



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Union Territories; State Legislature



Law: Parliament may by law create for the Union Territory of Puducherry a body elected or partly nominated to function as a Legislature, a Council of Ministers, or both. Parliament determines composition, powers, functions, and related matters.



Simple: Parliament can provide Puducherry with its own Legislature and Council of Ministers through law.



Related: Articles 239, 239A, 239B, 240.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 239AA — Special provisions with respect to Delhi

Type: Union Territory Administration; Special Provision



Status: Active; inserted and amended



Category: Constitution; Union Territories; Delhi; State Legislature; Lieutenant Governor



Law: Delhi is the National Capital Territory of Delhi and its Administrator is the Lieutenant Governor. Delhi has a directly elected Legislative Assembly and a Council of Ministers headed by a Chief Minister to aid and advise the Lieutenant Governor on matters for which the Assembly may legislate. Delhi’s Assembly may legislate on State List and Concurrent List matters except public order, police, land, and related entries. Parliament retains power to legislate for Delhi, and Union law prevails on inconsistency, subject to the Article’s reserved-Bill rule.



Simple: Delhi has an elected Assembly and Chief Minister, but it is a Union Territory, not a full State. Delhi cannot make laws on police, public order, or land, and Parliament has overriding legislative power.



Related: Articles 239, 239AA, 239AB, 239B, 246, 254.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 239AB — Provision in case of failure of constitutional machinery

Type: Union Territory Administration; Emergency-like Special Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Union Territories; Delhi; Emergency



Law: If the President, on a report from the Lieutenant Governor or otherwise, is satisfied that Delhi’s administration cannot be carried on according to Article 239AA or related law, the President may suspend the operation of Article 239AA provisions or related law for a stated period and make incidental or consequential provisions.



Simple: If Delhi’s constitutional system cannot function properly, the President can temporarily suspend parts of Delhi’s special governance arrangement.



Related: Articles 239AA, 239B, 356.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 239B — Power of Administrator to promulgate Ordinances during recess of Legislature

Type: Union Territory Administration; Ordinance Power



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Union Territories; Ordinance; Legislative Procedure



Law: When a Union Territory Legislature created under Article 239A is not in session, its Administrator may promulgate an Ordinance with the President’s instructions if immediate action is necessary. The Ordinance has the force of a Legislature Act but must be laid before the Legislature and normally ceases six weeks after reassembly, unless earlier disapproved or withdrawn.



Simple: When the Puducherry Legislature is not meeting, its Administrator may issue a temporary law with Presidential approval if urgent action is needed.



Related: Articles 123, 213, 239A, 239AA.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 240 — Power of President to make regulations for certain Union Territories

Type: Union Territory Administration; Regulation Power



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Union Territories; President; Legislative Procedure



Law: The President may make regulations for peace, progress, and good government of Andaman and Nicobar Islands, Lakshadweep, Dadra and Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu, Puducherry in specified circumstances, and Ladakh. Such regulations have the same force as Acts of Parliament and may repeal or amend laws applicable to the relevant Union Territory.



Simple: For specified Union Territories, the President can make regulations that work like laws passed by Parliament.



Related: Articles 239, 239A, 240, 241; Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; official Gazette regulation under Article 240.



Article 241 — High Courts for Union Territories

Type: Judiciary; Union Territories



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; High Courts; Union Territories



Law: Parliament may constitute a High Court for a Union Territory or declare any court in a Union Territory to be a High Court for all or specified purposes of the Constitution. Parliament may also extend or exclude High Court jurisdiction through law.



Simple: Parliament decides the High Court structure and jurisdiction for Union Territories.



Related: Articles 214, 226, 230, 231, 241.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 242 — Coorg

Type: Historical Constitutional Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 242, which related to Coorg, was omitted by the Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Simple: This Article is no longer active and should only be retained as historical context.



Related: Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956; Articles 238, 239.



Source:

Official Constitution of India



Article 243 — Definitions

Type: Panchayats; Definitions



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats



Law: Defines key Part IX terms, including district, Gram Sabha, intermediate level, Panchayat, Panchayat area, population, village, and village-level Panchayat.



Simple: This Article explains important words used in the Panchayat provisions.



Related: Articles 243A–243O.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243A — Gram Sabha

Type: Panchayats; Gram Sabha



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Gram Sabha



Law: A Gram Sabha may exercise powers and perform functions at village level as State Legislature law provides.



Simple: A Gram Sabha is the village-level body of registered voters. Its exact local-government powers depend on State law.



Related: Articles 243, 243B, 243G; Eleventh Schedule.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243B — Constitution of Panchayats

Type: Panchayats



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Rural Governance



Law: Panchayats must be constituted at village, intermediate, and district levels in every State. A State with a population not exceeding 20 lakh need not constitute Panchayats at the intermediate level.



Simple: Rural local government normally has village, block/intermediate, and district Panchayats. Smaller States may not need the middle level.



Related: Articles 243A, 243C, 243G.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243C — Composition of Panchayats

Type: Panchayats; Local Elections



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Local Elections



Law: Panchayat seats are filled by direct election from territorial constituencies. State law regulates representation of village-level chairpersons at higher Panchayats and of MPs, MLAs, MLCs, and other members. Panchayat chairpersons are elected as State law provides.



Simple: Citizens directly elect Panchayat members. State law decides how chairpersons and some representatives are chosen.



Related: Articles 243B, 243D, 243E, 243K.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243D — Reservation of seats

Type: Panchayats; Reservation



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Equality; Local Elections



Law: Seats are reserved for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes in proportion to their population in the Panchayat area. At least one-third of total seats, including SC/ST reserved seats, are reserved for women. At least one-third of chairperson offices are reserved for women. State law may reserve seats or chairperson offices for backward classes.



Simple: Panchayats reserve seats for SCs, STs, and women. States may also provide reservation for backward classes.



Related: Articles 15, 16, 243C, 243D, 243T.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243E — Duration of Panchayats

Type: Panchayats; Local Elections



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Local Elections



Law: Every Panchayat normally continues for five years from its first meeting. Elections must be completed before its term ends, or within six months of dissolution. A Panchayat formed for the remaining period after dissolution serves only that remaining period.



Simple: Panchayats usually last five years. If dissolved early, elections normally must happen within six months.



Related: Articles 243C, 243K, 243N.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243F — Disqualifications for membership

Type: Panchayats; Membership



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Local Elections



Law: A person is disqualified from Panchayat membership if disqualified under State Legislature membership law or under State Panchayat law. A person aged 21 or above cannot be disqualified merely because they are below 25. Questions about disqualification are decided by the authority and procedure set by State law.



Simple: State law decides most Panchayat disqualifications. A person aged 21 may contest even though MLA eligibility normally starts at 25.



Related: Articles 173, 191, 243C, 243K.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243G — Powers, authority and responsibilities of Panchayats

Type: Panchayats; Local Governance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Rural Governance



Law: State Legislatures may give Panchayats powers and authority needed to function as institutions of self-government, including preparation of plans for economic development and social justice and implementation of schemes concerning Eleventh Schedule matters.



Simple: State law can give Panchayats responsibility for village development, local planning, welfare schemes, and social-justice work.



Related: Articles 243A, 243G, 243H; Eleventh Schedule.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243H — Powers to impose taxes by, and Funds of, the Panchayats

Type: Panchayats; Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Public Finance



Law: State Legislatures may authorise Panchayats to levy, collect, and appropriate taxes, duties, tolls, and fees; assign State-collected revenues; provide grants-in-aid; and establish Panchayat funds.



Simple: State law can allow Panchayats to collect local taxes or fees, receive grants, and maintain funds for local work.



Related: Articles 243G, 243H, 243I.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243I — Constitution of Finance Commission to review financial position

Type: Panchayats; Constitutional Body



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Public Finance; Constitutional Bodies



Law: Every Governor must constitute a State Finance Commission every fifth year to review Panchayat finances and recommend tax distribution, revenue assignment, grants-in-aid, and measures to improve Panchayat funds.



Simple: Every five years, a State Finance Commission reviews how Panchayats should receive and use money.



Related: Articles 243H, 243I, 243Y, 280.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243J — Audit of accounts of Panchayats

Type: Panchayats; Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Public Finance



Law: State Legislatures may make law concerning maintenance and audit of Panchayat accounts.



Simple: State law decides how Panchayat accounts must be kept and checked.



Related: Articles 243H, 243I, 243J.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243K — Elections to the Panchayats

Type: Panchayats; Local Elections



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Local Elections; Constitutional Bodies



Law: Panchayat electoral rolls and elections are supervised, directed, and controlled by the State Election Commission, consisting of a State Election Commissioner appointed by the Governor. Removal protection is equivalent to that of a High Court Judge.



Simple: The State Election Commission conducts and supervises Panchayat elections, not the Panchayat itself.



Related: Articles 243E, 243K, 243O, 324.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243L — Application to Union Territories

Type: Panchayats; Union Territories



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Union Territories



Law: Part IX applies to Union Territories, subject to modifications the President may specify by public notification.



Simple: Panchayat rules can apply in Union Territories, but the President may adapt them for local conditions.



Related: Articles 239, 243B, 243L.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243M — Part not to apply to certain areas

Type: Panchayats; Special Areas



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Panchayats; Scheduled Areas; Tribal Areas



Law: Part IX does not automatically apply to Scheduled Areas and tribal areas under Article 244, certain specified north-eastern areas, hill areas, and Darjeeling Gorkha Hill Council areas. Parliament may extend it to Scheduled Areas and tribal areas with exceptions and modifications.



Simple: Normal Panchayat rules do not automatically apply in certain tribal and special areas because those areas have separate constitutional protections.



Related: Articles 244, 243M; Fifth and Sixth Schedules.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243N — Continuance of existing laws and Panchayats

Type: Panchayats; Transitional Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats



Law: Existing Panchayat laws inconsistent with Part IX continued until amended, repealed, or one year from commencement of the Constitution (Seventy-third Amendment) Act, 1992. Existing Panchayats continued until their term ended unless dissolved earlier by State resolution.



Simple: This Article managed the change from older Panchayat systems to the current constitutional Panchayat framework.



Related: Articles 243B, 243E, 243N.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243O — Bar to interference by courts in electoral matters

Type: Panchayats; Local Elections



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Panchayats; Local Elections; Legal Procedure



Law: Courts cannot question Panchayat constituency delimitation or seat-allocation laws. Panchayat elections may be challenged only through an election petition before the authority and procedure provided by State law.



Simple: Election disputes must use the special election-petition process. Courts generally do not stop or rework Panchayat elections through ordinary cases.



Related: Articles 243K, 243O, 329.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243P — Definitions

Type: Municipalities; Definitions



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Urban Governance



Law: Defines key Part IXA terms, including Committee, district, Metropolitan Area, Municipal Area, Municipality, Panchayat, population, and transitional area.



Simple: This Article explains important words used in the constitutional rules for city and town local governments.



Related: Articles 243Q–243ZG.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243Q — Constitution of Municipalities

Type: Municipalities; Urban Governance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Urban Governance



Law: Every State must constitute Nagar Panchayats for transitional areas, Municipal Councils for smaller urban areas, and Municipal Corporations for larger urban areas. The Governor specifies these areas after considering population, density, revenue, employment, economic importance, and other factors.



Simple: Different urban areas have different local bodies: Nagar Panchayats, Municipal Councils, or Municipal Corporations.



Related: Articles 243P, 243Q, 243R, 243W.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243R — Composition of Municipalities

Type: Municipalities; Local Elections



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Local Elections



Law: Municipality seats are filled by direct election from wards. State law may provide representation for persons with special knowledge, MPs, MLAs, MLCs, and committee chairpersons. Nominated persons with special knowledge have no voting rights in Municipal meetings.



Simple: Citizens elect ward representatives. State law can add certain representatives, but nominated experts cannot vote.



Related: Articles 243Q, 243R, 243S, 243T.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243S — Constitution and composition of Wards Committees, etc.

Type: Municipalities; Ward Committees



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Urban Governance



Law: Municipalities with population of three lakh or more must establish Ward Committees consisting of one or more wards. State law determines their composition, territorial area, and method of filling seats. Where a Ward Committee has one ward, that ward’s member is its chairperson.



Simple: Large cities must have Ward Committees so local neighbourhood issues can receive focused attention.



Related: Articles 243R, 243S, 243W.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243T — Reservation of seats

Type: Municipalities; Reservation



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Equality; Local Elections



Law: Seats are reserved for SCs and STs in proportion to their municipal-area population. At least one-third of total seats, including SC/ST reserved seats, are reserved for women. At least one-third of chairperson offices are reserved for women. State law may reserve seats or chairperson offices for backward classes.



Simple: Municipal bodies reserve seats for SCs, STs, and women. States may also reserve seats for backward classes.



Related: Articles 15, 16, 243D, 243R, 243T.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243U — Duration of Municipalities

Type: Municipalities; Local Elections



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Local Elections



Law: Every Municipality normally lasts five years from its first meeting. Elections must be completed before its term ends, or within six months after dissolution. A replacement Municipality serves only for the remaining period of the original term.



Simple: Municipal bodies normally have five-year terms. If dissolved early, elections usually must happen within six months.



Related: Articles 243U, 243ZA, 243ZF.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243V — Disqualifications for membership

Type: Municipalities; Membership



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Local Elections



Law: A person is disqualified from Municipal membership if disqualified under State Legislature membership law or State Municipal law. A person aged 21 or above cannot be disqualified merely because they are below 25. State law determines the authority deciding disqualification questions.



Simple: State law governs most Municipal-election disqualifications, but a person aged 21 can contest even though MLA eligibility normally begins at 25.



Related: Articles 173, 191, 243V, 243ZA.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243W — Powers, authority and responsibilities of Municipalities

Type: Municipalities; Urban Governance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Urban Governance



Law: State Legislatures may give Municipalities powers and authority to function as institutions of self-government, including planning for economic development and social justice and implementing schemes related to Twelfth Schedule matters.



Simple: State law can give Municipalities responsibility for city planning, local services, development, and welfare schemes.



Related: Articles 243Q, 243W, 243X; Twelfth Schedule.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243X — Power to impose taxes by, and Funds of, the Municipalities

Type: Municipalities; Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Public Finance



Law: State Legislatures may authorise Municipalities to levy, collect, and appropriate taxes, duties, tolls, and fees; assign State-collected revenues; provide grants-in-aid; and establish Municipal funds.



Simple: State law can let Municipalities collect local taxes and fees, receive grants, and use funds for city services.



Related: Articles 243W, 243X, 243Y.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243Y — Finance Commission

Type: Municipalities; Constitutional Body



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Public Finance; Constitutional Bodies



Law: The State Finance Commission constituted under Article 243I must also review Municipal financial position and recommend tax distribution, revenue assignment, grants-in-aid, and measures to improve Municipal finances.



Simple: The State Finance Commission also reviews how cities and towns should receive and manage funds.



Related: Articles 243I, 243X, 243Y, 280.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243Z — Audit of accounts of Municipalities

Type: Municipalities; Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Public Finance



Law: State Legislatures may make law regarding maintenance and audit of Municipal accounts.



Simple: State law decides how Municipal accounts are maintained and audited.



Related: Articles 243X, 243Y, 243Z.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243ZA — Elections to the Municipalities

Type: Municipalities; Local Elections



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Local Elections; Constitutional Bodies



Law: Municipal electoral rolls and elections are supervised, directed, and controlled by the State Election Commission. State law may provide all matters relating to Municipal elections.



Simple: The State Election Commission conducts and supervises Municipal elections.



Related: Articles 243K, 243U, 243ZA, 243ZG.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243ZB — Application to Union Territories

Type: Municipalities; Union Territories



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Union Territories



Law: Part IXA applies to Union Territories, subject to modifications the President may specify by public notification.



Simple: Municipal constitutional rules can apply in Union Territories, with changes the President may make for local conditions.



Related: Articles 239, 243L, 243ZB.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243ZC — Part not to apply to certain areas

Type: Municipalities; Special Areas



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Municipalities; Scheduled Areas; Tribal Areas



Law: Part IXA does not automatically apply to Scheduled Areas and tribal areas under Article 244 or certain specified north-eastern and hill areas. Parliament may extend it to Scheduled Areas and tribal areas with exceptions and modifications.



Simple: Regular Municipal rules do not automatically apply in some protected tribal and special areas.



Related: Articles 244, 243M, 243ZC; Fifth and Sixth Schedules.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243ZD — Committee for district planning

Type: Municipalities; Planning Body



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Panchayats; Urban Governance



Law: Every State must constitute a District Planning Committee at district level to consolidate plans prepared by Panchayats and Municipalities and prepare a draft development plan for the district. At least four-fifths of its members are elected by and from elected Panchayat and Municipal members.



Simple: District Planning Committees combine village and city plans into one district development plan.



Related: Articles 243G, 243W, 243ZD.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243ZE — Committee for Metropolitan planning

Type: Municipalities; Planning Body



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Urban Governance



Law: Every metropolitan area must have a Metropolitan Planning Committee to prepare a draft development plan for the area. At least two-thirds of members are elected by and from elected Municipal and Panchayat members in the metropolitan area.



Simple: Large urban regions must have a planning committee that coordinates development across multiple local bodies.



Related: Articles 243P, 243W, 243ZD, 243ZE.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243ZF — Continuance of existing laws and Municipalities

Type: Municipalities; Transitional Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities



Law: Existing Municipal laws inconsistent with Part IXA continued until amended, repealed, or one year from commencement of the Constitution (Seventy-fourth Amendment) Act, 1992. Existing Municipalities continued until their term ended unless dissolved earlier.



Simple: This Article managed the shift from older Municipal systems to the current constitutional Municipal framework.



Related: Articles 243Q, 243U, 243ZF.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243ZG — Bar to interference by courts in electoral matters

Type: Municipalities; Local Elections



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Local Government; Municipalities; Local Elections; Legal Procedure



Law: Courts cannot question Municipal constituency delimitation or seat-allocation laws. Municipal elections may be challenged only through an election petition before the authority and procedure provided by State law.



Simple: Municipal election disputes must use the special election-petition process, not ordinary court cases.



Related: Articles 243ZA, 243ZG, 329.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 243ZH — Definitions

Type: Co-operative Societies; Definitions



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies



Law: Defines Part IXB terms including authorised person, board, co-operative society, multi-State co-operative society, office bearer, Registrar, State Act, and State-level co-operative society.



Simple: This Article explains key terms used for constitutional co-operative-society provisions.



Related: Articles 43B, 243ZI–243ZT.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZI — Incorporation of co-operative societies

Type: Co-operative Societies



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies



Law: State Legislatures may make law for incorporation, regulation, and winding up of co-operative societies based on voluntary formation, democratic member control, member economic participation, and autonomous functioning.



Simple: Co-operatives should be voluntary, democratically run by members, and able to function independently, subject to applicable law.



Related: Articles 43B, 243ZH, 243ZI, 243ZR.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZJ — Number and term of members of board and its office bearers

Type: Co-operative Societies



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies



Law: A co-operative board must have no more than 21 directors. Its elected members and office bearers normally serve five years. The Article provides reservation for SC/ST and women on boards and permits co-opted expert directors without voting rights.



Simple: Co-operative boards normally have limited membership and five-year terms, with reserved representation for SC/ST members and women.



Related: Articles 243ZI, 243ZK, 243ZL.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZK — Election of members of board

Type: Co-operative Societies; Elections



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies; Elections



Law: Board elections must occur before the board’s term expires. Superintendence, direction, and control of preparation of electoral rolls and conduct of elections are vested in the body provided by State law.



Simple: Co-operative board elections should happen before the existing board’s term ends.



Related: Articles 243ZJ, 243ZK, 243ZL.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZL — Supersession and suspension of board and interim management

Type: Co-operative Societies; Governance



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies



Law: A co-operative board cannot ordinarily be superseded or kept under administrator control for more than six months. Supersession is permitted only on stated grounds, including persistent default, negligence, prejudicial conduct, stalemate, or election failure. The Article provides exceptions for specified banking co-operatives.



Simple: A government cannot normally remove a co-operative board for long periods. Removal or administrator control has constitutional limits.



Related: Articles 243ZJ, 243ZK, 243ZL.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZM — Audit of accounts of co-operative societies

Type: Co-operative Societies; Public Finance



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies; Public Finance



Law: Co-operative accounts must be audited at least once every financial year by auditors meeting qualifications and experience prescribed by State law. The Article identifies matters that audit must cover.



Simple: Co-operatives must have their accounts checked every year by qualified auditors.



Related: Articles 243ZL, 243ZM, 243ZP.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZN — Convening of general body meetings

Type: Co-operative Societies; Governance



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies



Law: A co-operative society must convene its annual general body meeting within six months of the end of each financial year. State law may provide for convening a special general body meeting.



Simple: Co-operative members should meet at least once every year to review the society’s work and accounts.



Related: Articles 243ZM, 243ZN, 243ZO.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZO — Right of a member to get information

Type: Co-operative Societies; Member Rights



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies



Law: Members have a right to obtain information concerning co-operative books, records, and affairs as State law provides.



Simple: A co-operative member can seek information about the society’s records and functioning under applicable law.



Related: Articles 243ZM, 243ZN, 243ZO.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZP — Returns

Type: Co-operative Societies; Compliance



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies; Public Finance



Law: Every co-operative society must file specified annual returns with the authority designated by applicable law, including audit reports, board reports, general-body meeting details, and information on elections.



Simple: Co-operatives must submit yearly reports and audit-related information to the relevant authority.



Related: Articles 243ZM, 243ZN, 243ZP.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZQ — Offences and penalties

Type: Co-operative Societies; Compliance



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies



Law: State Legislatures may make law providing offences and penalties for breach of Part IXB-related co-operative requirements.



Simple: Applicable co-operative law may penalise violations of required co-operative governance and reporting rules.



Related: Articles 243ZI, 243ZP, 243ZQ.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZR — Application to multi-State co-operative societies

Type: Co-operative Societies; Multi-State Co-operatives



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies; Union-State Relations



Law: Part IXB applies to multi-State co-operative societies, subject to necessary modifications and without affecting Parliament’s power to legislate under Entry 44 of the Union List.



Simple: Part IXB continues to apply to co-operatives operating across more than one State.



Related: Articles 43B, 243ZH–243ZT; Union List Entry 44.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZS — Application to Union Territories

Type: Co-operative Societies; Union Territories



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies; Union Territories



Law: Part IXB applies to Union Territories, subject to modifications the President may specify by public notification.



Simple: These co-operative provisions apply in Union Territories, with changes the President may make where needed.



Related: Articles 239, 243ZR, 243ZS.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.



Article 243ZT — Continuance of existing laws

Type: Co-operative Societies; Transitional Provision



Status: Operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories



Category: Constitution; Co-operative Societies; Transitional Provisions



Law: Existing co-operative laws inconsistent with Part IXB were to continue until amended, repealed, or one year from commencement of the Constitution (Ninety-seventh Amendment) Act, 2011. The Supreme Court held Part IXB operative only for multi-State co-operative societies and Union Territories, not State co-operative societies.



Simple: For State co-operatives, use the relevant State co-operative law. Part IXB remains constitutionally operative for multi-State co-operatives and Union Territories.



Related: Articles 43B, 243ZR, 243ZS; Article 368.



Source:

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, Union of India v. Rajendra N. Shah, 2021.

Part X — Scheduled and Tribal Areas

Article 244 — Administration of Scheduled Areas and Tribal Areas



Type: Scheduled Areas / Tribal Areas

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Scheduled Areas; Tribal Areas



Law: The Fifth Schedule governs the administration and control of Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes in most States, while the Sixth Schedule provides special arrangements for tribal areas in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram.



Simple: The Constitution gives certain tribal areas special protections and special administrative arrangements.



Related: Fifth Schedule; Sixth Schedule; Article 244A



Source: Constitution of India — Article 244.



Article 244A — Formation of an autonomous State comprising certain tribal areas in Assam and creation of local Legislature or Council of Ministers or both



Type: Tribal Areas / Autonomous State

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Tribal Areas; Assam



Law: Parliament may create an autonomous State within Assam from specified tribal areas and provide for a Legislature, Council of Ministers, or both.



Simple: Parliament can create a special autonomous State arrangement for certain tribal areas of Assam.



Related: Articles 244, 275; Sixth Schedule



Source: Constitution of India — Article 244A.



Part XI — Relations Between the Union and the States

Chapter I — Legislative Relations

Article 245 — Extent of laws made by Parliament and by the Legislatures of States



Type: Legislative Powers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations; Legislation



Law: Parliament may make laws for the whole or any part of India. A State Legislature may make laws for the whole or any part of that State. A Parliamentary law is not invalid merely because it has extra-territorial operation.



Simple: Parliament makes laws for India, while State Legislatures make laws for their States, within constitutional limits.



Related: Articles 246, 246A, 249–253



Source: Constitution of India — Article 245.



Article 246 — Subject-matter of laws made by Parliament and by the Legislatures of States



Type: Legislative Powers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations; Legislation



Law: Legislative subjects are divided mainly through the Union List, State List and Concurrent List in the Seventh Schedule. Parliament has exclusive power over the Union List; States generally legislate on the State List; both can legislate on the Concurrent List, subject to constitutional rules.



Simple: The Constitution decides which subjects Parliament and State Governments can make laws about.



Related: Seventh Schedule; Articles 245, 246A, 254



Source: Constitution of India — Article 246.



Article 246A — Special provision with respect to goods and services tax



Type: GST / Legislative Powers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; GST; Finance



Law: Parliament and, subject to the constitutional framework, State Legislatures have power to make laws concerning GST. Parliament has exclusive power regarding GST on inter-State supplies.



Simple: Both the Centre and States can make GST laws, while Parliament has special authority for inter-State GST.



Related: Article 269A; Article 279A



Source: Constitution of India — Article 246A.



Article 247 — Power of Parliament to provide for the establishment of certain additional courts



Type: Judicial / Legislative Power

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Parliament



Law: Parliament may establish additional courts for better administration of laws made by Parliament or of existing laws relating to matters within Parliament's legislative competence.



Simple: Parliament can create additional courts when needed to properly administer certain central laws.



Related: Article 246; Judiciary



Source: Constitution of India — Article 247.



Article 248 — Residuary powers of legislation



Type: Legislative Powers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; Union Powers



Law: Parliament has exclusive power to make laws on matters not included in the State List or Concurrent List, including taxation not mentioned in those Lists.



Simple: If a subject is not assigned to the States or shared with them, Parliament generally has the power to legislate on it.



Related: Articles 245, 246; Seventh Schedule



Source: Constitution of India — Article 248.



Article 249 — Power of Parliament to legislate with respect to a matter in the State List in the national interest



Type: Legislative Powers

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; National Interest



Law: Parliament may legislate on a State List matter when the Rajya Sabha passes a resolution by the required special majority declaring it necessary in the national interest.



Simple: In special national-interest situations, Parliament can temporarily make laws on subjects normally handled by States.



Related: Articles 250, 251



Source: Constitution of India — Article 249.



Article 250 — Power of Parliament to legislate with respect to any matter in the State List if a Proclamation of Emergency is in operation



Type: Emergency Legislative Power

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Emergency; Parliament



Law: While a National Emergency is in operation, Parliament may make laws on matters in the State List.



Simple: During a National Emergency, Parliament can temporarily make laws on subjects normally handled by State Legislatures.



Related: Articles 352, 251



Source: Constitution of India — Article 250.



Article 251 — Inconsistency between laws made by Parliament under Articles 249 and 250 and laws made by State Legislatures



Type: Legislative Conflict

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations



Law: When a Parliamentary law made under Article 249 or 250 conflicts with a State law on the same subject, the Parliamentary law prevails while it remains in force.



Simple: In these special situations, the applicable Parliamentary law overrides a conflicting State law.



Related: Articles 249, 250, 254



Source: Constitution of India — Article 251.



Article 252 — Power of Parliament to legislate for two or more States by consent



Type: Legislative Cooperation

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations



Law: Parliament may make laws for two or more States if those States request Parliament to legislate on a State subject. Other States may later adopt such a law.



Simple: States can ask Parliament to create one common law for them on a subject normally under State control.



Related: Articles 246, 253



Source: Constitution of India — Article 252.



Article 253 — Legislation for giving effect to international agreements



Type: Legislative Powers / International Relations

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Parliament; International Relations



Law: Parliament may make laws for implementing treaties, agreements, conventions, or decisions made at international conferences or bodies.



Simple: Parliament can make laws needed to implement India's international commitments.



Related: Article 51; Article 246



Source: Constitution of India — Article 253.



Article 254 — Inconsistency between laws made by Parliament and laws made by State Legislatures



Type: Legislative Conflict

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations; Legislation



Law: In cases covered by the Concurrent List, if a State law conflicts with a Parliamentary law, the Parliamentary law generally prevails, subject to the constitutional exceptions concerning Presidential assent and subsequent Parliamentary legislation.



Simple: When valid Central and State laws conflict on a shared subject, the Central law normally wins.



Related: Articles 246, 251, 252



Source: Constitution of India — Article 254.



Article 255 — Requirements as to recommendations and previous sanctions to be regarded as matters of procedure only



Type: Legislative Procedure

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Legislation



Law: Certain requirements for recommendations, previous sanctions or consent are treated as procedural matters and do not automatically invalidate a law merely because the requirement was not complied with as required.



Simple: Some procedural approval requirements do not automatically make an entire law invalid.



Related: Articles 109, 110, 117 and State legislative provisions



Source: Constitution of India — Article 255.



Chapter II — Administrative Relations

Article 256 — Obligation of States and the Union



Type: Administrative Relations

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations; Administration



Law: States must exercise their executive power to ensure compliance with Parliamentary laws and existing laws applicable in the State. The Union may give directions to a State for that purpose.



Simple: States must follow applicable Central laws, and the Union can issue directions to help ensure compliance.



Related: Articles 257, 355



Source: Constitution of India.



Article 257 — Control of the Union over States in certain cases



Type: Administrative Relations

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations; Administration



Law: State executive power must not interfere with the exercise of Union executive power. The Union may give directions regarding specified matters, including national or military importance of communications and protection of railways.



Simple: States must not obstruct certain national interests, and the Union can give directions in specified constitutional situations.



Related: Articles 256, 258, 355



Source: Constitution of India.



Article 258 — Power of the Union to confer powers, etc., on States in certain cases



Type: Administrative Relations

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations



Law: The President may, with the consent of a State Government, entrust Union functions to the State or its officers.



Simple: The Union can ask State authorities to carry out certain Union functions, with the State's consent, under the Constitution.



Related: Article 257



Source: Constitution of India.



Article 258A — Power of the States to entrust functions to the Union



Type: Administrative Relations

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations



Law: A Governor may, with the consent of the Government of India, entrust State functions to the Union or its officers.



Simple: A State can ask the Union to carry out certain State functions with the Union's consent.



Related: Article 258



Source: Constitution of India.



Article 259 — Omitted



Type: Historical Provision

Status: Omitted

Category: Constitution; Historical



Law: Article 259 is omitted and is not an active constitutional provision.



Simple: Article 259 no longer operates as current constitutional law.



Related: Part XI



Source: Constitution of India.



Article 260 — Jurisdiction of the Union in relation to territories outside India



Type: Union Powers / External Territories

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union Powers; International Relations



Law: The Government of India may, by agreement with a government outside India, undertake executive, legislative or judicial functions in that territory where the agreement so provides.



Simple: India can perform certain government functions in another territory if a valid agreement allows it.



Related: Articles 73, 253



Source: Constitution of India.



Article 261 — Public acts, records and judicial proceedings



Type: Inter-State Legal Recognition

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Union-State Relations



Law: Full faith and credit must be given throughout India to public acts, records and judicial proceedings of the Union and every State. Parliament may provide rules for their proof and effect.



Simple: Official government records and court decisions are generally recognized across India according to constitutional and legal rules.



Related: Judiciary; Evidence law



Source: Constitution of India.



Article 262 — Adjudication of disputes relating to waters of inter-State rivers or river valleys



Type: Inter-State Water Disputes

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations; Water



Law: Parliament may provide for adjudication of disputes over the waters of inter-State rivers or river valleys and may exclude the jurisdiction of courts over such disputes as provided by law.



Simple: Parliament can create a special legal system for disputes between States over shared rivers and may limit ordinary court jurisdiction over those disputes.



Related: Union-State Relations; Inter-State Water Disputes law



Source: Constitution of India.



Article 263 — Provisions with respect to an inter-State Council



Type: Inter-State Coordination

Status: Active

Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations; Inter-State Council



Law: The President may establish an Inter-State Council to inquire into and advise on disputes between States, discuss subjects of common interest, and make recommendations for better coordination of policy and action.



Simple: The Constitution allows a body to help the Centre and States work together and resolve issues between States.



Related: Articles 256–262



Source: Constitution of India.

Article 264 — Interpretation

Type: Finance; Interpretation



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance



Law: In Part XII, “Finance Commission” means the Commission constituted under Article 280.



Simple: This Article explains that “Finance Commission” in this Part refers to the constitutional body under Article 280.



Related: Articles 264, 280.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 265 — Taxes not to be imposed save by authority of law

Type: Taxation



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation



Law: No tax may be levied or collected except by authority of law.



Simple: The government cannot legally collect a tax unless a law authorises it.



Related: Articles 246, 265, 266, 268–281.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 266 — Consolidated Funds and public accounts of India and of the States

Type: Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Public Finance



Law: Union and State revenues, loans, and loan repayments form their Consolidated Funds. Other public money is credited to Public Accounts. Withdrawals from a Consolidated Fund require constitutional or legal authority.



Simple: Governments keep main revenue and borrowing money in Consolidated Funds. Other public money goes into Public Accounts.



Related: Articles 112, 202, 266, 267, 283.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 267 — Contingency Fund

Type: Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Public Finance



Law: Parliament and State Legislatures may establish Contingency Funds for unforeseen expenditure, placed at the disposal of the President or Governor.



Simple: Contingency Funds allow urgent government spending for unforeseen situations.



Related: Articles 112, 202, 266, 267.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 268 — Duties levied by the Union but collected and appropriated by the States

Type: Taxation; Union-State Relations



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; Union-State Relations



Law: Specified stamp duties and duties of excise on medicinal and toilet preparations are levied by the Union but collected and kept by States where levied.



Simple: Some taxes are imposed under Union authority but collected and retained by States.



Related: Articles 268, 269, 270; Union List.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 268A — Service tax levied by Union and collected and appropriated by Union and States

Type: Historical Taxation Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; GST



Law: Article 268A formerly dealt with service tax sharing between the Union and States.



Simple: This Article is no longer active. GST-related constitutional provisions now govern the relevant tax framework.



Related: Articles 246A, 269A, 270, 279A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 269 — Taxes levied and collected by the Union but assigned to the States

Type: Taxation; Union-State Relations



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; Union-State Relations



Law: Taxes on sale or purchase of goods, other than newspapers, in inter-State trade or commerce are levied and collected by the Union but assigned to States as Parliament provides.



Simple: Some taxes on inter-State goods transactions are collected by the Union and then assigned to States.



Related: Articles 246A, 269, 269A, 270.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 269A — Levy and collection of goods and services tax in course of inter-State trade or commerce

Type: GST; Taxation



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Finance; GST; Union-State Relations



Law: GST on inter-State supplies is levied and collected by the Union and apportioned between Union and States as Parliament provides on GST Council recommendation. Imports are treated as inter-State supplies.



Simple: IGST on transactions across State borders, including imports, is collected by the Union and shared under constitutional rules.



Related: Articles 246A, 269A, 270, 279A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 270 — Taxes levied and distributed between the Union and the States

Type: Taxation; Revenue Distribution



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; Union-State Relations



Law: Taxes and duties levied and collected by the Union, except stated exclusions, are distributed between the Union and States according to Finance Commission recommendations and parliamentary law.



Simple: Many Union taxes are shared with States through a constitutional revenue-sharing system.



Related: Articles 268, 269, 269A, 270, 280.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 271 — Surcharge on certain duties and taxes for purposes of the Union

Type: Taxation



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation



Law: Parliament may increase specified Union taxes and duties by surcharge for Union purposes. Surcharge proceeds form part of the Consolidated Fund of India.



Simple: Parliament can add a surcharge to certain taxes, and that money belongs to the Union.



Related: Articles 266, 270, 271.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 272 — Taxes which are levied and collected by the Union and may be distributed between Union and States

Type: Historical Taxation Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation



Law: Article 272 was omitted by the Constitution (Eightieth Amendment) Act, 2000.



Simple: This Article is no longer active; Union-State tax sharing is governed mainly by Article 270.



Related: Articles 269, 270, 280.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 273 — Grants in lieu of export duty on jute and jute products

Type: Grants; Transitional Finance



Status: Active in text; spent transitional provision



Category: Constitution; Finance; Union-State Relations



Law: Parliament could provide grants to specified States in lieu of jute export duty for a period not exceeding ten years from Constitution commencement.



Simple: This was a temporary financial arrangement connected with jute export duty and has no continuing ordinary effect.



Related: Articles 275, 282, 280.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 274 — Prior recommendation of President required for certain taxation Bills

Type: Legislative Procedure; Taxation



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; Parliament



Law: Bills affecting specified taxation and Union-State revenue-distribution matters require the President’s recommendation before introduction or moving, subject to the Article’s exceptions.



Simple: Some tax Bills need Presidential recommendation before Parliament can proceed with them.



Related: Articles 110, 117, 246A, 269, 270.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 275 — Grants from the Union to certain States

Type: Grants; Union-State Relations



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Union-State Relations; Scheduled Areas



Law: Parliament may provide grants-in-aid from Union revenues for States needing assistance and grants for Scheduled Tribe welfare and Scheduled Area administration.



Simple: The Union can provide grants to States, including for tribal welfare and Scheduled Areas.



Related: Articles 244, 275, 280; Fifth and Sixth Schedules.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 276 — Taxes on professions, trades, callings and employments

Type: Taxation



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; State Government



Law: States and local authorities may impose profession tax. The constitutional maximum payable by one person to a State or local authority is ₹2,500 annually.



Simple: States or local bodies may charge profession tax, but the Constitution caps it at ₹2,500 per year per person.



Related: Articles 246, 276, 277.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 277 — Savings

Type: Taxation; Saving Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation



Law: Taxes, duties, cesses, or fees lawfully levied before Constitution commencement could continue until Parliament provided otherwise, even if inconsistent with the normal legislative distribution.



Simple: Some old taxes were allowed to continue temporarily after the Constitution began.



Related: Articles 246, 265, 277, 372.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 278 — Agreements with States in Part B of the First Schedule with regard to certain financial matters

Type: Historical Finance Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Finance; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 278 was omitted by the Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Simple: This Article related to the former Part B State structure and is no longer active.



Related: Articles 1, 275, 278; Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 279 — Calculation of “net proceeds”, etc.

Type: Public Finance; Audit



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; Constitutional Bodies



Law: “Net proceeds” means tax or duty proceeds minus collection costs. The Comptroller and Auditor-General certifies net proceeds, and that certificate is final.



Simple: The CAG calculates the amount left after tax collection costs for constitutional revenue-sharing purposes.



Related: Articles 148–151, 270, 279, 280.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 279A — Goods and Services Tax Council

Type: Constitutional Body; GST



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; GST; Finance; Constitutional Bodies



Law: The President constitutes the GST Council, chaired by the Union Finance Minister, with Union and State representatives. It recommends GST rates, exemptions, model laws, thresholds, special rates, and other GST matters.



Simple: The GST Council is the constitutional forum where the Union and States coordinate GST policy.



Related: Articles 246A, 269A, 270, 279A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 280 — Finance Commission

Type: Constitutional Body; Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Union-State Relations; Constitutional Bodies



Law: The President constitutes a Finance Commission every fifth year or earlier. It recommends tax distribution, grants-in-aid, measures to augment State funds for Panchayats and Municipalities, and other referred financial matters.



Simple: The Finance Commission recommends how Union tax revenue should be shared with States and local bodies.



Related: Articles 270, 275, 280, 281, 243I, 243Y.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 281 — Recommendations of Finance Commission

Type: Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Parliament



Law: Finance Commission recommendations and an explanatory memorandum on governmental action must be laid before each House of Parliament.



Simple: Parliament must receive the Finance Commission report and the government’s explanation of what it will do about it.



Related: Articles 112, 280, 281.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 282 — Expenditure defrayable by the Union or a State out of its revenues

Type: Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Union-State Relations



Law: The Union or a State may make grants for any public purpose, even if that purpose is outside its normal legislative competence.



Simple: The Union and States can spend public money for a public purpose, including by giving grants.



Related: Articles 246, 275, 282.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 283 — Custody, etc., of Consolidated Funds, Contingency Funds and public accounts

Type: Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Public Finance



Law: Parliament and State Legislatures may regulate custody, payment into, and withdrawal from their Consolidated Funds, Contingency Funds, and Public Accounts.



Simple: Laws regulate how government money is kept, deposited, and withdrawn.



Related: Articles 266, 267, 283.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 284 — Custody of suitors’ deposits and other moneys received by public servants and courts

Type: Public Finance; Courts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Judiciary



Law: Money received by public servants or courts in connection with matters other than government revenue must be paid into the public account or otherwise kept as provided by law.



Simple: Money held by courts or officials for cases or other non-tax reasons must be safely handled under law.



Related: Articles 266, 283, 284.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 285 — Exemption of property of the Union from State taxation

Type: Taxation; Government Property



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; Government Property



Law: Union property is exempt from State or local taxation unless Parliament provides otherwise. Property used for commercial purposes may be taxed as Parliament permits.



Simple: States generally cannot tax Union Government property unless Parliament allows it.



Related: Articles 285, 289, 298.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 286 — Restrictions as to imposition of tax on sale or purchase of goods

Type: Taxation



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; GST



Law: States cannot tax supplies occurring outside the State, in import/export course, or in inter-State trade or commerce. Parliament may formulate principles determining when a supply falls within these categories.



Simple: A State cannot tax a transaction that legally belongs to another State, international trade, or inter-State trade.



Related: Articles 246A, 269A, 286, 301.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 287 — Exemption from taxes on electricity

Type: Taxation



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation



Law: States cannot tax electricity consumed by the Union or sold to the Union for Union consumption, unless Parliament provides otherwise.



Simple: States generally cannot tax electricity used by the Union Government.



Related: Articles 246, 287, 288.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 288 — Exemption from taxation by States in respect of water or electricity in certain cases

Type: Taxation; Union-State Relations



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; Union-State Relations



Law: States cannot impose specified taxes on water or electricity stored, generated, consumed, distributed, or sold by authorities established by Parliament without Presidential consent.



Simple: In some Union-controlled water and electricity projects, States need Presidential consent before imposing relevant taxes.



Related: Articles 246, 288, 289.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 289 — Exemption of property and income of a State from Union taxation

Type: Taxation; Government Property



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Taxation; Government Property



Law: State property and income are exempt from Union taxation. Parliament may tax State trade, business, or commercial undertakings, except activities declared incidental to ordinary government functions.



Simple: The Union generally cannot tax State property or income, but Parliament may tax commercial activities run by a State.



Related: Articles 285, 289, 298.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 290 — Adjustment in respect of certain expenses and pensions

Type: Public Finance; Transitional Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Union-State Relations



Law: The Union and States must make financial adjustments regarding specified pre-Constitution expenses and pensions as Parliament may provide.



Simple: This Article addresses certain historical financial adjustments between the Union and States.



Related: Articles 290, 290A, 300.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 290A — Annual payment to certain Devaswom Funds

Type: Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Religious Institutions



Law: ₹46.5 lakh must be charged annually on the Consolidated Fund of India and paid to the Devaswom Funds of Travancore and Cochin as specified.



Simple: The Constitution provides a fixed annual Union payment to specified Devaswom Funds.



Related: Articles 266, 290A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 291 — Privy purses and other privileges of Rulers of Indian States

Type: Historical Finance Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Finance; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 291 was omitted by the Constitution (Twenty-sixth Amendment) Act, 1971.



Simple: Privy-purse payments to former Rulers are not current constitutional entitlements.



Related: Article 363A; Constitution (Twenty-sixth Amendment) Act, 1971.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 292 — Borrowing by the Government of India

Type: Public Finance; Borrowing



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; Public Finance



Law: The Union may borrow on the security of the Consolidated Fund of India within limits fixed by Parliament.



Simple: The Union Government may borrow money, subject to limits Parliament sets.



Related: Articles 266, 292, 293.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 293 — Borrowing by States

Type: Public Finance; Borrowing



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Finance; State Government



Law: States may borrow within India on the security of their Consolidated Funds, subject to State legislative limits. A State owing money to the Union needs Union consent before borrowing, and consent may carry conditions.



Simple: States can borrow, but if they already owe money to the Union, they need Union consent for further borrowing.



Related: Articles 266, 292, 293.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 294 — Succession to property, assets, rights, liabilities and obligations in certain cases

Type: Government Property; Transitional Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Property; Government Property
