Law: Specified property, assets, rights, liabilities, and obligations of the former Dominion and Provinces passed to the Union or corresponding States at Constitution commencement.



Simple: This Article transferred government property and liabilities from the old colonial structure to the Union and States.



Related: Articles 295, 296, 300, 372.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 295 — Succession to property, assets, rights, liabilities and obligations in other cases

Type: Government Property; Transitional Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Property; Government Property



Law: This Article governs succession of property, assets, rights, liabilities, and obligations in cases involving Indian States and other pre-Constitution entities.



Simple: It deals with how certain former princely-State property and obligations passed to current governments.



Related: Articles 294, 295, 300, 372.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 296 — Property accruing by escheat or lapse or as bona vacantia

Type: Government Property



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Property; Government Property



Law: Property that would have accrued to the Crown by escheat, lapse, or as ownerless property accrues to the Union or State according to the constitutional allocation of executive authority.



Simple: Certain ownerless property, or property without legal heirs, can legally pass to the Union or State.



Related: Articles 296, 297, 300, 300A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 297 — Things of value within territorial waters, continental shelf and exclusive economic zone

Type: Government Property



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Property; Government Property; Union-State Relations



Law: All minerals and other things of value underlying the ocean within India’s territorial waters, continental shelf, and exclusive economic zone vest in the Union and are held for Union purposes.



Simple: Valuable offshore resources, such as minerals beneath the sea, belong to the Union Government.



Related: Articles 246, 297, 298; Union List.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 298 — Power to carry on trade, etc.

Type: Government Property; Trade



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Government Property; Trade \& Commerce



Law: Union and State executive power extends to carrying on trade or business, acquiring, holding, and disposing of property, and making contracts, subject to legislative competence.



Simple: Union and State governments may run businesses, own property, sell property, and make contracts within their lawful powers.



Related: Articles 162, 298, 299, 300.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 299 — Contracts

Type: Government Contracts



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Government Contracts; Public Finance



Law: Union contracts must be expressed to be made by the President and State contracts by the Governor, and executed by authorised persons. The President or Governor is not personally liable for such contracts.



Simple: Government contracts must follow formal constitutional requirements and be signed by authorised officials.



Related: Articles 154, 166, 298, 299, 300.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 300 — Suits and proceedings

Type: Government Liability; Legal Procedure



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Government Contracts; Legal Procedure



Law: The Government of India and State Governments may sue and be sued in their respective names, subject to constitutional and statutory limits.



Simple: A person may bring a lawful case against the Union or a State, and governments may also bring cases.



Related: Articles 299, 300, 300A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 300A — Persons not to be deprived of property save by authority of law

Type: Property Protection



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Property; Constitutional Rights



Law: No person may be deprived of property except by authority of law. Article 300A is outside Part III and is a constitutional legal protection, not a Fundamental Right.



Simple: The government cannot take your property unless a valid law gives it authority to do so. Property is protected constitutionally, but it is not a Fundamental Right.



Related: Articles 31 omitted, 300, 300A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 301 — Freedom of trade, commerce and intercourse

Type: Trade \& Commerce



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Trade \& Commerce; Union-State Relations



Law: Trade, commerce, and intercourse throughout India are free, subject to other Part XIII provisions.



Simple: India is intended to function as a common economic area where trade and movement of goods are generally free.



Related: Articles 301–307.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 302 — Power of Parliament to impose restrictions on trade, commerce and intercourse

Type: Trade \& Commerce



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Trade \& Commerce; Parliament



Law: Parliament may impose restrictions on trade, commerce, or intercourse between States or within India when required in the public interest.



Simple: Parliament can restrict trade if it is necessary for the public interest.



Related: Articles 301, 302, 303, 304.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 303 — Restrictions on legislative powers regarding trade and commerce

Type: Trade \& Commerce; Union-State Relations



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Trade \& Commerce; Union-State Relations



Law: Parliament and States cannot give preference to one State over another or discriminate between States in trade matters, except Parliament may do so to address scarcity of goods in any part of India.



Simple: Governments generally cannot unfairly favour one State over another in trade, except Parliament may act to deal with shortages.



Related: Articles 301, 302, 303, 304.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 304 — Restrictions on trade, commerce and intercourse among States

Type: Trade \& Commerce



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Trade \& Commerce; State Government



Law: States may tax imported goods if similar local goods are taxed equally and may impose reasonable public-interest restrictions on trade, but a Bill for such restrictions requires prior Presidential sanction.



Simple: States may regulate trade in limited ways, but cannot discriminate against goods from other States and need Presidential approval for certain restrictions.



Related: Articles 301–304.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 305 — Saving of existing laws and laws providing for State monopolies

Type: Trade \& Commerce; Saving Provision



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Trade \& Commerce; State Government



Law: Existing laws and laws creating State monopolies may continue despite Articles 301–303, subject to constitutional conditions.



Simple: Some existing trade laws and State-run monopolies can remain valid despite the general freedom of trade rule.



Related: Articles 19(1)(g), 301–305.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 306 — Power of certain States in Part B of the First Schedule to impose restrictions on trade and commerce

Type: Historical Trade Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Trade \& Commerce; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 306 was omitted by the Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Simple: This Article concerned the former Part B State structure and is no longer active.



Related: Articles 301–305; Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 307 — Appointment of authority for carrying out purposes of Articles 301 to 304

Type: Trade \& Commerce



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Trade \& Commerce; Parliament



Law: Parliament may appoint an authority considered appropriate for carrying out Articles 301–304 and confer powers and duties on it.



Simple: Parliament can create an authority to help implement constitutional rules on free trade and permitted trade restrictions.



Related: Articles 301–304, 307.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 308 — Interpretation

Type: Public Services; Interpretation



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; Government Employees



Law: In Part XIV, “State” does not include Jammu and Kashmir unless the context otherwise requires. The Article also explains terms relating to existing services.



Simple: This Article defines terms used in constitutional rules about government employees and public services.



Related: Articles 308–323.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 309 — Recruitment and conditions of service

Type: Public Services



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; Government Employees



Law: Parliament or State Legislatures may regulate recruitment and service conditions for Union or State public services. Until law is made, the President or Governor may make service rules.



Simple: Government employee recruitment and service rules come from laws or authorised rules made by the Union or State.



Related: Articles 309–311, 315–323.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 310 — Tenure of office of persons serving the Union or a State

Type: Public Services; Pleasure Doctrine



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; Government Employees



Law: Union civil servants hold office during the President’s pleasure; State civil servants hold office during the Governor’s pleasure, subject to Article 311. Certain contract appointments may include compensation for premature termination.



Simple: Government employees generally serve under the constitutional “pleasure” rule, but Article 311 gives important protections against arbitrary dismissal.



Related: Articles 309, 310, 311.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 311 — Dismissal, removal or reduction in rank of persons employed in civil capacities

Type: Public Services; Service Safeguard



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Public Services; Government Employees; Legal Procedure



Law: A civil servant cannot be dismissed, removed, or reduced in rank by an authority subordinate to the appointing authority. Ordinarily, the person must receive charges and a reasonable opportunity to defend them. Inquiry may be dispensed with in stated constitutional situations.



Simple: Government employees have procedural protection before serious disciplinary punishment, though exceptions exist for conviction, impracticable inquiry, or State-security reasons.



Related: Articles 309, 310, 311.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 312 — All-India services

Type: Public Services



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Public Services; Union-State Relations



Law: If the Rajya Sabha passes a resolution supported by at least two-thirds of members present and voting that it is necessary in national interest, Parliament may create one or more All-India Services common to the Union and States, including an All-India Judicial Service.



Simple: Parliament may create services such as IAS or IPS that serve both Union and State governments, after special Rajya Sabha approval.



Related: Articles 249, 309, 312, 312A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 312A — Power of Parliament to vary or revoke conditions of service of officers of certain services

Type: Public Services; Transitional Provision



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Public Services; Historical Provisions



Law: Parliament may vary or revoke service conditions, including pension and disciplinary rights, of specified officers appointed to pre-Constitution Crown services. The Article protects certain High Court and Supreme Court jurisdiction limits in this narrow historical context.



Simple: This Article deals with service conditions of certain officers from the pre-Constitution period.



Related: Articles 309–314; Constitution (Twenty-eighth Amendment) Act, 1972.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 313 — Transitional provisions

Type: Public Services; Transitional Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Public Services; Historical Provisions



Law: Existing public-service laws continued after Constitution commencement, subject to other constitutional provisions and later legislation.



Simple: Older service rules could continue until properly changed under the Constitution.



Related: Articles 309, 310, 313, 372.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 314 — Provision for protection of existing officers of certain services

Type: Historical Public Service Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Public Services; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 314 was omitted by the Constitution (Twenty-eighth Amendment) Act, 1972.



Simple: This former protection for certain pre-Constitution service officers is no longer active.



Related: Articles 312A, 313, 314.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 315 — Public Service Commissions for the Union and for the States

Type: Constitutional Body; Public Services



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; UPSC; State PSC; Constitutional Bodies



Law: There shall be a Union Public Service Commission for the Union and a State Public Service Commission for each State. Two or more States may have a Joint State Public Service Commission by parliamentary law.



Simple: UPSC recruits or advises for Union public services; State PSCs do so for State services. Some States may share a Joint PSC.



Related: Articles 315–323.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 316 — Appointment and term of office of members

Type: Constitutional Body; Public Services



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; UPSC; State PSC



Law: UPSC, State PSC, and Joint PSC members are appointed by the President or Governor as applicable. At least half must have held government office for at least ten years. Members serve six years or until age 65 for UPSC/Joint PSC and 62 for State PSC.



Simple: PSC members have fixed constitutional service terms and age limits to support independent recruitment bodies.



Related: Articles 315, 316, 317, 319.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 317 — Removal and suspension of a member of a Public Service Commission

Type: Constitutional Body; Public Services



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; UPSC; State PSC



Law: The President may remove PSC members for misbehaviour only after a Supreme Court inquiry and report. The President may suspend a member during inquiry and may remove members for stated grounds including insolvency, paid outside employment, or infirmity.



Simple: PSC members have strong removal protection. Serious misconduct normally requires a Supreme Court inquiry.



Related: Articles 315, 316, 317, 318.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 318 — Power to make regulations as to conditions of service of members and staff

Type: Constitutional Body; Public Services



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; UPSC; State PSC



Law: The President or Governor may make regulations concerning the number of PSC members, staff-service conditions, and member-service conditions, subject to constitutional limits.



Simple: The President or Governor may make rules about PSC staffing and members’ service conditions.



Related: Articles 315–319.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 319 — Prohibition as to holding offices by members of Commission on ceasing to be such members

Type: Constitutional Body; Public Services



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; UPSC; State PSC



Law: Former PSC members face constitutional restrictions on later government employment. The Chairman of UPSC may become Chairman of a State PSC, but not take other Union or State government employment; other members have specified limited eligibility.



Simple: PSC members cannot freely move into government jobs after leaving office, helping protect their independence.



Related: Articles 315–319.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 320 — Functions of Public Service Commissions

Type: Constitutional Body; Public Services



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; UPSC; State PSC



Law: UPSC and State PSCs conduct examinations for public-service appointments and advise on recruitment, appointments, promotions, transfers, disciplinary matters, claims for legal-cost reimbursement, and injury pensions, subject to regulations excluding specified matters.



Simple: PSCs conduct recruitment examinations and advise governments on important service and disciplinary matters.



Related: Articles 309, 315, 318, 320, 321.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 321 — Power to extend functions of Public Service Commissions

Type: Constitutional Body; Public Services



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; UPSC; State PSC



Law: Parliament or a State Legislature may extend PSC functions to services of local authorities, other bodies corporate, or public institutions.



Simple: Laws can give UPSC or a State PSC recruitment or advisory work for certain public bodies beyond ordinary government departments.



Related: Articles 315, 320, 321.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 322 — Expenses of Public Service Commissions

Type: Constitutional Body; Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; UPSC; State PSC; Finance



Law: PSC expenses, including salaries, allowances, and pensions of members and staff, are charged on the Consolidated Fund of India or the relevant State.



Simple: PSC core expenses are paid from the main government fund, supporting financial independence.



Related: Articles 266, 315, 322.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 323 — Reports of Public Service Commissions

Type: Constitutional Body; Public Accountability



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Public Services; UPSC; State PSC; Parliament; State Government



Law: UPSC submits annual reports to the President and State PSCs to Governors. The reports, with memoranda explaining cases where advice was not accepted, must be laid before Parliament or the State Legislature.



Simple: PSCs must report yearly, and governments must disclose to legislatures when PSC advice was not followed.



Related: Articles 315, 320, 322, 323.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India


Article 323A — Administrative tribunals

Type: Tribunals



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Tribunals; Public Services; Judiciary



Law: Parliament may establish administrative tribunals for disputes concerning recruitment and conditions of service of persons in Union, State, local-authority, or government-controlled public services. Parliament may define jurisdiction, procedure, remedies, and related matters by law.



Simple: Parliament can create special tribunals for disputes involving government jobs, recruitment, promotions, and service conditions.



Related: Articles 309–311, 323A, 323B; Administrative Tribunals Act, 1985.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 323B — Tribunals for other matters

Type: Tribunals



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Tribunals; Judiciary



Law: Parliament or a State Legislature may establish tribunals for specified matters within their legislative competence, including taxation, foreign exchange, industrial disputes, land reforms, urban property ceilings, elections, food matters, rent regulation, and offences connected with these matters.



Simple: Union and State laws can create specialised tribunals for subjects such as taxes, land, elections, labour disputes, and rent matters.



Related: Articles 246, 323A, 323B; Seventh Schedule.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 324 — Superintendence, direction and control of elections to be vested in an Election Commission

Type: Constitutional Body; Elections



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Elections; Election Commission; Constitutional Bodies



Law: The Election Commission of India has superintendence, direction, and control over electoral rolls and elections to Parliament, State Legislatures, and the offices of President and Vice-President. It consists of the Chief Election Commissioner and other Election Commissioners as the President may determine.



Simple: The Election Commission is the constitutional body responsible for managing and supervising national and State elections covered by this Article.



Related: Articles 324–329, 325, 326, 327, 328; Representation of the People Acts, 1950 and 1951.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 325 — No person to be ineligible for inclusion in, or to claim inclusion in, a special electoral roll on grounds of religion, race, caste or sex

Type: Elections; Electoral Rolls



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Elections; Voting Rights; Equality



Law: There can be only one general electoral roll for every territorial constituency. No person may be excluded from it, or claim a separate special roll, only on grounds of religion, race, caste, sex, or any of them.



Simple: Voter lists cannot separate or exclude people merely because of religion, caste, race, or sex.



Related: Articles 14, 15, 325, 326, 327.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 326 — Elections to the House of the People and to the Legislative Assemblies of States to be on the basis of adult suffrage

Type: Elections; Voting Rights



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Elections; Voting Rights



Law: Lok Sabha and State Legislative Assembly elections are based on adult suffrage. An Indian citizen aged 18 or above is entitled to be registered as a voter unless disqualified by law for non-residence, unsoundness of mind, crime, corrupt or illegal practice, or another lawful ground.



Simple: Indian citizens aged 18 or above can generally vote if they are registered and not legally disqualified.



Related: Articles 324, 325, 326, 327; Representation of the People Act, 1950.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 327 — Power of Parliament to make provision with respect to elections to Legislatures

Type: Elections; Legislative Power



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Elections; Parliament



Law: Parliament may make laws concerning elections to Parliament and State Legislatures, including electoral rolls, constituency delimitation, and all matters necessary to ensure proper constitution of those Legislatures.



Simple: Parliament makes major election laws for Lok Sabha, Rajya Sabha, State Assemblies, and Legislative Councils.



Related: Articles 324, 326–329; Representation of the People Acts, 1950 and 1951.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 328 — Power of Legislature of a State to make provision with respect to elections to such Legislature

Type: Elections; State Legislative Power



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Elections; State Government



Law: Subject to the Constitution and parliamentary law, a State Legislature may make laws concerning elections to its own Legislature, including electoral rolls and related election matters.



Simple: States may make some rules for their own Legislature’s elections, but Parliament’s election laws and the Constitution prevail.



Related: Articles 324, 327, 328, 329.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 329 — Bar to interference by courts in electoral matters

Type: Elections; Legal Procedure



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Elections; Legal Procedure



Law: Courts cannot question constituency-delimitation or seat-allocation laws made under Articles 327 or 328. An election to Parliament or a State Legislature may be challenged only through an election petition before the authority and procedure provided by parliamentary law.



Simple: Election disputes must normally use the special election-petition process rather than ordinary court cases.



Related: Articles 324, 327–329; Representation of the People Act, 1951.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 329A — Special provision as to elections to Parliament in the case of Prime Minister and Speaker

Type: Historical Election Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Elections; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 329A was omitted by the Constitution (Forty-fourth Amendment) Act, 1978.



Simple: This Article is not current law and should only be retained as historical constitutional context.



Related: Articles 324–329; Constitution (Forty-fourth Amendment) Act, 1978.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 330 — Reservation of seats for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes in the House of the People

Type: Reservation; Parliamentary Representation



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Elections; Reservation; Scheduled Castes; Scheduled Tribes



Law: Seats are reserved in the Lok Sabha for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes, including special provisions for Scheduled Tribes in Assam’s autonomous districts. Reservation is broadly linked to population and constitutional delimitation arrangements.



Simple: Some Lok Sabha seats are reserved so SC and ST communities have elected representation in Parliament.



Related: Articles 330, 330A, 332, 334, 341, 342; Delimitation provisions.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 330A — Reservation of seats for women in the House of the People

Type: Reservation; Women’s Representation



Status: Inserted; not yet operational



Category: Constitution; Elections; Reservation; Women \& Representation



Law: As nearly as possible, one-third of Lok Sabha seats, including one-third of SC/ST reserved seats, must be reserved for women. Under Article 334A, this takes effect only after delimitation is undertaken following publication of figures from the first census taken after commencement of the Constitution (One Hundred and Sixth Amendment) Act, 2023.



Simple: The Constitution provides for one-third reservation for women in the Lok Sabha, including within SC/ST reserved seats, but it begins only after the required future census-based delimitation process.



Related: Articles 330, 330A, 334A; Constitution (One Hundred and Sixth Amendment) Act, 2023.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 331 — Representation of the Anglo-Indian community in the House of the People

Type: Historical Representation Provision



Status: Historical / expired in operation



Category: Constitution; Elections; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 331 provided for Presidential nomination of up to two Anglo-Indians to the Lok Sabha if the community was not adequately represented. Its operation ceased under Article 334 after the applicable 70-year period ended in 2020.



Simple: Anglo-Indian nomination to the Lok Sabha is no longer operational.



Related: Articles 330, 331, 334.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 332 — Reservation of seats for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes in the Legislative Assemblies of the States

Type: Reservation; State Representation



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Elections; Reservation; Scheduled Castes; Scheduled Tribes; State Government



Law: Seats are reserved for SCs and STs in State Legislative Assemblies, generally in proportion to their population, with special provisions for tribal areas and autonomous districts in specified States.



Simple: Some MLA seats are reserved to ensure SC and ST representation in State Assemblies.



Related: Articles 332, 332A, 334, 341, 342; Fifth and Sixth Schedules.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 332A — Reservation of seats for women in the Legislative Assemblies of the States

Type: Reservation; Women’s Representation



Status: Inserted; not yet operational



Category: Constitution; Elections; Reservation; Women \& Representation; State Government



Law: As nearly as possible, one-third of State Assembly seats, including one-third of SC/ST reserved seats, must be reserved for women. Under Article 334A, this begins only after the required census-based delimitation exercise.



Simple: One-third of MLA seats are constitutionally reserved for women, including within SC/ST reserved seats, but the reservation has not begun until the required future delimitation process occurs.



Related: Articles 332, 332A, 334A; Constitution (One Hundred and Sixth Amendment) Act, 2023.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 333 — Representation of the Anglo-Indian community in the Legislative Assemblies of the States

Type: Historical Representation Provision



Status: Historical / expired in operation



Category: Constitution; Elections; Historical Provisions; State Government



Law: Article 333 provided for Governor nomination of one Anglo-Indian to a State Assembly if the community was not adequately represented. Its operation ended under Article 334 after the applicable 70-year period ended in 2020.



Simple: Anglo-Indian nomination to State Assemblies is no longer operational.



Related: Articles 332, 333, 334.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 334 — Reservation of seats and special representation to cease after certain period

Type: Reservation; Transitional Provision



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Elections; Reservation; Scheduled Castes; Scheduled Tribes



Law: SC/ST seat reservation in Lok Sabha and State Assemblies continues for 80 years from Constitution commencement, currently until 25 January 2030 unless constitutionally extended. Anglo-Indian special representation ceased after 70 years, in 2020.



Simple: SC/ST legislative-seat reservation currently continues until 2030 unless the Constitution is amended again. Anglo-Indian nomination ended in 2020.



Related: Articles 330–333, 334A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 334A — Taking effect of provisions of the Constitution (One Hundred and Sixth Amendment) Act, 2023

Type: Reservation; Women’s Representation



Status: Inserted; commencement mechanism pending



Category: Constitution; Elections; Reservation; Women \& Representation



Law: Women’s reservations under Articles 330A, 332A, and the corresponding Delhi Assembly provision take effect after a delimitation exercise undertaken after publication of figures of the first census taken after commencement of the 106th Amendment. They continue for 15 years from that commencement and may continue for a longer period if Parliament determines. Reserved seats rotate after each later delimitation.



Simple: Women’s legislative reservation is constitutionally approved but starts only after a future census and delimitation process. Once started, it lasts 15 years unless Parliament extends it.



Related: Articles 239AA, 330A, 332A, 334A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 335 — Claims of Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes to services and posts

Type: Reservation; Public Services



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Equality; Reservation; Scheduled Castes; Scheduled Tribes; Public Services



Law: SC/ST claims must be considered consistently with maintaining efficiency of administration in Union and State services. The Article permits relaxation in qualifying marks or standards for SC/ST reservation in promotion, as provided by law.



Simple: SC/ST representation in government jobs must be considered, while public administration must remain effective.



Related: Articles 16(4), 16(4A), 16(4B), 335, 338, 338A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 336 — Special provision for Anglo-Indian community in certain services

Type: Historical Public Service Provision



Status: Historical / spent transitional provision



Category: Constitution; Public Services; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 336 allowed temporary Anglo-Indian representation in specified railway, customs, postal, and telegraph services for a limited period from Constitution commencement.



Simple: This was a temporary post-Constitution service arrangement and has no current ordinary effect.



Related: Articles 334, 336, 337.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 337 — Special provision with respect to educational grants for benefit of Anglo-Indian community

Type: Historical Education Provision



Status: Historical / spent transitional provision



Category: Constitution; Education; Historical Provisions



Law: Article 337 provided temporary grants for Anglo-Indian educational institutions for a limited period after Constitution commencement, subject to student-admission conditions.



Simple: This was a temporary educational-grant arrangement and no longer has ordinary current effect.



Related: Articles 29, 30, 336, 337.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 338 — National Commission for Scheduled Castes

Type: Constitutional Commission



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Scheduled Castes; Constitutional Commissions; Social Justice



Law: The National Commission for Scheduled Castes investigates and monitors constitutional and legal safeguards for SCs, inquires into complaints, participates in planning, reports to the President, and may make recommendations. It has civil-court powers while investigating or inquiring.



Simple: The NCSC is a constitutional body that examines whether SC safeguards are working and can investigate complaints about their violation.



Related: Articles 17, 46, 338, 338A, 341.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 338A — National Commission for Scheduled Tribes

Type: Constitutional Commission



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Scheduled Tribes; Constitutional Commissions; Tribal Areas



Law: The National Commission for Scheduled Tribes monitors safeguards for STs, investigates complaints, advises on socio-economic development planning, reports to the President, and has civil-court powers for inquiries and investigations.



Simple: The NCST is a constitutional body that protects and monitors safeguards for Scheduled Tribes.



Related: Articles 46, 244, 338A, 339, 342; Fifth and Sixth Schedules.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 338B — National Commission for Backward Classes

Type: Constitutional Commission



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Backward Classes; Constitutional Commissions; Social Justice



Law: The National Commission for Backward Classes investigates and monitors constitutional and legal safeguards for socially and educationally backward classes, inquires into complaints, advises governments, reports to the President, and has civil-court powers for inquiries.



Simple: The NCBC is a constitutional body that examines safeguards and complaints relating to socially and educationally backward classes.



Related: Articles 15(4), 16(4), 338B, 340, 342A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 339 — Control of the Union over the administration of Scheduled Areas and welfare of Scheduled Tribes

Type: Scheduled Areas; Tribal Welfare



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Scheduled Areas; Tribal Areas; Union-State Relations



Law: The President may appoint a Commission to report on administration of Scheduled Areas and ST welfare. The Union may give directions to States concerning preparation and execution of schemes essential for ST welfare.



Simple: The Union can review tribal welfare and direct States on important ST welfare schemes.



Related: Articles 244, 275, 338A, 339, 342; Fifth Schedule.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 340 — Appointment of a Commission to investigate conditions of backward classes

Type: Backward Classes; Constitutional Commission



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Backward Classes; Social Justice



Law: The President may appoint a Commission to investigate the conditions and difficulties of socially and educationally backward classes and recommend measures for their advancement. The report is laid before Parliament with an action memorandum.



Simple: The President can appoint a commission to study backward-class conditions and recommend support measures.



Related: Articles 15(4), 16(4), 338B, 340, 342A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 341 — Scheduled Castes

Type: Scheduled Castes; Identification



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Scheduled Castes; Social Justice



Law: The President may notify which castes, races, tribes, or parts or groups are Scheduled Castes for a State or Union Territory, after consulting the Governor where a State is concerned. Parliament alone may later include or exclude groups from the notified list.



Simple: A person is treated as SC for constitutional purposes only if their community appears in the applicable official SC list for that State or Union Territory.



Related: Articles 15, 16, 17, 330, 332, 338, 341.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 342 — Scheduled Tribes

Type: Scheduled Tribes; Identification



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Scheduled Tribes; Tribal Areas; Social Justice



Law: The President may notify tribes, tribal communities, or parts or groups as Scheduled Tribes for a State or Union Territory, after consulting the Governor where a State is concerned. Parliament alone may later include or exclude groups from the notified list.



Simple: A person is treated as ST for constitutional purposes only if their community appears in the official ST list applicable to that State or Union Territory.



Related: Articles 46, 244, 330, 332, 338A, 342; Fifth and Sixth Schedules.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 342A — Socially and educationally backward classes

Type: Backward Classes; Identification



Status: Active; inserted and amended



Category: Constitution; Backward Classes; Reservation; Social Justice



Law: The President may specify socially and educationally backward classes for the Central List, after consultation with the Governor where a State is concerned. Parliament may include or exclude groups from the Central List. States and Union Territories may prepare and maintain their own SEBC lists for their own purposes, subject to constitutional and applicable legal limits.



Simple: The Union has a Central List of socially and educationally backward classes, while States and Union Territories may also maintain their own lists for State purposes.



Related: Articles 15(4), 16(4), 338B, 340, 342A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India


Article 343 — Official language of the Union

Type: Official Language



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Official Language



Law: Hindi in Devanagari script is the official language of the Union. English continues for official Union purposes as Parliament provides. The Constitution does not declare any language as India’s “national language.”



Simple: Hindi and English are used for Union Government official work. India has no constitutionally declared national language.



Related: Articles 344–351; Eighth Schedule.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 344 — Commission and Committee of Parliament on official language

Type: Official Language; Constitutional Commission



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Official Language; Parliament



Law: The President must constitute an Official Language Commission at the stated constitutional intervals. A Parliamentary Committee examines its recommendations on progressive Hindi use, English use, and language-related matters.



Simple: The Constitution provides bodies to advise on how official languages should be used by the Union.



Related: Articles 343, 344, 351.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 345 — Official language or languages of a State

Type: Official Language



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Official Language; State Government



Law: A State Legislature may adopt one or more languages used in the State, or Hindi, as official State language(s). English may continue for official purposes until State law provides otherwise.



Simple: States may choose their own official language or languages. State official languages can differ from Union official languages.



Related: Articles 343, 345–347, 350.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 346 — Official language for communication between one State and another or between a State and the Union

Type: Official Language



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Official Language; Union-State Relations



Law: The authorised Union official language is generally used for communication between States and between a State and the Union. Two or more States may agree to use Hindi for communication.



Simple: States and the Union use constitutionally authorised official languages when communicating with one another.



Related: Articles 343, 345, 346.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 347 — Special provision relating to language spoken by a section of the population of a State

Type: Linguistic Protection



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Official Language; Linguistic Rights



Law: If a substantial proportion of a State’s population demands recognition of a language, the President may direct that language be officially recognised throughout the State or a specified part for purposes stated in the direction.



Simple: A language spoken by many people in a State may receive special official recognition through a Presidential direction.



Related: Articles 29, 30, 345, 347, 350A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 348 — Language to be used in the Supreme Court and in the High Courts and for Acts, Bills, etc.

Type: Official Language; Judiciary



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Official Language; Judiciary



Law: Until Parliament provides otherwise, English is used in Supreme Court and High Court proceedings, authoritative texts of Bills, Acts, Ordinances, rules, regulations, and bye-laws. A Governor may authorise Hindi or another State official language in High Court proceedings with Presidential consent, but judgments must remain in English unless Parliament provides otherwise.



Simple: English is generally the authoritative language for higher courts and legislation, though limited State-language use may be authorised.



Related: Articles 343, 345, 348, 349.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 349 — Special procedure for enactment of certain laws relating to language

Type: Official Language; Legislative Procedure



Status: Active; spent transitional provision



Category: Constitution; Official Language; Parliament



Law: For 15 years after Constitution commencement, certain language Bills required Presidential sanction before introduction and consideration of the Article 344 Commission and Committee recommendations.



Simple: This was a temporary safeguard for language-law changes during the Constitution’s early years.



Related: Articles 343, 344, 348, 349.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 350 — Language to be used in representations for redress of grievances

Type: Linguistic Protection



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Official Language; Linguistic Rights



Law: Every person may submit a grievance representation to any Union or State authority in any language used in the Union or State.



Simple: You may submit a complaint or request to government in a language used in the Union or your State.



Related: Articles 19, 345, 350, 350A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 350A — Facilities for instruction in mother-tongue at primary stage

Type: Linguistic Protection; Education



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Education; Linguistic Rights



Law: Every State and local authority must endeavour to provide adequate facilities for mother-tongue instruction at the primary stage to children belonging to linguistic minority groups. The President may issue directions to ensure this.



Simple: Governments should try to help linguistic-minority children receive primary education in their mother tongue.



Related: Articles 21A, 29, 30, 350A, 350B.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 350B — Special Officer for linguistic minorities

Type: Constitutional Office; Linguistic Protection



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Linguistic Rights; Constitutional Bodies



Law: The President appoints a Special Officer for linguistic minorities to investigate safeguards for linguistic minorities and report to the President. Reports are laid before Parliament and sent to concerned State Governments.



Simple: A constitutional officer monitors whether language-minority protections are being followed.



Related: Articles 29, 30, 350A, 350B.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 351 — Directive for development of the Hindi language

Type: Directive; Official Language



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Official Language



Law: The Union must promote development of Hindi so it can serve as a medium for India’s composite culture, drawing primarily from Hindustani and other Indian languages while preserving its character.



Simple: The Union should support Hindi’s development while drawing from India’s diverse languages.



Related: Articles 343, 344, 351; Eighth Schedule.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 352 — Proclamation of Emergency

Type: National Emergency



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Emergency; National Emergency



Law: The President may proclaim Emergency if security of India or any part is threatened by war, external aggression, or armed rebellion. Written Union Cabinet advice is required. Both Houses must approve within one month; approval lasts six months and requires renewal. Lok Sabha may require revocation.



Simple: A National Emergency can be declared only for war, external aggression, or armed rebellion, with Cabinet advice and Parliamentary approval.



Related: Articles 353–359, 83, 172.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 353 — Effect of Proclamation of Emergency

Type: National Emergency



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Emergency; National Emergency; Union-State Relations



Law: During a National Emergency, Union executive power extends to directing States, and Parliament may legislate on State List matters. Such laws may continue for six months after Emergency ends unless earlier repealed.



Simple: During National Emergency, the Union receives wider powers over State matters.



Related: Articles 246, 250, 352, 353, 354.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 354 — Application of provisions relating to distribution of revenues while a Proclamation of Emergency is in operation

Type: National Emergency; Public Finance



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Emergency; Finance; Union-State Relations



Law: During a National Emergency, the President may modify the constitutional distribution of Union-State revenues by order, subject to parliamentary laying and time limits.



Simple: During National Emergency, revenue-sharing rules between the Union and States may be temporarily changed.



Related: Articles 268–281, 352–354.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 355 — Duty of the Union to protect States against external aggression and internal disturbance

Type: Union-State Relations



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Emergency; Union-State Relations



Law: The Union must protect every State against external aggression and internal disturbance and ensure that State government is carried on according to the Constitution.



Simple: The Union has a constitutional duty to protect States and help ensure constitutional government continues.



Related: Articles 352, 355, 356.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 356 — Provisions in case of failure of constitutional machinery in States

Type: President’s Rule



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Emergency; President's Rule; State Government



Law: If the President is satisfied, on Governor’s report or otherwise, that State government cannot be carried on according to the Constitution, the President may assume State functions, declare State Legislature powers exercisable by Parliament, and make consequential provisions. Both Houses must approve within two months. It normally lasts six months at a time, subject to constitutional maximums and conditions.



Simple: President’s Rule may be imposed when a State government cannot function constitutionally. Parliament must approve it.



Related: Articles 355–357, 365, 172, 174.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 357 — Exercise of legislative powers under Proclamation issued under Article 356

Type: President’s Rule; Legislative Power



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Emergency; President's Rule; Parliament



Law: During Article 356, Parliament may confer State legislative powers on the President or another authority. Laws and expenditure authorisations made during President’s Rule are subject to constitutional limits and post-Proclamation review.



Simple: During President’s Rule, Parliament or authorities it authorises can make laws for the State.



Related: Articles 356, 357, 213.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 358 — Suspension of provisions of Article 19 during emergencies

Type: National Emergency; Fundamental Rights



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Emergency; Freedom; Fundamental Rights



Law: Article 19 is automatically suspended only when a National Emergency is declared on grounds of war or external aggression, not armed rebellion. Laws and executive action must be connected with the Emergency, and post-Emergency protection applies as stated in the Article.



Simple: Article 19 freedoms are not suspended in every Emergency. This happens only for Emergency based on war or external aggression.



Related: Articles 19, 352, 358, 359.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 359 — Suspension of the enforcement of the rights conferred by Part III during emergencies

Type: National Emergency; Fundamental Rights



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Emergency; Fundamental Rights; Constitutional Remedies



Law: During National Emergency, the President may order suspension of the right to move courts for enforcement of specified Part III rights. Articles 20 and 21 cannot be included. The order may apply to all or part of India and must be laid before Parliament.



Simple: During National Emergency, court enforcement of certain Fundamental Rights may be temporarily suspended, but protections under Articles 20 and 21 remain enforceable.



Related: Articles 20, 21, 32, 352, 358, 359.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 359A — Application of this Part to the State of Punjab

Type: Historical Emergency Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Emergency; Historical Constitutional Provisions



Law: Article 359A was a special emergency provision concerning Punjab and was later omitted.



Simple: This Article is not current law and is retained only for historical context.



Related: Articles 352, 359; Constitution (Sixty-third Amendment) Act, 1989.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 360 — Provisions as to financial emergency

Type: Financial Emergency



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Emergency; Financial Emergency; Finance



Law: The President may proclaim Financial Emergency if India’s financial stability or credit is threatened. Both Houses must approve within two months. The President may issue directions on financial propriety, reduce salaries and allowances, including of constitutional office-holders, and require State financial Bills to be reserved.



Simple: Financial Emergency is for a serious threat to India’s financial stability. Parliament must approve it.



Related: Articles 112, 202, 266, 360.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 361 — Protection of President and Governors and Rajpramukhs

Type: Miscellaneous Constitutional Protection



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Miscellaneous; President; Governor



Law: The President and Governors are not answerable to courts for exercise of official powers, subject to constitutional impeachment or removal processes. No criminal proceedings may begin or continue during their term, and no arrest or imprisonment process may issue. Civil proceedings require two months’ prior notice.



Simple: The President and Governors have special legal protections while in office, but constitutional accountability procedures still apply.



Related: Articles 61, 156, 361, 361A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 361A — Protection of publication of proceedings of Parliament and State Legislatures

Type: Miscellaneous Constitutional Protection



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Miscellaneous; Parliament; State Legislature



Law: No person is liable in civil or criminal proceedings for substantially true publication of Parliament or State Legislature proceedings, unless publication is proved malicious. This protection does not apply to secret sittings.



Simple: Fair and substantially accurate reporting of legislative proceedings is constitutionally protected, except for secret sittings or malicious publication.



Related: Articles 105, 194, 361A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 361B — Disqualification for appointment on remunerative political post

Type: Anti-Defection Provision



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Elections; State Government; Parliament



Law: A member disqualified under the Tenth Schedule is disqualified from holding any remunerative political post from the date of disqualification until the stated constitutional endpoint.



Simple: A legislator disqualified for defection cannot immediately receive a paid political post.



Related: Articles 102, 191, 361B; Tenth Schedule.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 362 — Rights and privileges of Rulers of Indian States

Type: Historical Constitutional Provision



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Historical Constitutional Provisions



Law: Article 362 was omitted by the Constitution (Twenty-sixth Amendment) Act, 1971.



Simple: This former provision concerning Rulers of Indian States is no longer active.



Related: Articles 291 omitted, 363A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 363 — Bar to interference by courts in disputes arising out of certain treaties, agreements, etc.

Type: Miscellaneous; Jurisdiction Bar



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Miscellaneous; Judiciary



Law: Courts cannot interfere in disputes arising from specified pre-Constitution treaties, agreements, covenants, engagements, sanads, or similar instruments involving Rulers of Indian States, unless Parliament provides otherwise.



Simple: Certain old princely-State treaty disputes cannot normally be decided by courts.



Related: Articles 131, 363, 363A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 363A — Recognition granted to Rulers of Indian States to cease and privy purses to be abolished

Type: Historical Constitutional Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Historical Constitutional Provisions



Law: Recognition of Rulers of Indian States ceased, and privy purses, privileges, and rights connected with such recognition were abolished.



Simple: Former princely rulers no longer have constitutional recognition or privy-purse entitlements.



Related: Articles 291 omitted, 362 omitted, 363A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 364 — Special provisions as to major ports and aerodromes

Type: Miscellaneous; Union-State Relations



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Miscellaneous; Union-State Relations



Law: The President may direct that laws made by a State Legislature do not apply, or apply with modifications, to major ports and aerodromes.



Simple: The President can limit how certain State laws apply to major ports and airports.



Related: Articles 246, 364; Union List.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 365 — Effect of failure to comply with, or to give effect to, directions given by the Union

Type: Union-State Relations; Emergency



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Union-State Relations; Emergency; State Government



Law: If a State fails to comply with Union directions under the Constitution, the President may treat this as a situation in which State government cannot be carried on according to the Constitution.



Simple: Ignoring valid Union constitutional directions can contribute to action under Article 356.



Related: Articles 256, 257, 355, 356, 365.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 366 — Definitions

Type: Constitutional Definitions



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Miscellaneous



Law: Defines numerous constitutional terms, including agricultural income, Anglo-Indian, borrowed money, corporation tax, goods and services tax, Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes, and other terms used across the Constitution.



Simple: This is the Constitution’s main dictionary for many technical terms.



Related: Articles 1, 246A, 366, 367; Schedules.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 367 — Interpretation

Type: Constitutional Interpretation



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Miscellaneous



Law: The General Clauses Act, 1897 applies to Constitution interpretation, subject to adaptations. The Article also provides interpretation rules for references to Rulers and for Jammu and Kashmir-related constitutional references.



Simple: This Article explains how certain constitutional words and references should be legally understood.



Related: Articles 366, 367, 370; General Clauses Act, 1897.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 368 — Power of Parliament to amend the Constitution and procedure therefor

Type: Constitutional Amendment



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Constitutional Amendments; Parliament



Law: Parliament may amend the Constitution through a Bill introduced in either House. The Bill requires a majority of total membership and at least two-thirds of members present and voting in each House. Specified federal provisions also require ratification by at least half of State Legislatures before Presidential assent. Judicial interpretation: the Supreme Court’s Basic Structure doctrine limits Parliament from destroying the Constitution’s basic structure.



Simple: Parliament can amend the Constitution using a special majority. Some changes also need approval from at least half of State Legislatures. Courts have held that Parliament cannot destroy the Constitution’s basic structure.



Related: Articles 13, 32, 131, 136, 226, 368; Supreme Court basic-structure jurisprudence.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 369 — Temporary power to Parliament to make laws with respect to certain matters in the State List

Type: Transitional Legislative Provision



Status: Spent transitional provision



Category: Constitution; Parliament; Historical Constitutional Provisions



Law: For five years from Constitution commencement, Parliament could legislate on specified State List trade and commerce matters.



Simple: This was a temporary early-Constitution power and no longer has ordinary current effect.



Related: Articles 246, 301–307, 369.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 370 — Temporary provisions with respect to Jammu and Kashmir

Type: Special State Provision; Jammu \& Kashmir



Status: Active in text; special operation ceased by Presidential action upheld judicially



Category: Constitution; Jammu \& Kashmir; Special State Provisions



Law: Article 370 contained special constitutional arrangements for Jammu and Kashmir. In 2019, Presidential Orders applied the Constitution broadly to Jammu and Kashmir and declared Article 370 provisions inoperative except as specified. The Supreme Court upheld the constitutional validity of the 2019 measures in 2023.



Simple: Jammu and Kashmir’s former special constitutional arrangement is no longer operational in its earlier form. The Constitution now applies there under the post-2019 framework.



Related: Articles 1, 3, 367, 370, 371; Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court Article 370 judgment, 11 December 2023.



Article 371 — Special provision with respect to the States of Maharashtra and Gujarat

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; State Government



Law: The President may provide for separate development boards for specified regions in Maharashtra and Gujarat and require equitable allocation of development funds, technical education facilities, and public-employment opportunities.



Simple: Maharashtra and Gujarat may have special regional development arrangements to reduce regional inequality.



Related: Articles 38, 371, 371A–371J.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 371A — Special provision with respect to the State of Nagaland

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; Nagaland; Tribal Areas



Law: Parliamentary laws on Naga religious or social practices, customary law and procedure, civil/criminal justice involving customary law, and ownership or transfer of land and resources do not apply to Nagaland unless its Assembly resolves otherwise. The Governor has specified law-and-order responsibilities.



Simple: Nagaland has special constitutional protection for Naga customs, customary justice, and land and resource matters.



Related: Articles 244, 371A; Sixth Schedule.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 371B — Special provision with respect to the State of Assam

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; Assam; Tribal Areas



Law: The President may provide for an Assembly committee consisting of members elected from Assam’s tribal areas specified in the Sixth Schedule and other members as specified.



Simple: Assam may have a special Assembly committee to represent and consider tribal-area interests.



Related: Articles 244, 371B; Sixth Schedule.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 371C — Special provision with respect to the State of Manipur

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; Manipur; Tribal Areas



Law: The President may provide for a Hill Areas Committee in the Manipur Legislative Assembly. The Governor has special responsibility to report to the President on administration of Hill Areas.



Simple: Manipur has a special constitutional arrangement for representation and administration of its Hill Areas.



Related: Articles 371C, 371; Manipur Hill Areas provisions.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 371D — Special provisions with respect to the State of Andhra Pradesh or the State of Telangana

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; Andhra Pradesh; Telangana; Public Services



Law: The President may provide for equitable opportunities and facilities in public employment and education for people from different parts of Andhra Pradesh or Telangana, including local-area arrangements and administrative tribunals as provided by law.



Simple: Andhra Pradesh and Telangana may have special arrangements to ensure fair education and government-job opportunities across regions.



Related: Articles 16, 371D, 371E.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 371E — Establishment of Central University in Andhra Pradesh

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; Andhra Pradesh; Education



Law: Parliament may establish a Central University in Andhra Pradesh by law.



Simple: Parliament may create a Central University in Andhra Pradesh.



Related: Articles 246, 371D, 371E.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 371F — Special provisions with respect to the State of Sikkim

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; Sikkim



Law: Provides special arrangements for Sikkim, including Assembly composition, representation, continuation of existing laws, Governor responsibilities, and protection of different sections of Sikkim’s population.



Simple: Sikkim has special constitutional provisions reflecting its accession and distinctive social and political conditions.



Related: Articles 1, 371F, 372.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 371G — Special provision with respect to the State of Mizoram

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; Mizoram; Tribal Areas



Law: Parliamentary laws on Mizo religious or social practices, customary law and procedure, civil/criminal justice involving customary law, and ownership or transfer of land do not apply to Mizoram unless its Assembly resolves otherwise. The Assembly must have at least 40 members.



Simple: Mizoram has special constitutional protection for Mizo customs, customary justice, and land matters.



Related: Articles 244, 371G; Sixth Schedule.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 371H — Special provision with respect to the State of Arunachal Pradesh

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; Arunachal Pradesh



Law: The Governor of Arunachal Pradesh has special responsibility for law and order, acting after consulting the Council of Ministers. The State Assembly must have at least 30 members.



Simple: Arunachal Pradesh’s Governor has a special constitutional role concerning law and order.



Related: Articles 163, 371H.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 371I — Special provision with respect to the State of Goa

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; Goa



Law: Goa’s Legislative Assembly must consist of not fewer than 30 members.



Simple: Goa must have at least 30 MLAs in its Legislative Assembly.



Related: Articles 170, 371I.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 371J — Special provisions with respect to the State of Karnataka

Type: Special State Provision



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Special State Provisions; Karnataka; Public Services; Education



Law: The President may establish a development board for Karnataka’s Hyderabad-Karnataka region and provide equitable fund allocation, education facilities, and reservation in public employment and education for persons belonging to that region, as law provides.



Simple: Karnataka may have special development, education, and employment arrangements for the Hyderabad-Karnataka region.



Related: Articles 16, 371J.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 372 — Continuance in force of existing laws and their adaptation

Type: Transitional Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Transitional Provisions



Law: Pre-Constitution laws continue unless altered, repealed, or amended by a competent authority, subject to the Constitution. The President could adapt such laws for constitutional conformity.



Simple: Older laws could continue after 1950 unless they conflicted with the Constitution or were later changed.



Related: Articles 13, 372, 372A, 395.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 372A — Power of the President to adapt laws

Type: Transitional Provision



Status: Active in text; spent transitional power



Category: Constitution; Transitional Provisions



Law: The President could adapt laws to facilitate constitutional transition within three years from the Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Simple: This was a temporary power to update laws after major constitutional restructuring.



Related: Articles 372, 372A; Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 373 — Power of President to make order in respect of persons under preventive detention in certain cases

Type: Transitional Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Transitional Provisions; Preventive Detention



Law: The President could make orders concerning persons under preventive detention immediately before Constitution commencement, subject to constitutional limits.



Simple: This Article addressed preventive-detention cases during the Constitution’s transition period.



Related: Articles 22, 373, 372.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 374 — Provisions as to Judges of the Federal Court and proceedings pending in the Federal Court or before His Majesty in Council

Type: Transitional Judiciary Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Historical Constitutional Provisions



Law: The Federal Court became the Supreme Court at Constitution commencement, and pending proceedings were transferred or dealt with under stated transitional rules.



Simple: This Article transferred the old Federal Court system into the new Supreme Court system.



Related: Articles 124, 135, 374.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 375 — Courts, authorities and officers to continue to function subject to the provisions of the Constitution

Type: Transitional Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Transitional Provisions; Government Administration



Law: Courts, authorities, and officers existing before Constitution commencement continued to function subject to the Constitution and competent-law changes.



Simple: Government offices and courts did not stop when the Constitution began; they continued under the new constitutional framework.



Related: Articles 372, 375.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 376 — Provisions as to Judges of High Courts

Type: Transitional Judiciary Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Judiciary; Historical Constitutional Provisions



Law: Judges of existing High Courts continued as High Court Judges under the Constitution, subject to constitutional provisions on tenure and service.



Simple: Existing High Court Judges continued after the Constitution came into force.



Related: Articles 214, 217, 221, 376.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 377 — Provisions as to Comptroller and Auditor-General of India

Type: Transitional Constitutional Body Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Finance; Constitutional Bodies



Law: The Auditor-General of India at Constitution commencement continued as the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India under the Constitution.



Simple: This Article continued the national audit office when the Constitution began.



Related: Articles 148–151, 377.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 378 — Provisions as to Public Service Commissions

Type: Transitional Constitutional Body Provision



Status: Active; transitional in character



Category: Constitution; Public Services; Constitutional Bodies



Law: Existing Public Service Commission members continued under the Constitution, subject to stated constitutional conditions.



Simple: Existing public-service recruitment commissions continued when the Constitution started.



Related: Articles 315–323, 378.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 378A — Special provision as to duration of Andhra Pradesh Legislative Assembly

Type: Historical Transitional Provision



Status: Historical / spent transitional provision



Category: Constitution; Historical Constitutional Provisions; State Government



Law: Article 378A temporarily extended the duration of the Andhra Pradesh Legislative Assembly under conditions stated in the Article.



Simple: This was a temporary historical arrangement and has no ordinary current effect.



Related: Articles 172, 378A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Articles 379–391 — Transitional provisions

Type: Historical Constitutional Provisions



Status: Omitted / Historical



Category: Constitution; Historical Constitutional Provisions



Law: Articles 379–391, concerning provisional Parliament, provisional State arrangements, transitional elections, pending Bills, expenditure, and temporary Presidential powers, were omitted by the Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Simple: These Articles governed the early transition to the Constitution and are no longer current law.



Related: Articles 372–378A, 392; Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 392 — Power of the President to remove difficulties

Type: Transitional Provision



Status: Active in text; spent transitional power



Category: Constitution; Transitional Provisions



Law: The President could make orders to remove constitutional-transition difficulties within three years of Constitution commencement.



Simple: This was a short-term power used to help the Constitution begin working in 1950.



Related: Articles 372, 392.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 393 — Short title

Type: Miscellaneous Constitutional Provision



Status: Active



Category: Constitution; Miscellaneous



Law: The Constitution is called the Constitution of India.



Simple: This is the Constitution’s official name.



Related: Articles 393–395.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 394 — Commencement

Type: Commencement Provision



Status: Active; historical operation complete



Category: Constitution; Historical Constitutional Provisions



Law: Specified Articles commenced immediately, while the remaining Constitution commenced on 26 January 1950.



Simple: Most of the Constitution became effective on 26 January 1950, celebrated as Republic Day.



Related: Articles 393–395.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 394A — Authoritative text in the Hindi language

Type: Official Language; Constitutional Text



Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Official Language



Law: The President must publish an authoritative Hindi translation of the Constitution and constitutional amendments. The Hindi text has the meaning stated by constitutional law and must conform to the authoritative English text where required.



Simple: The Constitution has an official Hindi text as well as its authoritative English text.



Related: Articles 343, 348, 394A.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Article 395 — Repeals

Type: Repeal Provision



Status: Active; historical operation complete



Category: Constitution; Historical Constitutional Provisions



Law: The Indian Independence Act, 1947 and the Government of India Act, 1935, along with amending or supplementing enactments, were repealed subject to the Constitution’s provisions.



Simple: The Constitution replaced the main British-era laws that previously governed India.



Related: Articles 372, 374, 395.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Schedules: Separate verification required — all current Schedules will be added in the dedicated LAWOUD Schedule knowledge-base batch.

First Schedule — The States and the Union Territories

Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; States \& Union Territories; Union-State Relations



Law: Lists India’s States and Union Territories and, for States, describes territorial composition by reference to districts, territories, and historical arrangements. It works with Articles 1–4, under which Parliament may alter State names, areas, boundaries, or create new States and Union Territories.



Simple: This Schedule is India’s constitutional list of States and Union Territories. It helps identify which areas belong to each State or Union Territory.



Related: Articles 1–4, 239, 239AA, 240.



Important Notes: The original Part A, Part B, Part C, and Part D classification was replaced by the Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956. State and Union Territory entries have changed through later constitutional and parliamentary reorganisation measures.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Second Schedule — Provisions as to the President and the Governors of States; the Speakers and the Deputy Speakers of the Houses of Parliament and of the Legislative Assemblies of States, and the Chairmen and the Deputy Chairmen of the Legislative Councils in States; the Judges of the Supreme Court and of the High Courts; and the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India

Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Constitutional Offices; Parliament; Judiciary; Finance



Law: Contains provisions concerning salaries, allowances, privileges, leave, pension, residence, and related service conditions of specified constitutional office-holders, including the President, Governors, legislative presiding officers, Supreme Court and High Court Judges, and the Comptroller and Auditor-General.



Simple: This Schedule sets constitutional service and payment arrangements for important public offices.



Related: Articles 59, 75, 97, 125, 148, 158, 164, 186, 221, 316.



Important Notes: Actual amounts and several service details may be determined or revised by ordinary legislation, subject to the constitutional protections for the office concerned.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Third Schedule — Forms of Oaths or Affirmations

Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Oaths; Constitutional Offices; Parliament; Judiciary



Law: Prescribes forms of oaths or affirmations for Union Ministers, Parliament candidates and members, Supreme Court and CAG office-holders; State Ministers, State Legislature candidates and members, High Court Judges, Governors, and other specified constitutional offices.



Simple: Before taking certain public offices or legislative seats, a person must make the constitutional oath or affirmation found in this Schedule.
