Related: Articles 75, 84, 99, 124, 148, 159, 164, 173, 188, 219.



Important Notes: A person may make an affirmation instead of a religious oath. The prescribed wording includes allegiance to the Constitution and faithful performance of duties.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Fourth Schedule — Allocation of Seats in the Council of States

Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Parliament; Rajya Sabha; States \& Union Territories



Law: Allocates seats in the Rajya Sabha among States and eligible Union Territories. The allocation is part of the constitutional structure of the Council of States and may be changed through constitutional amendment.



Simple: This Schedule decides how many Rajya Sabha seats each State and relevant Union Territory receives.



Related: Articles 80, 81, 82, 170.



Important Notes: The Schedule has changed as States and Union Territories were reorganised or created. Rajya Sabha members are generally elected by elected State Legislative Assembly members through proportional representation, except specified Union Territory arrangements.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Fifth Schedule — Provisions as to the Administration and Control of Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes

Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Scheduled Areas; Tribal Areas; State Government



Law: Governs administration of Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes in States other than areas covered by the Sixth Schedule. It provides for Presidential declaration of Scheduled Areas, Governor reports to the President, Tribes Advisory Councils, and special Governor regulations concerning land transfers, money-lending, and application of laws.



Simple: This Schedule gives special constitutional protection to tribal areas in several States. It allows special rules to protect tribal land, welfare, and administration.



Related: Articles 244(1), 275, 339, 342.



Important Notes: A Tribes Advisory Council generally consists of up to 20 members, as nearly as possible three-fourths being ST representatives in the State Assembly. Governor regulations under this Schedule require Presidential assent.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Sixth Schedule — Provisions as to the Administration of Tribal Areas in the States of Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram

Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Tribal Areas; Local Government; Special State Provisions



Law: Provides autonomous administration for specified tribal areas in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, and Mizoram through Autonomous District Councils and, where applicable, Autonomous Regional Councils. These councils may make laws and administer specified matters such as land, forests other than reserved forests, social customs, village administration, and certain local taxes.



Simple: Some tribal areas in four north-eastern States have autonomous councils with special local law-making and administrative powers.



Related: Articles 244(2), 275, 330, 332, 371A, 371B, 371G.



Important Notes: The Sixth Schedule differs from the Fifth Schedule: it establishes autonomous councils, while the Fifth Schedule mainly provides special State administration and Governor powers for Scheduled Areas.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Seventh Schedule — Union List, State List and Concurrent List

Status: Active; substantially amended



Category: Constitution; Legislative Powers; Union-State Relations



Law: Divides legislative subjects into the Union List, State List, and Concurrent List. Parliament generally legislates on Union List subjects; States generally legislate on State List subjects; both may legislate on Concurrent List subjects, with Union law prevailing where there is inconsistency, subject to constitutional exceptions.



Simple: This Schedule decides which subjects Parliament can make laws about, which subjects States can make laws about, and which subjects both can legislate on.



Related: Articles 245–255, 246, 246A, 248, 254; Articles 301–307.



Important Notes: The Constitution (Forty-second Amendment) Act, 1976 shifted some subjects to the Concurrent List. The Constitution (One Hundred and First Amendment) Act, 2016 significantly changed tax entries for GST.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Eighth Schedule — Languages

Status: Active; amended



Category: Constitution; Languages; Linguistic Rights; Official Language



Law: Lists 22 constitutionally recognised languages: Assamese, Bengali, Bodo, Dogri, Gujarati, Hindi, Kannada, Kashmiri, Konkani, Maithili, Malayalam, Manipuri, Marathi, Nepali, Odia, Punjabi, Sanskrit, Santali, Sindhi, Tamil, Telugu, and Urdu.



Simple: This Schedule recognises 22 languages for constitutional purposes. Inclusion does not make a language India’s national language; India has no constitutionally declared national language.



Related: Articles 29, 30, 343–351.



Important Notes: The original Constitution listed 14 languages. Sindhi, Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali, Bodo, Dogri, Maithili, and Santali were added through later constitutional amendments.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Ninth Schedule — Validation of Certain Acts and Regulations

Status: Active; subject to judicial review limits



Category: Constitution; Property; Land Reform; Constitutional Remedies



Law: Contains Acts and Regulations given protection through Article 31B, originally to support land-reform measures against Part III challenges. Laws placed in the Ninth Schedule are constitutional text entries, but the listed laws remain ordinary Acts or Regulations.



Simple: This Schedule gives listed laws special constitutional protection, especially laws connected with land reform. It is not a complete shield from all court review.



Related: Articles 13, 31A, 31B, 32, 368.



Important Notes: Judicial interpretation: constitutional amendments adding laws to the Ninth Schedule on or after 24 April 1973 may be tested if they damage the Constitution’s basic structure.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

; Supreme Court, I.R. Coelho v. State of Tamil Nadu, 2007.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Tenth Schedule — Provisions as to Disqualification on Ground of Defection

Status: Active; inserted and amended



Category: Constitution; Anti-Defection; Parliament; State Legislature; Elections



Law: Provides for disqualification of MPs and MLAs/MLCs who voluntarily give up party membership, vote or abstain contrary to party direction without required condonation, or otherwise fall within its terms. Independent members are disqualified if they join a party; nominated members face limits on joining a party. The Speaker or Chairman ordinarily decides disqualification questions. A merger exception requires at least two-thirds of a legislature party.



Simple: Legislators may lose their seat for defection, such as leaving their party or voting against its official direction in covered situations.



Related: Articles 102, 191, 122, 212, 361B.



Important Notes: Inserted by the Constitution (Fifty-second Amendment) Act, 1985; amended by the Constitution (Ninety-first Amendment) Act, 2003, which removed the former split exception.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Eleventh Schedule — Powers, Authority and Responsibilities of Panchayats

Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Panchayats; Local Government; Rural Governance



Law: Lists 29 subjects that State Legislatures may devolve to Panchayats under Article 243G. Subjects include agriculture, land improvement, irrigation, animal husbandry, rural housing, drinking water, roads, rural electrification, poverty alleviation, education, health, women and child development, social welfare, and public distribution.



Simple: This Schedule lists local-development subjects that States may give Panchayats responsibility for.



Related: Articles 40, 243A–243O, especially Article 243G.



Important Notes: Inserted by the Constitution (Seventy-third Amendment) Act, 1992. The Schedule does not itself automatically transfer every subject to every Panchayat; State law determines actual devolution.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Twelfth Schedule — Powers, Authority and Responsibilities of Municipalities

Status: Active; inserted



Category: Constitution; Municipalities; Local Government; Urban Governance



Law: Lists 18 subjects that State Legislatures may devolve to Municipalities under Article 243W. These include urban planning, land-use regulation, roads, water supply, public health, sanitation, fire services, urban forestry, slum improvement, poverty alleviation, parks, street lighting, parking, burial grounds, and regulation of certain urban services.



Simple: This Schedule lists city and town functions that States may assign to Municipalities.



Related: Articles 243P–243ZG, especially Article 243W.



Important Notes: Inserted by the Constitution (Seventy-fourth Amendment) Act, 1992. The Schedule does not automatically give every Municipal body all listed powers; State law determines the actual powers and funds transferred.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Last Verified: 20 August 2026



Historical / Omitted / Replaced Constitutional Provisions

Historical / Omitted Provision

Status: Historical / Replaced



Provision: First Schedule — Part A, Part B, Part C and Part D State classifications.



What changed: The Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956 replaced the original four-part State classification with the present States and Union Territories structure.



Simple: India no longer uses the old Part A, Part B, Part C, and Part D State categories.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Historical / Omitted Provision

Status: Historical / Amended



Provision: Eighth Schedule language list.



What changed: The original list of 14 languages was expanded to 22 through later constitutional amendments.



Simple: The recognised-language list has grown over time and currently contains 22 languages.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Historical / Omitted Provision

Status: Historical / Inserted



Provision: Ninth Schedule.



What changed: Inserted by the Constitution (First Amendment) Act, 1951 and linked to Article 31B.



Simple: The Ninth Schedule was added after the Constitution began; it was not part of the original 1950 text.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.



Historical / Omitted Provision

Status: Historical / Inserted



Provision: Tenth, Eleventh and Twelfth Schedules.



What changed: The Tenth Schedule was inserted by the Constitution (Fifty-second Amendment) Act, 1985; the Eleventh by the Seventy-third Amendment, 1992; and the Twelfth by the Seventy-fourth Amendment, 1992.



Simple: Anti-defection, Panchayat, and Municipality constitutional schedules were added later, not included in the original Constitution.



Source: 

Official Constitution of India

.
